arXiv:2306.00940v2 [hep-th] 01 Nov 2023




Burns space and holography



and Atul Sharma



Abstract
    

We elaborate on various aspects of our top-down celestial holographic duality wherein the semiclassical bulk spacetime is a 4d asymptotically flat, self-dual Kähler geometry known as Burns space. The bulk theory includes an open string sector comprising a 4d WZW model and a closed string sector called “Mabuchi gravity” capturing fluctuations of the Kähler potential. Starting with the type I topological B-model on the twistor space of flat space, we obtain the twistor space of Burns space from the backreaction of a stack of  N NN coincident D1 branes, while the chiral algebra is obtained from (a twist of) the brane worldvolume theory. One striking consequence of this duality is that all loop-level scattering amplitudes of the theory on Burns space can be expressed as correlation functions of an explicit 2d chiral algebra.
    

We also present additional large- N NN checks, matching several 2 and 3-point amplitudes and their collinear expansions in the WZW4 sector, and the mixed WZW4-Mabuchi sector, of the bulk theory to the corresponding 2 and 3-point vacuum correlators and operator product expansions in the dual chiral algebra. Key features of the duality, along with our main results, are summarized in the introduction.
  




1 Introduction & Conclusion




The holographic principle is by now widely believed to be intrinsic to any complete description of quantum gravity. Holography has been easiest to formulate in (asymptotically) anti-de Sitter space, with asymptotia at spatial infinity and observables equivalent to those, e.g. correlation functions, in a boundary conformal field theory. Most difficult, but also most relevant for the real world, is the case of de Sitter space, with its temporal infinities and the attendant subtleties in defining observables. Formulating a holographic principle in asymptotically flat spacetimes, with null asymptotia and a well-defined S-matrix, may now serve as a Goldilocks case for concrete formulations of the holographic principle, particularly in the interesting case when the gravitating spacetime is four dimensional, which falls outside of the purview of the high-dimensional matrix models understood so far (see, e.g., [1, 2]).




There is a renewed surge of interest in holography for asymptotically flat spacetimes on the heel of refined studies of asymptotic symmetry algebras (see [3] for a review), most recently exhibiting 2d chiral algebraic structures at the classical level [4, 5, 6, 7]. Related suites of proposals collectively known as “celestial holography” have posited that the form of a putative holographic dual for such theories takes the form of an exotic, non-unitary conformal field theory (often called the celestial CFT or CCFT) supported on the celestial sphere at asymptotic null infinity. For reviews and results, see [8, 9, 10] and references therein.




There are many challenges to interpreting a would-be CFT dual at null infinity, ranging from the puzzling (“How can we understand a CFT exhibiting an integral shift symmetry in its conformal weights, which is the avatar of 4d translation invariance in an eigenbasis of boost weights?”) to the fundamental (“How can 4d gravitational dynamics in flat spacetimes be encoded locally on the celestial sphere, particularly in such a way as to guarantee stringent CFT axioms?”) Various bottom-up approaches have been suggested to ameliorate these issues, for instance by leveraging judicious transforms on tempered distributions called shadow and light transforms in the latter case [11, 12, 13].




In this paper, we elaborate on an explicit, example top-down duality engineered from string theory, after the fashion of the AdS/CFT correspondence. This example was constructed with the aim of sharpening and addressing some of these questions. While we do not claim the strong features exhibited by our duality are necessary to describe or construct a CCFT (or other type of holographic dual for flat spacetime) outside of this toy example, we do stress that it highlights some sufficient conditions for such a construction to exist. In particular, one is guaranteed to obtain a 2d chiral algebra with a local, associative OPE if its dual theory is integrable. Further, in 4d the condition of integrability geometrizes to a theory admitting a local holomorphic lift to twistor space. Recent advances in computing and cancelling gauge anomalies in 6d holomorphic theories [14, 15] enable us to study a class of such 4d integrable theories at the quantum level by appealing to their twistorial uplift. Furthermore, the holomorphic theory on twistor space can, in favorable cases, be identified with sectors of a topological string theory, to which the methods of twisted holography [16, 17], especially homological algebra [18], can be applied. Important earlier work, closely related to twisted holography, on the protected boundary chiral algebra subsector of 4d  \mathcal{N}=4 𝒩=4\mathcal{N}=4 and its bulk dual can be found in [19].




When the 4d ‘‘bulk’’ theories are not integrable, one must contend with the rich non-analyticities of scattering amplitudes (outside special classes of scattering problems, such as the tree level MHV sector in gauge theory), in which the local twistorial perspective will be insufficient (and a non-local twistorial approach may not be the most expedient). Mapping such scattering problems to the celestial sphere results in non-localities, in violation of the standard axioms of the chiral algebras, which must be reckoned with in any CCFT.11
            1
            
            
            
          Though see [20] for a proposed nonlocal mathematical extension of vertex algebras pre-dating celestial holography. Relatedly, CCFT states will acquire non-vanishing anomalous scaling dimensions. Furthermore, in classically integrable 4d theories, there is a map between obstructions to the integrability of its quantization, and gauge anomalies in the local twistorial uplift. These non-vanishing anomalies in 6d encoding 4d non-integrability at the quantum level have recently been understood to lead to violations in associativity of (the quantum deformation of) celestial chiral algebras [21, 22, 23, 24]. It will be crucial to address these issues when formulating the holographic dual of a general 4d bulk theory.




Still, we believe it is worth understanding in detail even the non-generic situation in which the 4d bulk theory is integrable, since there a “celestial” chiral algebra of asymptotic symmetries can be described explicitly without any modifications of the standard CFT axioms. Whether or how the above issues can be addressed in a general theory, admitting true black hole solutions and chaotic dynamics, is beyond the scope of this work. We will construct such a well-defined holographic duality from the top-down, in type I string theory. In the remainder of this introduction, we will motivate and sketch this construction.




It is perhaps not surprising that the setting for a holographic duality for integrable theories lies in the context of the topological string (see, e.g., [25] for relevant earlier work) or twisted holography, and it is precisely in the setting of twisted theories, which are either holomorphic or topological theories, where the technique of Koszul duality has the most teeth: in the twisted world, the symmetries of a system enhance dramatically to infinite-dimensional algebras, providing constraints powerful enough to fix some or all of the resulting dynamics. In the developing subject of twisted holography, it is expected that one may even be able to use this enormous symmetry to compute or fix observables to all orders in a  1/N 1/N1/N expansion. The precise duality we study in this paper arises as follows.




We study the topological B-model of the type I string, compactified on twistor space.22
            2
            
            
            
          Defining the topological B-model on a non-Calabi Yau manifold requires a slight modification of the usual topological string construction, which we describe in the main text. As is well-known, the vacuum state of the type I string includes both open and closed string sectors described in the topological string framework by a holomorphic Chern-Simons theory and a Kodaira-Spencer theory of gravity, respectively. When these sectors are coupled, this theory has no gauge anomaly [14] if and only if the gauge group is  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8). Consequently, it reduces to an integrable theory in 4d spacetime. Roughly, the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory reduces to a 4d WZW model [26, 27] with target manifold the group manifold  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8), while the Kodaira-Spencer theory reduces to a theory, described by the Mabuchi functional [28], of dynamical fluctuations of the Kähler potential governing the 4d Kähler metric. We stress that in 4d flat spacetime, the  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) WZW4 model has vanishing scattering amplitudes, as befits an integrable theory: WZW4 theories for general  G GG are not integrable at the quantum level. Equivalently, their local holomorphic uplifts to twistor space cannot be consistently quantized. Again, while the 4d models for other  G GG are interesting and perfectly well-behaved quantum field theories, we do not know how to define a dual 2d chiral algebra for these theories. Indeed, we expect naive attempts to define a chiral algebra of asymptotic symmetries for these theories will result in failures of associativity when quantum effects are incorporated.




To get a full-fledged holographic duality, we add a large number of D-branes in 6d, which backreact on the twistor space of flat space and so, in turn, backreact on 4d flat spacetime. In particular, we add  N\gg 1 N≫1N\gg 1 Euclidean B-model D1-branes wrapping the zero section  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} of the twistor fibration. This  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} is precisely the celestial sphere from the 4d point of view. The worldvolume theory of these branes in the B-model is a 2d chiral algebra, arising from dimensional reduction of holomorphic Chern-Simons theory [29, 16], supported on the celestial sphere, as desired in a celestial holographic duality. Furthermore, we explicitly compute the backreaction in the 6d Kodaira-Spencer theory, amounting to a deformation of the background complex structure, and find that we obtain the so-called twistor space of Burns space [30]. Reducing to 4d, the deformed spacetime metric is the asymptotically flat (Euclidean) Burns metric [31]. Therefore, the 4d “bulk” theory in our holographic duality can be described semiclassically as a coupled  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) WZW4 model + Mabuchi gravity on Burns space. Crucially, our 4d theories acquire nontrivial scattering amplitudes (i.e. nonvanishing scattering at 4 points and higher) in this curved background, which can be matched to chiral correlators. In particular, we can explicitly match collinear limits of this scattering with the OPE of the dual chiral algebra per standard celestial reasoning. We do so in the  N\rightarrow\infty N→∞N\rightarrow\infty limit in this paper, though we expect the duality holds at finite  N NN, and we plan to study this in future work.




Our construction employs the twistor space associated to analytically continued spacetime, in which one may work without specializing to a particular spacetime signature (or only doing so at a later stage). We remark that our full nonperturbative duality33
            3
            
            
            
          Perturbatively, one may continue to Lorentzian signature and compute scattering amplitudes in the Burns space metric [32], and our duality will still hold. is perhaps most naturally formulated in Euclidean signature spacetime. One way to see this is to notice that, as is familiar from the self-dual sectors of gauge theory and gravity, evaluating the action on nontrivial saddles in Lorentzian signature requires the introduction of complex fields. However, in any signature, the theories under consideration in this paper are non-unitary. While a CCFT dual may be expected to be non-unitary on general grounds, a non-unitary bulk spacetime theory is a priori less desirable; indeed, we view it as a serious shortcoming of our stringent construction, closely related to the existence of a twistorial uplift/associative OPE, that must be overcome in other top-down constructions of flat space holography.




The WZW4 model, plus Mabuchi gravity, is an interacting and non-renormalizable system. One surprising consequence of this conjectural holographic duality is that it implies a non-perturbative (finite- N NN) isomorphism between scattering in this theory on a curved spacetime (Burns space) and correlation functions in a 2d chiral algebra! That such a strong dynamical statement holds in an asymptotically flat spacetime is a testament to the underlying simplicity of the 4d theory.




Many questions remain about this duality, and putative dualities for generic theories in asymptotically flat spacetimes. In future work, we aim to study, for instance, non-planar corrections to this duality and more aspects of its embedding into full 10d string theory. A smaller, immediate puzzle is whether or not the other integrable/twistorial theories studied in [15, 21, 33, 34] admit 10d uplifts to string theory, and hence their own twisted holographic correspondences. Similarly, seeing as Burns space is only scalar-flat but not Ricci-flat, the study of celestial chiral algebras on Ricci-flat self-dual backgrounds like ALE spaces also promises to be a rich avenue for exploration [35].




The most pressing question in this program is the incorporation of complete gravitational dynamics, including all metric degrees of freedom, into the 4d spacetime. Our holographic duality enjoys a standard gravitational description in 6d, with fluctuating metric components corresponding to complex structure deformations and gauged diffeomorphisms. The passage from 6d to 4d involves a gauge fixing of these diffeomorphisms, leaving us with a Liouville-like closed string sector, as we describe in more detail in the main text, and in particular not a covariant theory. Again, this is perhaps unsurprising due to the close connections between integrable or exactly-solvable models in various dimensions, but it deprives us of the richness of gravitational physics in this toy model. We expect that incorporating genuine gravitational dynamics in a 4d spacetime will require a potentially quite dramatic modification of standard CFT axioms, or perhaps a holographic formulation of a different type such as an adaptation of matrix theory to lower dimensions.




1.1 Summary of the paper




This work is a companion to our Letter [36]. In the remainder of this paper we expand on this duality in detail.

1. 



In section 2 we introduce the topological B-model on twistor space. The topological B-model is typically studied on Calabi-Yau manifolds and, with a careful treatment of boundary conditions, can be generalized to complex  3 33-folds equipped with a meromorphic volume form. A result of Pontecorvo [37] shows that twistor spaces of scalar-flat Kähler  4 44-manifolds are equipped with a meromorphic  3 33-form, and so are the natural twistor spaces on which to place the topological B-model. We show that the closed string sector of the topological B-model gives rise to Mabuchi gravity: a theory of a dynamical scalar field interpreted as the Kähler potential. The equations of motion of Mabuchi gravity imply that the associated Kähler metric has vanishing scalar curvature. We show, following [38, 39], that the open string sector is the WZW4 model [26, 40, 27]. Our analysis focuses on the type I topological B-model [14], whose open string sector consists of holomorphic Chern-Simons for  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8).

2. 



In section 3 we consider the backreaction of a stack of D1 branes in the topological B-model on the twistor space of flat  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. The analysis follows that in [16]. We find that the backreacted twistor space contains  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and is the twistor space of the asymptotically flat Burns metric [31, 30], a certain scalar-flat Kähler metric on the complex manifold  \mathscr{O}(-1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} 𝒪⁡(−1)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathscr{O}(-1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}. The backreaction changes the topology of the four-dimensional spacetime by introducing a non-contractible  S^{2} S2S^{2}. This was first anticipated in [41] and is somewhat similar to the Gopakumar-Vafa [42] geometric transition.

3. 



In section 4, we analyze the holographic dual chiral algebra. This is the theory living on a stack of  N NN D1 branes wrapping a curve in the twistor space of flat  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. The algebra is the BRST reduction of a collection of free symplectic bosons by  \operatorname{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\operatorname{Sp}(N), and can also be realized as the chiral algebra associated by [43] to a family of  \mathcal{N}=2 𝒩=2\mathcal{N}=2 SCFTs in dimension  4 44.




An important subtlety in our analysis is that this chiral algebra has point defects associated to the locus where the meromorphic volume form on twistor space has poles. Conformal blocks of the chiral algebra in the presence of these defects are shown to be in one-to-one correspondence with the Hilbert space of the bulk theory on Burns space.

4. 



In section 5, we describe the relationship between our Burns space holography and familiar  \mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} AdS3×S3\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} holography. The twistor space of Burns space contains  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} SL2​(ℂ)≃AdS3×S3\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3}. The holographic dual chiral algebra lives on the boundary of  \mathrm{AdS}_{3} AdS3\mathrm{AdS}_{3}. We identify where this boundary lives in twistor space, and we show how the twistor lines of Burns space are copies of  S^{2} S2S^{2} which foliate  \mathrm{AdS}_{3} AdS3\mathrm{AdS}_{3}.

5. 



In section 6 we analyze the holographic dictionary on twistor space in more detail. We explicitly identify the states on twistor space which match the large- N NN states in our chiral algebra, and we show that the defects in our chiral algebra can be seen from the holographic dual theory on twistor space.




In this section we prove one of our main results: the tree level scattering amplitudes of WZW4 plus Mabuchi gravity on Burns space have a surprising symmetry enhancement, from the  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) SU⁡(2)×U⁡(1)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) isometry group of the Burns geometry to a larger  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)×SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) group, and indeed to an infinite-dimensional algebra. This allows us to identify these amplitudes with vacuum correlators in the chiral algebra, in the absence of defects.




We conjecture (with quite strong evidence) that this result continues to hold at loop level. This conjecture, together with the holographic correspondence and the Penrose transform, implies something rather remarkable: all scattering amplitudes of WZW4 plus Mabuchi gravity on Burns space can be expressed as the correlators of a rather simple chiral algebra. Since the chiral algebra is relatively simple, this implies that these scattering amplitudes are all in principle computable.




At tree level, the amplitudes for WZW4 coincide with the all-plus amplitudes for Yang-Mills theory. This result implies that these Yang-Mills amplitudes on Burns space match planar correlators in the chiral algebra.

6. 



In sections 7 and 8, we initiate the analysis of the holographic duality directly on Burns space, as opposed to its twistor space. We identify the scattering states on Burns space holographically dual to the single-trace operators in the large  N NN chiral algebra. For WZW4 states, these are certain explicit solutions of the Laplace equation. For states of Mabuchi gravity, they solve instead a fourth-order equation involving the Paneitz operator. We phrase the conjectured duality as a match between scattering amplitudes of explicit states on Burns space and chiral algebra correlators.

7. 



In section 9, we turn to tests of the duality directly on Burns space, in the planar limit. We compute the tree level two-point function of states in the WZW4 sector by the standard holographic method [44]. This had already been computed by Hawking, Page and Pope [32] by a different method, yielding the same result.44
                    4
                    
                    
                    
                  Hawking et al. were computing the two-point scattering amplitude of a conformally-coupled scalar on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}, but since this manifold is conformally equivalent to Burns space, and the scalar curvature of Burns space vanishes, the result is the same. The result is a certain Bessel function, whose series expansion matches perfectly the two-point function of states in the chiral algebra.




We also compute certain terms in the collinear limits of 3-point WZW4 amplitudes, and find that they match exactly with the OPE coefficients of the dual chiral algebra. A similar calculation is performed for the OPE and 3-point amplitude involving two WZW4 states and one “graviton” (Kähler scalar).








2 Topological strings on twistor space




In this section, we provide a concise review of B-model topological strings on twistor spaces of self-dual spacetimes. The B-model can only be studied on Calabi-Yau manifolds, but twistor spaces are not Calabi-Yau. Nevertheless, the twistor space of a spacetime with a self-dual Kähler metric comes equipped with a meromorphic 3-form. Excising the polar divisor of this 3-form from the twistor space yields a non-compact Calabi-Yau 3-fold. The topological string makes sense with defects (or holomorphic boundary conditions) along the divisor. We will study a type I version of the open+closed topological B-model on two particular examples of such 3-folds: the twistor spaces of flat space and Burns space.




2.1 Twistor geometry




A metric on a 4-manifold is termed self-dual (SD) if its Weyl tensor is self-dual. Self-dual metrics span integrable subsectors of Einstein as well as conformal gravity [45]. Integrability in four dimensions is often intimately linked to the existence of a twistor space: a complex 3-fold that encodes 4-dimensional physics in terms of complex analytic geometry. As a smooth manifold, the twistor space  \pi:Z\to M π:Z→M\pi:Z\to M of a smooth, oriented 4-manifold  M MM with Riemannian metric  g gg is given by the bundle of pointwise metric- and orientation-compatible almost complex structures. It has  \mathrm{SO}(4)/\mathrm{U}(2)\simeq\mathbb{CP}^{1} SO⁡(4)/U⁡(2)≃ℂ​ℙ1\mathrm{SO}(4)/\mathrm{U}(2)\simeq\mathbb{CP}^{1} fibers and is diffeomorphic to the unit sphere bundle  S(\Lambda^{-}) S⁡(Λ−)S(\Lambda^{-}) of the rank 3 bundle of anti-self-dual (ASD) 2-forms  \Lambda^{-}\to M Λ−→M\Lambda^{-}\to M. Alternatively, it is diffeomorphic to the projective 2-spinor bundle of  M MM.




 Z ZZ can be equipped with the Atiyah-Hitchin-Singer almost complex structure. To construct this, one splits the tangent space of every point  p\in Z p∈Zp\in Z into vertical and horizontal components using the Levi-Civita connection of  g gg induced on  S(\Lambda^{-}) S⁡(Λ−)S(\Lambda^{-}). The almost complex structure at  p pp is taken to be the direct sum of the standard complex structure on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} with the almost complex structure on the tangent space  T_{\pi(p)}M\otimes\mathbb{C} Tπ⁡(p)​M⊗ℂT_{\pi(p)}M\otimes\mathbb{C} parametrized by  p pp. It is well-known that this becomes an integrable almost complex structure on  Z ZZ if and only if  g gg is self-dual [46, 47]; see also [48] for a review. The  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} fiber over a point  x\in M x∈Mx\in M is known as the twistor line corresponding to  x xx and will be denoted  L_{x} LxL_{x}. When this almost complex structure is integrable, these twistor lines become holomorphic rational curves in  Z ZZ cut out by the so-called incidence relations. They have normal bundle  \mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1) 𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1). Moreover, the antipodal map on each  L_{x} LxL_{x} gives rise to an antiholomorphic involution without fixed points, giving  Z ZZ a real structure.




When  g gg is self-dual as well as Ricci-flat, the twistor space  Z ZZ is called a nonlinear graviton [46]. In this case,  (M,g) (M,g)(M,g) becomes a hyperkähler manifold. Such metrics describe self-dual Einstein gravity without a cosmological constant. Instead, in the rest of this work we will mainly be interested in the case when  g gg is not necessarily Ricci-flat but is only required to be scalar-flat and Kähler.




If  g gg is a Kähler metric on a 4-manifold  M MM, the results of [49, 50] show that it is self-dual55
              5
              
              
              
            With respect to the orientation in which the Kähler form is anti-self-dual. if and only if it is scalar-flat, i.e., has zero Ricci scalar  R=0 R=0R=0. So we will usually refer to such self-dual metrics as scalar-flat Kähler. As observed by Hitchin in [51], the twistor space  Z ZZ of any SD 4-manifold  (M,g) (M,g)(M,g) is spin, i.e., its canonical bundle  K_{Z} KZK_{Z} always admits a square root  K_{Z}^{1/2} KZ1/2K_{Z}^{1/2}. If  M MM itself is spin, then  K_{Z} KZK_{Z} also admits a fourth root  K_{Z}^{1/4} KZ1/4K_{Z}^{1/4}. Furthermore, a theorem by Pontecorvo [37] shows that if  g gg is self-dual as well as Kähler, then it gives rise to a globally holomorphic section  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} of  K_{Z}^{-1/2} KZ−1/2K_{Z}^{-1/2}. This can be inverted to produce a meromorphic section  \Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2} Ω=ωˇ−2\Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2} of  K_{Z} KZK_{Z}.




Another standard fact is that the restriction of  K_{Z} KZK_{Z} to every twistor line is given by  \mathscr{O}(-4)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} 𝒪⁡(−4)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathscr{O}(-4)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}. As a result,  K_{Z}^{-1/2}|_{L_{x}}\simeq\mathscr{O}(2) KZ−1/2|Lx≃𝒪(2)K_{Z}^{-1/2}|_{L_{x}}\simeq\mathscr{O}(2), so the section  \check{\omega}|_{L_{x}} ωˇ|Lx\check{\omega}|_{L_{x}} has two zeroes on every twistor line  L_{x} LxL_{x}. These correspond to a pair of almost complex structures on the tangent space  T_{x}M\otimes\mathbb{C} Tx​M⊗ℂT_{x}M\otimes\mathbb{C}. In fact, Pontecorvo constructs the section  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} in such a way that one of these zeroes parametrizes precisely the integrable almost complex structure  J JJ with respect to which  g gg is Kähler, and the other zero corresponds to the conjugate almost complex structure  -J −J-J. As we vary  L_{x} LxL_{x}, the zeroes of  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} sweep out a quadric in  Z ZZ that acts as the polar divisor of  \Omega Ω\Omega. The Kähler form  \omega ω\omega on  M MM is recovered by performing a contour integral





 \omega(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Gamma\subset L_{x}}\Omega ω⁡(x)=12​π​∮Γ⊂LxΩ\omega(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Gamma\subset L_{x}}\Omega

(2.1)


where the contour  \Gamma Γ\Gamma separates the zeroes of  \check{\omega}|_{L_{x}} ωˇ|Lx\check{\omega}|_{L_{x}} and circles the zero at  (x,J|_{x}) (x,J|x)(x,J|_{x}) clockwise. Our orientation and normalization conventions will be such that the resulting Kähler form is a real ASD 2-form on  M MM and satisfies  \omega^{2}=-2\,\mathrm{vol}_{g} ω2=−2​volg\omega^{2}=-2\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}.




For the reader’s convenience, we provide a more extensive review of this construction using local coordinates on twistor space in appendix A. See also [41] for a comparable review.



Example: Flat space.




The paradigmatic example of the twistor correspondence is the twistor space of flat space  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} with its Euclidean metric. This helps us in setting up our local coordinates and spinor conventions. We will mainly follow the conventions of [15, 52].




Let  x^{\mu} xμx^{\mu} denote the standard coordinates on  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. We can define double null coordinates  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} xα​α˙x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}},  \alpha=1,2,\dot{\alpha}=\dot{1},\dot{2} α=1,2,α˙=1˙,2˙\alpha=1,2,\dot{\alpha}=\dot{1},\dot{2}, by setting





 x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}x^{0}+\mathrm{i}x^{3}&&x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}\\
-x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}&&x^{0}-\mathrm{i}x^{3}\end{pmatrix}\,. xα​α˙=12​(x0+i​x3x2+i​x1−x2+i​x1x0−i​x3).x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}x^{0}+\mathrm{i}x^{3}&&x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}\\
-x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}&&x^{0}-\mathrm{i}x^{3}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(2.2)


The indices  \alpha,\dot{\alpha} α,α˙\alpha,\dot{\alpha} are spinor indices of  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) of opposite chirality. In terms of these, the Euclidean metric can be expressed as





 \mathrm{d}s^{2}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\beta\dot{\beta}}\,. d​s2=δμ​ν​d​xμ​d​xν=ϵα​β​ϵα˙​β˙​d​xα​α˙​d​xβ​β˙.\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\beta\dot{\beta}}\,.

(2.3)


Here,  \epsilon_{\alpha\beta},\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}} ϵα​β,ϵα˙​β˙\epsilon_{\alpha\beta},\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}} are  2\times 2 2×22\times 2 Levi-Civita symbols that we will ubiquitously use to raise, lower and contract spinor indices with the conventions





 \lambda^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\lambda_{\beta}\,,\qquad\lambda_{\beta}=\lambda^{\alpha}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,,\qquad\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\mu_{\dot{\beta}}\,,\qquad\mu_{\dot{\beta}}=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}} λα=ϵα​β​λβ,λβ=λα​ϵα​β,μα˙=ϵα˙​β˙​μβ˙,μβ˙=μα˙​ϵα˙​β˙\lambda^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\lambda_{\beta}\,,\qquad\lambda_{\beta}=\lambda^{\alpha}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,,\qquad\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\mu_{\dot{\beta}}\,,\qquad\mu_{\dot{\beta}}=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}

(2.4)


etc. Spinor contractions will be abbreviated using square and angle brackets





 \langle\lambda\,\kappa\rangle\vcentcolon=\lambda^{\alpha}\kappa_{\alpha}\,,\qquad[\mu\,\nu]\vcentcolon=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}\nu_{\dot{\alpha}} ⟨λ​κ⟩:=λα​κα,[μ​ν]:=μα˙​να˙\langle\lambda\,\kappa\rangle\vcentcolon=\lambda^{\alpha}\kappa_{\alpha}\,,\qquad[\mu\,\nu]\vcentcolon=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}\nu_{\dot{\alpha}}

(2.5)


that are invariant under  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) rotations of the dotted or undotted indices.




We will mostly work with the following complex coordinates built out of  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} xα​α˙x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}},





 u^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=x^{1\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=x^{2\dot{\alpha}}=(-\overline{u^{\dot{2}}},\overline{u^{\dot{1}}})\equiv(-\bar{u}^{2},\bar{u}^{1})\,. uα˙:=x1​α˙,u^α˙:=x2​α˙=(−u2˙¯,u1˙¯)≡(−u¯2,u¯1).u^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=x^{1\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=x^{2\dot{\alpha}}=(-\overline{u^{\dot{2}}},\overline{u^{\dot{1}}})\equiv(-\bar{u}^{2},\bar{u}^{1})\,.

(2.6)


In the last equality, we are using the convention that the complex conjugate of a dotted spinor is an undotted spinor. In contrast, the spinor  \hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} u^α˙\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} built from the complex conjugates transforms in the same  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) representation as  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} due to the property  \widehat{Lu}=L\hat{u} L​u^=L​u^\widehat{Lu}=L\hat{u} valid for matrices  L\in\mathrm{SU}(2) L∈SU⁡(2)L\in\mathrm{SU}(2). Because of this, the map  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙↦u^α˙u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} is known as quaternionic conjugation. In these coordinates, the Euclidean metric reads





 \mathrm{d}s^{2}=2\,\|\mathrm{d}u\|^{2}=2\left(|\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}|^{2}+|\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}|^{2}\right)\,. d​s2=2​‖d​u‖2=2​(|d​u1˙|2+|d​u2˙|2).\mathrm{d}s^{2}=2\,\|\mathrm{d}u\|^{2}=2\left(|\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}|^{2}+|\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}|^{2}\right)\,.

(2.7)


Here  \|u-v\|^{2}=|u^{\dot{1}}-v^{\dot{1}}|^{2}+|u^{\dot{2}}-v^{\dot{2}}|^{2} ‖u−v‖2=|u1˙−v1˙|2+|u2˙−v2˙|2\|u-v\|^{2}=|u^{\dot{1}}-v^{\dot{1}}|^{2}+|u^{\dot{2}}-v^{\dot{2}}|^{2} is the Euclidean norm. We note the useful relations





 [\hat{u}\,u]=\|u\|^{2}\,,\qquad x^{\mu}x_{\mu}=2\|u\|^{2}\,. [u^​u]=‖u‖2,xμ​xμ=2​‖u‖2.[\hat{u}\,u]=\|u\|^{2}\,,\qquad x^{\mu}x_{\mu}=2\|u\|^{2}\,.

(2.8)


The factor of  2 22 here is a convenient convention that is common in twistor theory [53, 54]. The associated volume form is





 \mathrm{d}^{4}x\equiv\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}=\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2} d4​x≡d​x0∧d​x1∧d​x2∧d​x3=d​u1˙∧d​u¯1∧d​u2˙∧d​u¯2\mathrm{d}^{4}x\equiv\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}=\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2}

(2.9)


which defines our orientation convention.




The twistor space of  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} is traditionally denoted  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT}. It is the total space of a rank 2 holomorphic vector bundle over the Riemann sphere,





 \mathbb{PT}=\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\,. ℙ​𝕋=𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)→ℂ​ℙ1.\mathbb{PT}=\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\,.

(2.10)


Let  z zz be an affine coordinate along its  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} base, and let  v^{\dot{\alpha}}=(v^{\dot{1}},v^{\dot{2}}) vα˙=(v1˙,v2˙)v^{\dot{\alpha}}=(v^{\dot{1}},v^{\dot{2}}) denote coordinates along the  \mathbb{C}^{2} ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} fibers. Under  z\mapsto 1/z z↦1/zz\mapsto 1/z, the fiber coordinates transform as





 z\mapsto\frac{1}{z}\implies v^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}{z}\,. z↦1z⟹vα˙↦vα˙z.z\mapsto\frac{1}{z}\implies v^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}{z}\,.

(2.11)


This holomorphic transition function, defined on the annulus  z\neq 0,\infty z≠0,∞z\neq 0,\infty, endows  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} with the structure of a complex 3-fold. Equivalently, it is also standard to view  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} as an open subset of  \mathbb{CP}^{3} ℂ​ℙ3\mathbb{CP}^{3} given by  \mathbb{PT}=\mathbb{CP}^{3}-\mathbb{CP}^{1} ℙ​𝕋=ℂ​ℙ3−ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{PT}=\mathbb{CP}^{3}-\mathbb{CP}^{1}. As an application of this second viewpoint, one defines line bundles  \mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{PT} 𝒪⁡(n)→ℙ​𝕋\mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{PT} as restrictions of the standard line bundles  \mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{CP}^{3} 𝒪⁡(n)→ℂ​ℙ3\mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{CP}^{3} to this subset, or equivalently as pull-backs from  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}.




Every point  x\in\mathbb{R}^{4} x∈ℝ4x\in\mathbb{R}^{4} corresponds to a holomorphic global section of  \mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} 𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1},





 L_{x}\;:\;v^{\dot{\alpha}}=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,. Lx:vα˙=uα˙+z​u^α˙.L_{x}\;:\;v^{\dot{\alpha}}=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(2.12)


These are taken to be the twistor lines. The line bundles  \mathscr{O}(n) 𝒪⁡(n)\mathscr{O}(n) restrict to the standard line bundles  \mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} 𝒪⁡(n)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathscr{O}(n)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} on each  L_{x} LxL_{x}. The antipodal map of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} extends to  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} as a fixed-point-free antiholomorphic involution





 (z,v^{\dot{\alpha}})\mapsto\left(-\frac{1}{\bar{z}},-\frac{\hat{v}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\bar{z}}\right)\,,\qquad\hat{v}^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=(-\bar{v}^{2},\bar{v}^{1})\,. (z,vα˙)↦(−1z¯,−v^α˙z¯),v^α˙:=(−v¯2,v¯1).(z,v^{\dot{\alpha}})\mapsto\left(-\frac{1}{\bar{z}},-\frac{\hat{v}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\bar{z}}\right)\,,\qquad\hat{v}^{\dot{\alpha}}\vcentcolon=(-\bar{v}^{2},\bar{v}^{1})\,.

(2.13)


The twistor lines  L_{x} LxL_{x} are invariant under this map, i.e., if  (z,v^{\dot{\alpha}}) (z,vα˙)(z,v^{\dot{\alpha}}) lies on  L_{x} LxL_{x}, then so does its antipodal point. Since knowing two points on a (projective) line completely determines the line, we obtain a diffeomorphism  \mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{PT} ℝ4×ℂ​ℙ1→ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{PT} given by  (x,z)\mapsto(v^{\dot{\alpha}},z)=(u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}},z) (x,z)↦(vα˙,z)=(uα˙+z​u^α˙,z)(x,z)\mapsto(v^{\dot{\alpha}},z)=(u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}},z). Moreover, this identifies the twistor lines  L_{x} LxL_{x} as the fibers of the projection  \mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4×ℂ​ℙ1→ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4}.




The Euclidean metric (2.7) is Kähler in the complex coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}}. It has Kähler form





 \omega=\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}=\mathrm{i}\,\bigl(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1}+\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2}\bigr)\,. ω=i​ϵα˙​β˙​d​uα˙∧d​u^β˙=i⁡(d​u1˙∧d​u¯1+d​u2˙∧d​u¯2).\omega=\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}=\mathrm{i}\,\bigl(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1}+\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2}\bigr)\,.

(2.14)


This is an ASD 2-form and satisfies  \frac{1}{2}\,\omega^{2}=-\mathrm{d}^{4}x 12​ω2=−d4​x\frac{1}{2}\,\omega^{2}=-\mathrm{d}^{4}x. It is instructive to obtain this Kähler form from twistor space via Pontecorvo’s theorem. The canonical bundle of  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} is  \mathscr{O}(-4) 𝒪⁡(−4)\mathscr{O}(-4). To get the Kähler form  \omega ω\omega, we take  \check{\omega}=z ωˇ=z\check{\omega}=z as our choice of global section of  K_{\mathbb{PT}}^{-1/2}=\mathscr{O}(2) Kℙ​𝕋−1/2=𝒪(2)K_{\mathbb{PT}}^{-1/2}=\mathscr{O}(2). The associated meromorphic 3-form is given by





 \Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}v^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}v^{\dot{2}}}{z^{2}}\,. Ω=ωˇ−2=d​z∧d​v1˙∧d​v2˙z2.\Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}v^{\dot{1}}\wedge\mathrm{d}v^{\dot{2}}}{z^{2}}\,.

(2.15)


Using the transition function (2.11), we see that this has poles of order  2 22 at  z=0 z=0z=0 and  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty each.




We can use the diffeomorphism  v^{\dot{\alpha}}=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} vα˙=uα˙+z​u^α˙v^{\dot{\alpha}}=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+z\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} to pull back  \Omega Ω\Omega to  \mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1} ℝ4×ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1},





 \Omega=\frac{\mathrm{d}z\wedge(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}-z\,\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2})\wedge(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}+z\,\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1})}{z^{2}}\,. Ω=d​z∧(d​u1˙−z​d​u¯2)∧(d​u2˙+z​d​u¯1)z2.\Omega=\frac{\mathrm{d}z\wedge(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{1}}-z\,\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{2})\wedge(\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{2}}+z\,\mathrm{d}\bar{u}^{1})}{z^{2}}\,.

(2.16)


Integrating it in  z zz along a contour that surrounds the pole at  z=0 z=0z=0 picks out the Kähler form of flat space:





 \frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{|z|=1}\Omega=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\wedge\omega=\omega\,. 12​π​∮|z|=1Ω=12​π​i​∮|z|=1d​zz∧ω=ω.\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{|z|=1}\Omega=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\wedge\omega=\omega\,.

(2.17)


In future sections, we will come across similar calculations in the context of more interesting scalar-flat Kähler manifolds.






2.2 Open strings and the WZW4 model




As we have seen, twistor spaces of scalar-flat Kähler manifolds are complex  3 33-folds equipped with meromorphic volume forms. The topological B-model can be studied on any complex  3 33-fold with a holomorphic volume form, and careful choices of boundary conditions [16, 15] allow one to define the topological B-model on  3 33-folds equipped with a meromorphic volume form as well. This suggests a general correspondence between topological string theory and field theories on scalar-flat Kähler manifolds. In this subsection and the next, we will describe the  4 44-dimensional theories corresponding to the open and closed topological B-model.




We first consider the open string sector. Let  Z ZZ be a complex 3-fold equipped with a globally holomorphic 3-form  \Omega Ω\Omega. Holomorphic Chern-Simons theory on  Z ZZ has the classical action





 S_{\text{hCS}}[\mathcal{A}]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}\int_{Z}\Omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\,\mathcal{A}\wedge\bar{\partial}\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{3}\,\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\right)\,. ShCS​[𝒜]=1(2​π​i)3​∫ZΩ∧tr⁡(12​𝒜∧∂¯​𝒜+13​𝒜∧𝒜∧𝒜).S_{\text{hCS}}[\mathcal{A}]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}\int_{Z}\Omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\,\mathcal{A}\wedge\bar{\partial}\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{3}\,\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\right)\,.

(2.18)


It is a gauge theory governing the integrability of smooth partial connections  \mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,\mathfrak{g}) 𝒜∈Ω0,1​(Z,𝔤)\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,\mathfrak{g}) on complex vector bundles  E\to Z E→ZE\to Z. Here,  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} is the antiholomorphic exterior derivative on  Z ZZ, and  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g} is the Lie algebra of the gauge group. When  Z ZZ is Calabi-Yau, holomorphic Chern-Simons arises as the string field theory of open strings in the topological B-model with target space  Z ZZ [29]. We will focus on a type I analogue of the B-model introduced in [14]. Its gauge group  G GG is determined by a stack of space-filling D5 branes. The gauge group is arbitrary at the classical level, but will be chosen to be  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) for the open string sector to couple to the closed string sector in an anomaly-free manner at the quantum level.




We will be interested in the case when  Z ZZ is the twistor space of some SD 4-manifold  M MM. This case was studied for flat twistor space  Z=\mathbb{PT} Z=ℙ​𝕋Z=\mathbb{PT} in [55, 38, 39, 15], but their analysis generalizes straightforwardly to curved twistor spaces.66
              6
              
              
              
            One generally needs a Kähler metric to write the worldsheet theory of the  (2,2) (2,2)(2,2)  \sigma σ\sigma-model, although the B-model topological twist does not require this. It is also standard to use a Kähler metric to impose harmonic gauges in the string field actions, although again this is not strictly necessary. For a compact self-dual 4-manifold  M MM, twistor space is Kähler if and only if  M=S^{4} M=S4M=S^{4} or  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} [51]. More generally, it is bimeromorphic to Kähler if and only if it is Moishezon [56, 57, 58]. We will always work on spacetimes (eg.  \mathbb{C}^{2} ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} or  \mathscr{O}(-1) 𝒪⁡(−1)\mathscr{O}(-1)) obtained from removing points from compact 4-manifolds (viz.  S^{4} S4S^{4} or  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}) possessing Kähler twistor spaces. This corresponds to removing projective lines from the twistor spaces, and the Kähler structure restricts naturally to the resulting geometry.
The canonical bundle of a general twistor space  Z ZZ is nontrivial. However, as we reviewed in the previous section, if the self-dual metric on  M MM is also Kähler, then  Z ZZ does admit a meromorphic 3-form  \Omega Ω\Omega with double poles on a quadric  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0. So we can still study holomorphic Chern-Simons on  Z-\{\check{\omega}=0\} Z−{ωˇ=0}Z-\{\check{\omega}=0\} by taking this  \Omega Ω\Omega as the 3-form in the action (2.18). This is equivalent to studying (2.18) on  Z ZZ but with “boundary conditions” on the partial connection  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} that ensure a well-defined variational principle. In order for the holomorphic Chern-Simons Lagrangian to be free of poles, we will demand the boundary conditions





 \mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,\mathscr{O}(-D)\otimes\mathrm{End}\,E)\,, 𝒜∈Ω0,1​(Z,𝒪⁡(−D)⊗End​E),\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,\mathscr{O}(-D)\otimes\mathrm{End}\,E)\,,

(2.19)


that is,  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} vanishes holomorphically to first order on the divisor  D=\{\check{\omega}=0\} D={ωˇ=0}D=\{\check{\omega}=0\}. Geometrically, this means that the holomorphic bundle built from  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} is fixed on the divisor  D DD. If we assume that our background bundle  E EE is trivialized on  D DD, then the bundles obtained from varying  E EE are also trivialized.




We can write  \mathcal{A}=\check{\omega}\,\varphi 𝒜=ωˇ​φ\mathcal{A}=\check{\omega}\,\varphi for some smooth  \varphi\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,K_{Z}^{1/2}\otimes\mathrm{End}\,E) φ∈Ω0,1​(Z,KZ1/2⊗End​E)\varphi\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,K_{Z}^{1/2}\otimes\mathrm{End}\,E). Using  \Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2} Ω=ωˇ−2\Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2}, the Lagrangian in terms of  \varphi φ\varphi is found to be (up to normalization)





 \frac{1}{2}\int\operatorname{tr}\varphi\bar{\partial}\varphi+\frac{1}{3}\int\check{\omega}\operatorname{tr}\varphi^{3}\,. 12​∫tr⁡φ​∂¯​φ+13​∫ωˇ​tr⁡φ3.\frac{1}{2}\int\operatorname{tr}\varphi\bar{\partial}\varphi+\frac{1}{3}\int\check{\omega}\operatorname{tr}\varphi^{3}\,.

(2.20)


The integrands in both terms in this equation are  (0,3) (0,3)(0,3) forms valued in the canonical bundle  K_{Z} KZK_{Z}. This is because  \varphi φ\varphi is twisted by  K_{Z}^{1/2} KZ1/2K_{Z}^{1/2} and  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} is twisted by  K_{Z}^{-1/2} KZ−1/2K_{Z}^{-1/2}. It is important to note that the kinetic term in this exression is non-degenerate, and that the interaction term tends to zero on Pontecorvo’s quadric where  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} vanishes.




Smooth automorphisms of  E EE induce gauge transformations





 \mathcal{A}\mapsto h^{-1}\mathcal{A}h+h^{-1}\bar{\partial}h\,,\qquad h\in\Gamma(\text{Aut}\,E)\,. 𝒜↦h−1​𝒜​h+h−1​∂¯​h,h∈Γ⁡(Aut​E).\mathcal{A}\mapsto h^{-1}\mathcal{A}h+h^{-1}\bar{\partial}h\,,\qquad h\in\Gamma(\text{Aut}\,E)\,.

(2.21)


Holomorphic Chern-Simons on  Z ZZ as defined above is invariant under those gauge transformations for which the transformed field continues to satisfy the boundary condition (2.19). We will also restrict attention to vector bundles  E\to Z E→ZE\to Z whose restrictions  E|_{L_{x}} E|LxE|_{L_{x}} to every twistor line  L_{x} LxL_{x} are trivial.77
              7
              
              
              
            The moduli space of  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) bundles on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} has two components classified by  \mathbb{Z}_{2} ℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}, the fundamental group of  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8). So bundles on  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} that restrict to non-trivial bundles on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} may indeed occur. Unfortunately, their spacetime interpretation is far from clear. A preliminary line of attack for Penrose transforming bundles that are non-trivial on a finite number of twistor lines is described in [59], and such bundles have in fact already started to occur in celestial holography in the guise of twistorial monopoles [60]! Alternatively, one can use  \mathrm{Spin}(8) Spin⁡(8)\mathrm{Spin}(8) as the gauge group (which a priori isn’t ruled out by chiral anomaly cancellation), and all stable  \mathrm{Spin}(8) Spin⁡(8)\mathrm{Spin}(8) bundles on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} are trivial.




Each twistor line carries two canonical points we call  0 00 and  \infty ∞\infty, where Pontecorvo’s quadric intersects  L_{x} LxL_{x}. The boundary conditions for our gauge field mean that the bundle is trivialized on the quadric, and so at the points  0,\infty\in L_{x} 0,∞∈Lx0,\infty\in L_{x}. Because the bundle is trivial on  L_{x} LxL_{x}, the trivialization at  0 00 extends uniquely to a trivialization on all of  L_{x} LxL_{x} (and similarly for the trivialization at  \infty ∞\infty). The two trivalizations differ by a point  \mathsf{g}(x)\in G 𝗀⁡(x)∈G\mathsf{g}(x)\in G. This means that we have associated a space-time field





 \mathsf{g}\in\text{Maps}(M,G) 𝗀∈Maps​(M,G)\mathsf{g}\in\text{Maps}(M,G)

(2.22)


to a field configuration on twistor space.




An equivalent way to think of  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g} is that it is the value of an open holomorphic Wilson line wrapping  L_{x} LxL_{x}. An open Wilson line is gauge invariant because the gauge field vanishes at  0 00 and  \infty ∞\infty.




Explicitly, we can find a frame  H\in\text{Maps}(Z,G) H∈Maps​(Z,G)H\in\text{Maps}(Z,G) for sections of  E EE that trivializes  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} when restricted to each  L_{x} LxL_{x}, i.e.,





 \mathcal{A}|_{L_{x}}=H^{-1}\bar{\partial}|_{L_{x}}H\qquad\forall\;x\in M\,. 𝒜|Lx=H−1​∂¯|Lx​H∀x∈M.\mathcal{A}|_{L_{x}}=H^{-1}\bar{\partial}|_{L_{x}}H\qquad\forall\;x\in M\,.

(2.23)


 H HH can be gauge fixed to equal the identity matrix in a neighborhood of  \infty ∞\infty on each  L_{x} LxL_{x}, and equal  \mathsf{g}(x) 𝗀⁡(x)\mathsf{g}(x) on a neighborhood of  0 00.
One can decompose  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} into horizontal and vertical  (0,1) (0,1)(0,1)-forms with respect to the Levi-Civita connection induced on  Z ZZ. The horizontal part of  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} enters quadratically in the action and can be integrated out by imposing its equation of motion. As reviewed in appendix B, in the frame (2.23) we can partially solve for  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} using this equation of motion. This yields





 \mathcal{A}=H^{-1}(\bar{\partial}+\pi_{0,1}A)H\,, 𝒜=H−1​(∂¯+π0,1​A)​H,\mathcal{A}=H^{-1}(\bar{\partial}+\pi_{0,1}A)H\,,

(2.24)


written in terms of a  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g}-valued spacetime 1-form  A AA (trivially pulled back to  Z ZZ using the projection  \pi:Z\to M π:Z→M\pi:Z\to M). The notation  \pi_{p,q}\alpha πp,q​α\pi_{p,q}\alpha denotes the  (p,q) (p,q)(p,q)-part of any  (p+q) (p+q)(p+q)-form  \alpha α\alpha on  Z ZZ.




The new field  A AA acts as a spacetime gauge field; alternatively it may be thought of as the zero mode of  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} under KK reduction of holomorphic Chern-Simons along the  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} fibers of  Z\to M Z→MZ\to M. Higher KK modes drop out and end up never contributing to the reduction. Using the boundary conditions on  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} and  H HH mentioned above, we obtain





 A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1}\,, A=−∂¯​𝗀​𝗀−1,A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1}\,,

(2.25)


fixing  A AA in terms of a single “positive helicity” degree of freedom  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g}. Here and in what follows, whenever  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} acts on a spacetime object, it represents the dbar operator on  M MM in the complex structure associated to its Kähler metric  g gg.




The compactification of (2.18) along the fibers of  Z\to M Z→MZ\to M is also briefly reviewed in appendix B. It is performed by plugging the solution (2.24) for  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} into (2.18) and integrating fiberwise. The dynamical dependence of (2.24) is purely along  M MM up to factors of the frame  H HH; and even the frame is pure gauge except at the two poles of  \Omega Ω\Omega where it can be completely fixed in terms of  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g}. As a result, the integral over the fibers can be performed explicitly without generating any KK modes. This results in a 4-dimensional Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW4) model on the scalar-flat Kähler manifold  M MM,





 S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\mathsf{g},\omega]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\mathsf{g}^{-1}\partial\mathsf{g}\wedge\mathsf{g}^{-1}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M\times[0,1]}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\tilde{\mathsf{g}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}\right)^{3}\,. SWZW4[𝗀,ω]=−i8​π2∫Mω∧tr(𝗀−1∂𝗀∧𝗀−1∂¯𝗀)+i24​π2∫M×[0,1]ω∧tr(𝗀~−1d𝗀~)3.S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\mathsf{g},\omega]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\mathsf{g}^{-1}\partial\mathsf{g}\wedge\mathsf{g}^{-1}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M\times[0,1]}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\tilde{\mathsf{g}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}\right)^{3}\,.

(2.26)


This action is a functional of the dynamical field  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g} as well as the choice of background Kähler form  \omega ω\omega on  M MM. Its first term is a standard kinetic term, with  \partial ∂\partial and  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} denoting holomorphic and antiholomorphic exterior derivatives on  M MM. The second is a 5-dimensional Wess-Zumino term.  \tilde{\mathsf{g}} 𝗀~\tilde{\mathsf{g}} is an extension of  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g} to  M\times[0,1] M×[0,1]M\times[0,1] representing a homotopy of  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g} to a fixed reference profile  \mathsf{g}_{0} 𝗀0\mathsf{g}_{0}. We will implicitly choose  \mathsf{g}_{0}=\mathbbm{1} 𝗀0=𝟙\mathsf{g}_{0}=\mathbbm{1} for the rest of this work.  \mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{g}} d​𝗀~\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{g}} represents the 5-dimensional exterior derivative of  \tilde{\mathsf{g}} 𝗀~\tilde{\mathsf{g}} on  M\times[0,1] M×[0,1]M\times[0,1], while  \omega ω\omega continues to denote the 4-dimensional Kähler form on  M MM in both terms.




The WZW4 model has been studied in many contexts [26, 27, 40]. As reviewed here, it describes the effective spacetime dynamics of open topological strings on twistor space. In the past, it provided a classical action principle for the self-dual Yang-Mills equation on  M MM [45], although the relation to gauge theory does not persist beyond tree level.88
              8
              
              
              
            Nonetheless, WZW4 seems to be related to 4d  \mathcal{N}=2 𝒩=2\mathcal{N}=2 heterotic strings at tree as well as loop level [61]. The equation of motion of  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g} reads





 \omega\wedge\bar{\partial}(\mathsf{g}^{-1}\partial\mathsf{g})=0\,. ω∧∂¯​(𝗀−1​∂𝗀)=0.\omega\wedge\bar{\partial}(\mathsf{g}^{-1}\partial\mathsf{g})=0\,.

(2.27)


This is known as Yang’s equation [62]. On its support, the gauge field  A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1} A=−∂¯​𝗀​𝗀−1A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1} solves the self-dual Yang-Mills equation  F_{A}^{-}=0 FA−=0F_{A}^{-}=0 on  M MM. As in the case for a 2-dimensional WZW model, the derivation of (2.27) involves cancelling certain contributions from the kinetic term against contributions from the Wess-Zumino term. For the action and the variational problem to be well-defined, one requires that the Wess-Zumino term be independent of the 5d extension  \tilde{\mathsf{g}} 𝗀~\tilde{\mathsf{g}} of the 4d field  \mathsf{g} 𝗀\mathsf{g}. This imposes the quantization condition [27]





 \frac{\omega}{2\pi}\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})\,, ω2​π∈H2​(M,ℤ),\frac{\omega}{2\pi}\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})\,,

(2.28)


showing that  \omega ω\omega acts as a 4d analogue of the Kac-Moody level familiar from 2d WZW models.




For perturbative calculations, it is convenient to work locally on the group manifold of  G GG in terms of an adjoint-valued scalar  \phi ϕ\phi by writing





 \mathsf{g}=\mathrm{e}^{\phi}\,,\qquad\phi\in\text{Maps}(M,\mathfrak{g})\,. 𝗀=eϕ,ϕ∈Maps​(M,𝔤).\mathsf{g}=\mathrm{e}^{\phi}\,,\qquad\phi\in\text{Maps}(M,\mathfrak{g})\,.

(2.29)


As its 5-dimensional extension, one can take  \tilde{\mathsf{g}}=\mathrm{e}^{t\phi} 𝗀~=et​ϕ\tilde{\mathsf{g}}=\mathrm{e}^{t\phi}, where  t\in[0,1] t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1] is a coordinate along the interval. Performing such a field redefinition and integrating out  t tt converts the action (2.26) to





 S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\omega]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi-\frac{1}{3}\,\phi\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{4})\right)\,, SWZW4[ϕ,ω]=−i8​π2∫Mω∧tr(∂ϕ∧∂¯ϕ−13ϕ[∂ϕ,∂¯ϕ]+O(ϕ4)),S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\omega]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi-\frac{1}{3}\,\phi\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{4})\right)\,,

(2.30)


where  [\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\equiv\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi+\bar{\partial}\phi\wedge\partial\phi [∂ϕ,∂¯​ϕ]≡∂ϕ∧∂¯​ϕ+∂¯​ϕ∧∂ϕ[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\equiv\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi+\bar{\partial}\phi\wedge\partial\phi is standard notation. The field equation of  \phi ϕ\phi reads





 \omega\wedge\bigg(\partial\bar{\partial}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{3})\bigg)=0\,. ω∧(∂∂¯​ϕ+12​[∂ϕ,∂¯​ϕ]+O⁡(ϕ3))=0.\omega\wedge\bigg(\partial\bar{\partial}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{3})\bigg)=0\,.

(2.31)


The corresponding linearized field equation is  \omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=0 ω∧∂∂¯​ϕ=0\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=0, which is just the Laplace equation associated to the Kähler metric on  M MM.




We will use this form of the action in later sections to build Feynman rules and derive some simple 2- and 3-point tree amplitudes of WZW4 on Burns space. Remembering the classical equivalence of this model with (a gauge-fixed formulation of) SD Yang-Mills, we will often refer to these amplitudes as tree level all-plus “gluon” amplitudes, though we emphasize that WZW4 is not actually a gauge theory.





2.3 Closed strings and Mabuchi gravity




The closed string sector of the topological B-model governs complex structure deformations of a Calabi-Yau 3-fold  Z ZZ. Locally, a complex structure deformation is described by a Beltrami differential  \mu\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,T^{1,0}Z) μ∈Ω0,1​(Z,T1,0​Z)\mu\in\Omega^{0,1}(Z,T^{1,0}Z) that deforms its dbar operator





 \bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu\,. ∂¯↦∂¯+μ.\bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu\,.

(2.32)


The deformed almost complex structure is integrable if and only if  \mu μ\mu solves the Maurer-Cartan equation





 \bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\,[\mu,\mu]=0\,. ∂¯​μ+12​[μ,μ]=0.\bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\,[\mu,\mu]=0\,.

(2.33)


Here,  [\mu,\mu] [μ,μ][\mu,\mu] denotes the wedge product on the  (0,1) (0,1)(0,1)-form factors in  \mu μ\mu and the Lie bracket on the  (1,0) (1,0)(1,0)-vector field factors.




The Bershadsky-Cecotti-Ooguri-Vafa (BCOV) [63] theory – also known as Kodaira-Spencer gravity – is an action principle that gives rise to this Maurer-Cartan equation as its equation of motion. It arises as the string field theory describing closed strings in the B-model [63]. The BCOV action is nonlocal in nature,





 S_{\text{BCOV}}[\mu]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}\int_{Z}\frac{1}{2}\,\bar{\partial}(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)\wedge\partial^{-1}(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)+\frac{1}{6}\,(\mu^{3}\!\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)\wedge\Omega\,, SBCOV​[μ]=1(2​π​i)3​∫Z12​∂¯​(μ​⌟​Ω)∧∂−1(μ​⌟​Ω)+16​(μ3​⌟​Ω)∧Ω,S_{\text{BCOV}}[\mu]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}\int_{Z}\frac{1}{2}\,\bar{\partial}(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)\wedge\partial^{-1}(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)+\frac{1}{6}\,(\mu^{3}\!\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)\wedge\Omega\,,

(2.34)


where  \partial ∂\partial is the holomorphic exterior derivative on  Z ZZ and  \lrcorner ⌟\lrcorner denotes interior product. The field  \eta η\eta is constrained by





 \partial\eta=0\,,\qquad\eta\vcentcolon=\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega\in\Omega^{2,1}(Z)\,. ∂η=0,η:=μ​⌟​Ω∈Ω2,1​(Z).\partial\eta=0\,,\qquad\eta\vcentcolon=\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega\in\Omega^{2,1}(Z)\,.

(2.35)


This implies that the  (2,1) (2,1)(2,1)-form  \eta η\eta as defined here is locally  \partial ∂\partial-exact,





 \eta=\partial\gamma\,,\qquad\gamma\in\Omega^{1,1}(Z)\,, η=∂γ,γ∈Ω1,1​(Z),\eta=\partial\gamma\,,\qquad\gamma\in\Omega^{1,1}(Z)\,,

(2.36)


so that  \partial^{-1}\eta=\gamma ∂−1η=γ\partial^{-1}\eta=\gamma modulo  \ker\partial ker∂\ker\partial. In spite of having a nonlocal kinetic term, the BCOV action has a perfectly well-behaved equation of motion and perturbative expansion. It is also invariant under smooth diffeomorphisms of  Z ZZ generated by exponentiating the linearized transformations





 \delta\mu=\bar{\partial}\xi+\mu\,\lrcorner\,\partial\xi-\xi\,\lrcorner\,\partial\mu δ​μ=∂¯​ξ+μ​⌟​∂ξ−ξ​⌟​∂μ\delta\mu=\bar{\partial}\xi+\mu\,\lrcorner\,\partial\xi-\xi\,\lrcorner\,\partial\mu

(2.37)


for some  \xi\in\Omega^{0}(Z,T^{1,0}Z) ξ∈Ω0​(Z,T1,0​Z)\xi\in\Omega^{0}(Z,T^{1,0}Z) satisfying  \partial(\xi\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)=0 ∂(ξ​⌟​Ω)=0\partial(\xi\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)=0.




Again, we specialize to the case when  Z ZZ is the twistor space of a scalar-flat Kähler spacetime. Our boundary conditions are simply that the  (2,1) (2,1)(2,1) form  \eta η\eta remains smooth at the divisor  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0. This implies that  \mu μ\mu must vanish to second order at  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0. The BCOV Lagrangian is then smooth everywhere and leads to a well-defined variational problem. Its kinetic term is unchanged when written in terms of  \eta η\eta, so that it has a well-defined propagator and pertubative expansion. Its diffeomorphism symmetry is also reduced to the consideration of those diffeomorphisms of  Z ZZ that preserve these boundary conditions. At the level of linearized diffeomorphisms, this simply requires that  \xi ξ\xi vanish to second order at  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0.




The reduction of the BCOV action to spacetime gives rise to a theory of scalar-flat Kähler fluctuations of the background scalar-flat Kähler metric [15]. Compactification along the twistor lines is now much more involved, so we only provide an executive summary in appendix B.2.




The dynamical field on spacetime is found to be a single scalar field  \rho(x) ρ⁡(x)\rho(x). In the linearized theory, this is related to the Beltrami differential by a Penrose integral formula





 \rho(x)=\int_{L_{x}}\gamma\,. ρ⁡(x)=∫Lxγ.\rho(x)=\int_{L_{x}}\gamma\,.

(2.38)


This scalar field is interpreted as a perturbation of the background Kähler potential of  M MM





 K\mapsto\mathcal{K}=K+\rho\,. K↦𝒦=K+ρ.K\mapsto\mathcal{K}=K+\rho\,.

(2.39)


The function  \rho ρ\rho depends on the choice of  (1,1) (1,1)(1,1) form  \gamma γ\gamma with  \partial\gamma=\eta ∂γ=η\partial\gamma=\eta. However, the closed  1 11-form  \mathrm{d}\rho d​ρ\mathrm{d}\rho on spacetime does not depend on the choice of  \gamma γ\gamma. This means that  \rho ρ\rho is defined up to the addition of a constant. The deformed Kähler metric takes the form





 \mathrm{d}s^{2}=2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{K}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}} d​s2=2​∂2𝒦∂uα˙​∂u^β˙​d​uα˙​d​u^β˙\mathrm{d}s^{2}=2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{K}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}

(2.40)


written in local complex coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙,u^α˙u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} that are modeled after the flat space coordinates of (2.6).




When  \mu μ\mu obeys its equation of motion on twistor space, the deformed Kähler metric on spacetime continues to be scalar-flat. The Ricci scalar can be viewed either as a trace of the Ricci tensor with respect to the metric, or as a trace of the Ricci form with respect to the Kähler form. Using the latter viewpoint, scalar-flatness is best imposed as the orthogonality of the Kähler form and the Ricci form:





 \varpi\wedge\mathcal{P}=0\,, ϖ∧𝒫=0,\varpi\wedge\mathcal{P}=0\,,

(2.41)


where  \varpi ϖ\varpi and  \mathcal{P} 𝒫\mathcal{P} are the deformed metric’s Kähler and Ricci forms,





 \displaystyle\varpi ϖ\displaystyle\varpi
 \displaystyle=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}\,, =ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ=i​∂∂¯​𝒦,\displaystyle=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}\,,

(2.42)



 \displaystyle\mathcal{P} 𝒫\displaystyle\mathcal{P}
 \displaystyle=P-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\,, =P−i​∂∂¯​log⁡ϖ2ω2,\displaystyle=P-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\,,

(2.43)


and  P=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\log\det(\partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}K) P=−i∂∂¯logdet(∂uα˙∂u^β˙K)P=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\log\det(\partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}K) denotes the Ricci form of the background Kähler metric on  M MM.




There is a standard action functional for this equation known as the Mabuchi functional, first introduced in [28] for the study of constant scalar curvature Kähler metrics. We will propose the following variant of this functional as the gravitational sector of our duality:





 S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\varpi^{2}\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+\mathrm{i}\,P\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho\,. SM[ρ]=−18​π2∫Mϖ2logϖ2ω2+iP∧∂ρ∧∂¯ρ.S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\varpi^{2}\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+\mathrm{i}\,P\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho\,.

(2.44)


For the scalar-flat case under consideration, this is equivalent to the Mabuchi functional (as displayed for instance in the recent review [64]) up to boundary terms. So we continue to refer to it as the Mabuchi action. A few integrations by parts show that this can also be written in a background invariant form reminiscent of a  \mathrm{U}(1) U⁡(1)\mathrm{U}(1) WZW4 model,





 S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\mathcal{P}\wedge\partial\mathcal{K}\wedge\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}\,. SM[ρ]=−i8​π2∫M𝒫∧∂𝒦∧∂¯𝒦.S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\mathcal{P}\wedge\partial\mathcal{K}\wedge\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}\,.

(2.45)


Since boundary terms tend to be a tricky point in holography, we will choose to treat (2.44) as our bulk action in what follows. We note that all forms of the action are invariant under the gauge symmetry of  \rho ρ\rho which shifts it by a constant. They are also invariant under Kähler transformations  \mathcal{K}(u,\hat{u})\mapsto\mathcal{K}(u,\hat{u})+f(u)+\tilde{f}(\hat{u}) 𝒦⁡(u,u^)↦𝒦⁡(u,u^)+f⁡(u)+f~​(u^)\mathcal{K}(u,\hat{u})\mapsto\mathcal{K}(u,\hat{u})+f(u)+\tilde{f}(\hat{u}) up to boundary terms.




Remembering  \varpi=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho ϖ=ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ\varpi=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho and background scalar-flatness  \omega\wedge P=0 ω∧P=0\omega\wedge P=0, it is straightforward to compute the variation of (2.44) with respect to  \rho ρ\rho,





 \begin{split}\delta S_{\text{M}}&=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}(\delta\rho)\wedge\varpi\left(\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+1\right)-\delta\rho\,\varpi\wedge P\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\delta\rho\;\varpi\wedge\mathcal{P}\,,\end{split} δ​SM=−14​π2∫Mi∂∂¯(δρ)∧ϖ(logϖ2ω2+1)−δρϖ∧P=14​π2​∫Mδ​ρ​ϖ∧𝒫,\begin{split}\delta S_{\text{M}}&=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}(\delta\rho)\wedge\varpi\left(\log\frac{\varpi^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+1\right)-\delta\rho\,\varpi\wedge P\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\delta\rho\;\varpi\wedge\mathcal{P}\,,\end{split}

(2.46)


having dropped any boundary terms. Hence, the critical points of the Mabuchi functional correspond to scalar-flat Kähler metrics on  M MM obtained from perturbing a fixed background scalar-flat Kähler metric.




The field content of Mabuchi gravity also couples to the WZW4 model in a canonical fashion. One simply makes the replacement





 \omega\mapsto\varpi\,,\qquad S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\omega]\mapsto S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\varpi] ω↦ϖ,SWZW4​[ϕ,ω]↦SWZW4​[ϕ,ϖ]\omega\mapsto\varpi\,,\qquad S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\omega]\mapsto S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\varpi]

(2.47)


in the action (2.26). At the level of twistor space, this coupling arises from replacing  \bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu ∂¯↦∂¯+μ\bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu in the holomorphic Chern-Simons action (2.18). If  \rho ρ\rho is taken to be a globally defined scalar field,  \omega ω\omega and  \varpi=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho ϖ=ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ\varpi=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho live in the same Kähler class. In particular, the Kähler class of  \varpi ϖ\varpi continues to be integral, which was essential for the WZW4 action to be well-defined. In what follows, we will take this to be the case.




To better understand the perturbative expansion of Mabuchi gravity, let us expand (2.44) to cubic order in  \rho ρ\rho. Recalling the formula  \triangle\rho=4\mathrm{i}\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho/\omega^{2} △​ρ=4​i​ω∧∂∂¯​ρ/ω2\triangle\rho=4\mathrm{i}\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho/\omega^{2} for the Laplacian on Kähler 4-manifolds, we can first compute the expansion of  \log\varpi^{2}/\omega^{2} log⁡ϖ2/ω2\log\varpi^{2}/\omega^{2},





 \displaystyle\log\frac{(\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}} log⁡(ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ)2ω2\displaystyle\log\frac{(\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}
 \displaystyle=\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathrm{i}\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho}{\omega^{2}}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\right)=\log\left(1+\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\right) =log⁡(1+2​i​ω∧∂∂¯​ρω2−(∂∂¯​ρ)2ω2)=log⁡(1+△​ρ2−(∂∂¯​ρ)2ω2)\displaystyle=\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathrm{i}\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho}{\omega^{2}}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\right)=\log\left(1+\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\right)





 \displaystyle=\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}-\frac{(\triangle\rho)^{2}}{8}+\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{(\triangle\rho)^{3}}{24}+\mathrm{O}(\rho^{4})\,, =△​ρ2−(∂∂¯​ρ)2ω2−(△​ρ)28+△​ρ2​(∂∂¯​ρ)2ω2+(△​ρ)324+O⁡(ρ4),\displaystyle=\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}-\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}-\frac{(\triangle\rho)^{2}}{8}+\frac{\triangle\rho}{2}\frac{(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{(\triangle\rho)^{3}}{24}+\mathrm{O}(\rho^{4})\,,

(2.48)


To cubic order, the corresponding expansion of the Mabuchi action reads





 S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{1}{64\pi^{2}}\int_{M}(\triangle\rho)^{2}\omega^{2}+8\mathrm{i}P\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho-4(\triangle\rho)(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}-\frac{1}{6}(\triangle\rho)^{3}\omega^{2}+\mathrm{O}(\rho^{4})\,, SM[ρ]=−164​π2∫M(△ρ)2ω2+8iP∧∂ρ∧∂¯ρ−4(△ρ)(∂∂¯ρ)2−16(△ρ)3ω2+O(ρ4),S_{\text{M}}[\rho]=-\frac{1}{64\pi^{2}}\int_{M}(\triangle\rho)^{2}\omega^{2}+8\mathrm{i}P\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho-4(\triangle\rho)(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2}-\frac{1}{6}(\triangle\rho)^{3}\omega^{2}+\mathrm{O}(\rho^{4})\,,

(2.49)


where we have dropped globally exact terms like  (\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2} (∂∂¯​ρ)2(\partial\bar{\partial}\rho)^{2} and  \omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho ω∧∂∂¯​ρ\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho from the expanded Lagrangian. In flat space, the background Ricci form vanishes,  P=0 P=0P=0. Up to normalization conventions for the Kähler form, this action then reduces to the one derived in [15] by compactifying BCOV theory along the  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} fibers of the twistor space of  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. On a more general background, it is also appended with the term  P\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho P∧∂ρ∧∂¯​ρP\wedge\partial\rho\wedge\bar{\partial}\rho proportional to the background Ricci form. This extra term is crucial for deriving the correct linearized wavefunctions for  \rho ρ\rho, which will enter some of the tests of our holographic duality in later sections.




The relative normalization between the WZW4 action and the Mabuchi action is fixed by demanding that the coupled theory lifts to an anomaly-free, holomorphic theory on twistor space. This is accomplished through a Green-Schwarz mechanism that we review below. Alternatively, on spacetime, it is determined by demanding that the one loop 4-point  \phi ϕ\phi amplitude vanish in the coupled theory in flat space. The interested reader may refer to [15] for more details of the precise normalizations.





2.4 Anomaly cancellation and renormalizability




Holomorphic Chern-Simons theory and BCOV theory are naturally coupled, which is immediate from their origins as open (resp., closed) string sectors of topological string theory.
Holomorphic Chern-Simons theory on a complex three-fold suffers from a one-loop gauge anomaly, associated to the diagram in Figure 1.

 Refer to caption 
Figure 1: The gauge anomaly for holomorphic Chern-Simons.  




In [14], it was shown that, for certain gauge groups, this anomaly can be cancelled by a Green-Schwarz mechanism, involving the exchange of a closed string field. This is depicted in Figure 2
.

 Refer to caption 
Figure 2: The Green-Schwarz mechanism, showing cancellation of the one-loop open string anomaly by a tree level diagram involving exchange of closed string fields. 




(This tadpole cancellation mechanism was previously known in the topological A-model from the world-sheet perspective [65].) In [14] it was shown that with holomorphic Chern-Simons gauge group  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8), anomaly cancellation occurs at all orders99
                9
                
                
                
              There is a folklore belief that anomalies only occur at one loop, but this is false. A counterexample is given in [66].  in perturbation theory.




Further, the constraint that all anomalies are cancelled fixes all counter-terms uniquely. This is worth noting, because BCOV theory and holomorphic Chern-Simons theory are both non-renormalizable by power counting. Intuitively, one should think that renormalizability of these theories is due to the large amount of gauge symmetry. In contrast to ordinary gauge theory and gravity, the group of gauge transformations in these theories which preserve the zero field configuration is infinite dimensional. It is the group of holomorphic gauge transformations or the group of holomorphic volume-preserving diffeomorphisms respectively.




This result has an important consequence for the four-dimensional theories we are considering, which was emphasized in [15].



Theorem 1.



Mabuchi gravity coupled to WZW4 for the group  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) admits a canonically defined quantization on a scalar-flat Kähler manifold, despite being non-renormalizable.





This canonically defined quantization is characterized by asking that it lifts, at the quantum level, to a local theory on twistor space. From the spacetime perspective, any theory that lifts to twistor space in a local manner has the following features:

1. 



It has no scattering amplitudes on flat space.

2. 



On flat space, the analytically-continued RG flow is periodic, with imaginary period  2\pi\mathrm{i} 2​π​i2\pi\mathrm{i}. (This means that all coupling constants only depend on the logarithm  \lambda λ\lambda of the scale by periodic functions  e^{n\lambda} en​λe^{n\lambda} for  n nn an integer).

3. 



On any scalar-flat Kähler manifold, all correlation functions are meromorphic functions of the complexified spacetime coordinates.



These features strongly constrain all interactions and counter-terms. For instance, in [15] it was shown that the cubic term in the Mabuchi functional is distinguished (among interactions of dimension  6 66) by the constraint that it does not generate any logarithmic OPEs.





2.5 The Mabuchi theory as a gravitational theory




Mabuchi gravity is a theory of a dynamical scalar field, which has the geometric interpretation as the Kähler potential of a metric. The diffeomorphism symmetry, however, is not gauged.




Gravitational actions where diffeomorphism symmetry is not gauged are familiar in other contexts. For instance, the Liouville theory in dimension  2 22 is a Lagrangian for a scalar field  \phi ϕ\phi which can also be interpreted as a model for quantum gravity (e.g. [67]). If one interprets  e^{\phi} eϕe^{\phi} as a conformal factor multiplying a flat metric, then Liouville’s equation for  \phi ϕ\phi is equivalent to Einstein’s equation.




In Liouville theory, as in Mabuchi theory, one does not gauge diffeomorphism symmetries, so it is a gauge-fixed form of Einstein gravity. Earlier work coupling Liouville and Mabuchi actions in dimension  2 22 appears in [68].




One might object that Mabuchi gravity should not really be thought of as “gravitational” for this reason. One might further object that standard holography, where the boundary CFT has a well-behaved stress tensor, cannot hold unless the bulk gravitational theory gauges diffeomorphism symmetry.




Our holographic duality is saved because it is not a standard holographic duality on four-dimensional spacetime. It only becomes a standard holographic duality on twistor space. In the next section, we will show that the twistor space of the Burns metric is essentially  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{H}^{3}\times S^{3} SL2​(ℂ)≃ℍ3×S3\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{H}^{3}\times S^{3}, i.e., Euclidean AdS {}_{3}\times S^{3} 3×S3{}_{3}\times S^{3} (up to certain extra boundary divisors). On twistor space, our duality is a completely standard holographic duality. Moreover, diffeomorphisms are gauged on twistor space! It is only the boundary conditions at  0,\infty 0,∞0,\infty on the twistor  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}’s that allow us to fully gauge-fix the diffeomorphism symmetry.






3 Burns space from brane backreaction




To construct our holographic duality, we start with the twistor space of  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. Recall that this was given by the bundle  \mathbb{PT}=\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} ℙ​𝕋=𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{PT}=\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}. We will wrap a stack of  N NN D1 branes on the zero section of this bundle. Wrapping D1 branes on the zero section is a natural choice from the point of view of celestial holography: by construction, the zero section is the celestial sphere of the origin of flat four-dimensional spacetime (i.e. prior to backreacting). We will compute the backreaction as a deformation of complex structure. The resulting backreacted geometry will get identified with the twistor space of a scalar-flat Kähler spacetime known as Burns space.




3.1 The physical string setup




Before passing to the twisted holographic setup, it is useful to outline the 10d physical string uplift of our basic model. We will restrict ourselves to the string theory compactified on the twistor space of flat space (i.e. before computing the backreaction arising from the addition of  N NN D-branes), and will pursue the computation of the backreaction to the twistor space of Burns space only in the corresponding topological string theory. We leave to future work the study of the open/closed duality in the full physical string theory.




The ingredients for our basic setup have been anticipated in [15]. The starting point is a  3 33-fold  X XX which is a fibration  \pi:X\rightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{1} π:X→ℂ​ℙ1\pi:X\rightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{1} where the fiber is an elliptic K3 surface. As explained in [15], we require that the fibration must be chosen to ensure the following condition on the canonical bundle
