4.1 Large  N NN BRST cohomology




States on Burns space will be dual to BRST invariant single-trace operators of the chiral algebra in the large  N NN limit. The BRST cohomology can be computed at large  N NN using tools from homological algebra [87, 88]. Details are provided in appendix C. Massive states like giant gravitons [89] are also expected to play an interesting role at finite  N NN, but they lie beyond the scope of this work.




In what follows, for each  \dot{\alpha}=\dot{1},\dot{2}, α˙=1˙,2˙,\dot{\alpha}=\dot{1},\dot{2}, we will view  (X^{\dot{\alpha}})^{i}{}_{j}\equiv\omega^{ik}X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{kj} (Xα˙)i≡jωi​kXk​jα˙(X^{\dot{\alpha}})^{i}{}_{j}\equiv\omega^{ik}X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{kj} as endomorphisms of the fundamental representation  V VV of  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N). Similarly,  I_{r}{}^{i}\equiv\omega^{ij}I_{rj} Iri≡ωi​jIr​jI_{r}{}^{i}\equiv\omega^{ij}I_{rj} and  I_{ri} Ir​iI_{ri} will be viewed as a vector or covector in the fundamental representation, with  r rr an  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) index. A basis for the BRST cohomology is





 \begin{split}J_{rs}[m,n]&=\omega\bigl(I_{r},(X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}I_{s}\bigr)\,,\\
E[m,n]&=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl((X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}\bigr)\,,\\
F[m,n]&=\operatorname{Tr}\left([X\,\partial X](X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}\right)+\text{terms with ghosts}\,.\end{split} Jr​s​[m,n]=ω⁡(Ir,(X1˙)(mCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENn)​Is),E⁡[m,n]=Tr⁡((X1˙)(mCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENn)),F⁡[m,n]=Tr⁡([X​∂X]​(X1˙)(mCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENn))+terms with ghosts.\begin{split}J_{rs}[m,n]&=\omega\bigl(I_{r},(X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}I_{s}\bigr)\,,\\

(4.17)


For example,  J_{rs}[1,0]=I_{ri}(X^{\dot{1}})^{i}{}_{j}I_{s}{}^{j} Jr​s[1,0]=Ir​i(X1˙)iIsjjJ_{rs}[1,0]=I_{ri}(X^{\dot{1}})^{i}{}_{j}I_{s}{}^{j},  E[2,0]=\omega^{ij}(X^{\dot{1}})_{ik}(X^{\dot{1}})^{k}{}_{j} E[2,0]=ωi​j(X1˙)i​k(X1˙)kjE[2,0]=\omega^{ij}(X^{\dot{1}})_{ik}(X^{\dot{1}})^{k}{}_{j}, etc.




In these expressions,  (X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)} (X1˙)(mCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENn)(X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)} is shorthand notation for the symmetrized product of these  n+m n+mn+m matrices. Also,  [X\,\partial X]=X^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial X_{\dot{\alpha}} [X​∂X]=Xα˙​∂Xα˙[X\,\partial X]=X^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial X_{\dot{\alpha}} uses the notation for spinor contractions introduced earlier. The currents  F[m,n] F⁡[m,n]F[m,n] receive corrections containing ghosts that ensure BRST closure, but we have suppressed these as we will not be needing them in the sequel. (In particular,  F[0,0] F⁡[0,0]F[0,0] is the stress tensor operator).




The currents  J_{rs}[m,n] Jr​s​[m,n]J_{rs}[m,n] are anti-symmetric in the  r,s r,sr,s indices, because  \omega ω\omega is anti-symmetric and because





 \omega(I_{r},X^{\dot{\alpha}}I_{s})=\omega(X^{\dot{\alpha}}I_{r},I_{s}). ω⁡(Ir,Xα˙​Is)=ω⁡(Xα˙​Ir,Is).\omega(I_{r},X^{\dot{\alpha}}I_{s})=\omega(X^{\dot{\alpha}}I_{r},I_{s}).

(4.18)


Therefore we can view  r,s r,sr,s as being an index in the adjoint for  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8). We will often suppress the  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) inner product and write these currents simply as  J_{rs}[m,n]=I_{r}(X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}I_{s} Jr​s​[m,n]=Ir​(X1˙)(mCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENn)​IsJ_{rs}[m,n]=I_{r}(X^{\dot{1}})^{(m}(X^{\dot{2}})^{n)}I_{s}, interpreting  I_{r} IrI_{r} as a covector and  I_{s} IsI_{s} as a vector of  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N). To lighten notation, the composite index  rs r​srs will also often be replaced with an adjoint index  a aa for  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8).





4.2 Defect boundary conditions from Koszul duality




To complete the duality, we would like to prescribe boundary conditions on our chiral algebra fields near the defects at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty. We will obtain boundary conditions on the composite currents  J[k,l],E[k,l],F[k,l] J⁡[k,l],E⁡[k,l],F⁡[k,l]J[k,l],E[k,l],F[k,l] with relative ease using the Koszul duality 1818
              18
              
              
              
            One can also use Koszul duality to study maps between modules of Koszul dual algebras, which has been explored recently from a twistorial perspective in [60]. It would be interesting to study Koszul dual modules further, but we will not do so in this work. approach espoused in [17, 18, 33]. Boundary conditions on the fundamental fields  X,I X,IX,I that give rise to these boundary conditions on the composite currents are described in the next section.




Koszul duality helps determine the most general couplings of the D1-brane chiral algebra to topological strings in the bulk. The chiral algebra couples in a standard way to the fields of the type I B-model on  \mathbb{C}^{3} ℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3}, with volume form





 \mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v\,. d​z​d2​v.\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v\,.

(4.19)


We are interested in a chiral algebra which couples to the fields of the type I B-model on  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT}, the twistor space of  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, with the meromorphic volume form





 \Omega=z^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v\,. Ω=z−2​d​z​d2​v.\Omega=z^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v\,.

(4.20)


Building such couplings will lead us to impose appropriate boundary conditions on the chiral algebra operators.




Let us first recall how the chiral algebra couples to  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) holomorphic Chern-Simons theory on  \mathbb{C}^{3} ℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3} with the volume form  \mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v d​z​d2​v\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{2}v. If





 \mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C}^{3},\mathfrak{so}(8)) 𝒜∈Ω0,1​(ℂ3,𝔰​𝔬​(8))\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C}^{3},\mathfrak{so}(8))

(4.21)


is the gauge field of holomorphic Chern-Simons theory, the coupling (at tree level) is by





 \sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\mathrm{d}z\;J[m,n]\;\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A}\,. ∑m,n≥01m!​n!​∫vα˙=0d​z​J​[m,n]​∂v1˙m∂v2˙n𝒜.\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\mathrm{d}z\;J[m,n]\;\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A}\,.

(4.22)


Including  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty, flat space  \mathbb{C}^{3} ℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3} naturally completes to the resolved conifold





 \mathscr{O}(-1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(-1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\,. 𝒪⁡(−1)⊕𝒪⁡(−1)→ℂ​ℙ1.\mathscr{O}(-1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(-1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\,.

(4.23)


Since  \mathscr{O}(-1) 𝒪⁡(−1)\mathscr{O}(-1) is the square root of the canonical bundle on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}, the operator  \partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} ∂vα˙\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} is of spin  -\frac{1}{2} −12-\frac{1}{2}. Since  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n] is of spin  1+\frac{1}{2}(m+n) 1+12​(m+n)1+\frac{1}{2}(m+n), the whole expression is of spin  1 11 and is therefore something that can be integrated against the  (0,1) (0,1)(0,1) form  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A}.




Away from the locus  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty, this formula can be adapted to twistor space. This patch of twistor space is isomorphic to a patch of  \mathbb{C}^{3} ℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3} when we use the coordinates  z,z^{-1}v^{\dot{\alpha}} z,z−1​vα˙z,z^{-1}v^{\dot{\alpha}}. The point is that the volume form on twistor space in these coordinates is





 \Omega=\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}(z^{-1}v^{\dot{1}})\wedge\mathrm{d}(z^{-1}v^{\dot{2}})\,. Ω=d​z∧d⁡(z−1​v1˙)∧d⁡(z−1​v2˙).\Omega=\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}(z^{-1}v^{\dot{1}})\wedge\mathrm{d}(z^{-1}v^{\dot{2}})\,.

(4.24)


Therefore, the theory on the D1 brane couples to holomorphic Chern-Simons theory on this patch by the formula





 \sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\mathrm{d}z\;z^{m+n}J[m,n]\;\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A} ∑m,n≥01m!​n!​∫vα˙=0d​z​zm+n​J​[m,n]​∂v1˙m∂v2˙n𝒜\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\mathrm{d}z\;z^{m+n}J[m,n]\;\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A}

(4.25)


which is obtained by replacing  v^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto z^{-1}v^{\dot{\alpha}} vα˙↦z−1​vα˙v^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto z^{-1}v^{\dot{\alpha}} in (4.22). This makes sense, because on twistor space  \partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} ∂vα˙\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} is of spin  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}, but  z\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} z∂vα˙z\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} is of spin  -\frac{1}{2} −12-\frac{1}{2}.1919
              19
              
              
              
            A description in homogeneous coordinates may be helpful for practitioners of twistor theory. If one introduces homogeneous coordinates  [\mu^{\dot{\alpha}},\lambda_{\alpha}] [μα˙,λα][\mu^{\dot{\alpha}},\lambda_{\alpha}] on  \mathbb{PT}=\mathbb{CP}^{3}-\mathbb{CP}^{1} ℙ​𝕋=ℂ​ℙ3−ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{PT}=\mathbb{CP}^{3}-\mathbb{CP}^{1} by setting  z=\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1},v^{\dot{\alpha}}=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}/\lambda_{1} z=λ2/λ1,vα˙=μα˙/λ1z=\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1},v^{\dot{\alpha}}=\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}/\lambda_{1}, then the fields  X,I X,IX,I live on the twistor line  \mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=0 μα˙=0\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=0 and are valued in  \mathscr{O}(-1) 𝒪⁡(−1)\mathscr{O}(-1) each. The composite current  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n] is  \mathscr{O}(-m-n-2) 𝒪⁡(−m−n−2)\mathscr{O}(-m-n-2)-valued. So it couples to the bulk through the coupling 




 \frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\langle\lambda\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\rangle\;(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{m+n}J[m,n]\;\partial_{\mu^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{\mu^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A}\,. 1m!​n!​∫μα˙=0⟨λ​𝑑λ⟩​(λ1​λ2)m+n​J​[m,n]​∂μ1˙m∂μ2˙n𝒜.\frac{1}{m!n!}\int_{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=0}\langle\lambda\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\rangle\;(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{m+n}J[m,n]\;\partial_{\mu^{\dot{1}}}^{m}\partial_{\mu^{\dot{2}}}^{n}\mathcal{A}\,.

This reduces to the coupling in (4.25) if one sets  \lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z) λα=(1,z)\lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z),  \mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=v^{\dot{\alpha}} μα˙=vα˙\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}=v^{\dot{\alpha}}. Similar expressions can be written for couplings to  E[m,n] E⁡[m,n]E[m,n],  F[m,n] F⁡[m,n]F[m,n].




A similar, but more complicated, analysis applies to the closed string fields. The closed string field in the topological string is an element  \eta\in\Omega^{2,1} η∈Ω2,1\eta\in\Omega^{2,1} which is  \partial ∂\partial-closed. We can write





 \eta=\partial\gamma η=∂γ\eta=\partial\gamma

(4.26)


for a  (1,1) (1,1)(1,1)-form  \gamma γ\gamma, defined up to  \gamma\sim\gamma+\partial\alpha γ∼γ+∂α\gamma\sim\gamma+\partial\alpha for some  \alpha\in\Omega^{0,1} α∈Ω0,1\alpha\in\Omega^{0,1}. Then the coupling between the  E,F E,FE,F towers and  \eta η\eta is given by





 \begin{split}&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int E[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\gamma\\
&+\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int\mathrm{d}z\;F[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\iota_{\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v_{\dot{\alpha}}}}\gamma.\end{split} ∑m,n≥01m!​n!​∫E⁡[m,n]​ℒ∂v1˙m​ℒ∂v2˙n​γ+∑m,n≥01m!​n!∫dzF[m,n]ℒ∂v1˙mℒ∂v2˙nι∂vα˙ℒ∂vα˙γ.\begin{split}&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int E[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\gamma\\
&+\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int\mathrm{d}z\;F[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\iota_{\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v_{\dot{\alpha}}}}\gamma.\end{split}

(4.27)


Here,  \mathcal{L}_{\xi}\gamma ℒξ​γ\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\gamma denotes Lie derivative with respect to a vector  \xi ξ\xi, and  \iota_{\xi}\gamma\equiv\xi\,\lrcorner\,\gamma ιξ​γ≡ξ​⌟​γ\iota_{\xi}\gamma\equiv\xi\,\lrcorner\,\gamma is standard abbreviation for the interior product. It is easy to check that this coupling transforms by a total derivative under  \gamma\mapsto\gamma+\partial\alpha γ↦γ+∂α\gamma\mapsto\gamma+\partial\alpha.




This is the expression for the coupling on  \mathbb{C}^{3} ℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3}. When we transform to twistor space, by the analysis above, we find that the coupling becomes





 \begin{split}&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int z^{m+n}E[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\gamma\\
&+\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int\mathrm{d}z\;z^{m+n+2}F[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\iota_{\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v_{\dot{\alpha}}}}\gamma.\end{split} ∑m,n≥01m!​n!​∫zm+n​E​[m,n]​ℒ∂v1˙m​ℒ∂v2˙n​γ+∑m,n≥01m!​n!∫dzzm+n+2F[m,n]ℒ∂v1˙mℒ∂v2˙nι∂vα˙ℒ∂vα˙γ.\begin{split}&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int z^{m+n}E[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\gamma\\
&+\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}\int\mathrm{d}z\;z^{m+n+2}F[m,n]\;\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{1}}}}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v^{\dot{2}}}}^{n}\iota_{\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{v_{\dot{\alpha}}}}\gamma.\end{split}

(4.28)


Recalling the expression (4.17) for  E,F E,FE,F in terms of  X XX, we see that each appearance of  X^{\dot{\alpha}} Xα˙X^{\dot{\alpha}} introduces a  z\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} z∂vα˙z\partial_{v^{\dot{\alpha}}} into the coupling.




This analysis tells us that the chiral algebra we have constructed is not quite the Koszul dual to the algebra of bulk operators. Recall [17, 18] that the Koszul dual chiral algebra is the universal algebra that couples to the bulk system. Since the coupling between our chiral algebra and the bulk system has zeroes at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty, it is not the most general system we can couple in a gauge invariant way. The most general system will allow the currents  J,E,F J,E,FJ,E,F to have the following poles at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty:





 \begin{split}J[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m+1\text{ at }z=0,\infty\\
E[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m\text{ at }z=0,\infty\\
F[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m+2\text{ at }z=0,\infty\end{split} J⁡[m,n] pole of order ​n+m+1​ at ​z=0,∞E⁡[m,n] pole of order ​n+m​ at ​z=0,∞F⁡[m,n] pole of order ​n+m+2​ at ​z=0,∞\begin{split}J[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m+1\text{ at }z=0,\infty\\
E[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m\text{ at }z=0,\infty\\
F[m,n]&\ \ \text{ pole of order }n+m+2\text{ at }z=0,\infty\end{split}

(4.29)


Since  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} has a first order zero and  \gamma γ\gamma is regular at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty, the couplings (4.25) and (4.28) remain regular for these configurations of poles.




The modes  J_{l}[m,n] Jl​[m,n]J_{l}[m,n],  E_{l}[m,n] El​[m,n]E_{l}[m,n],  F_{l}[m,n] Fl​[m,n]F_{l}[m,n] (where  l ll refers to the spin of the mode) are defined by the expansions2020
              20
              
              
              
            We are using conventions where modes with negative mode numbers  l ll (for large enough  |l| |l||l|) are the annihilation operators, which is the opposite of the usual convention in physics.





 \begin{split}J[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{1+\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\\
E[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\\
F[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{2+\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\,,\end{split} J​[m,n]​(z)=∑lJl​[m,n]z1+m+n2−lE​[m,n]​(z)=∑lJl​[m,n]zm+n2−lF​[m,n]​(z)=∑lJl​[m,n]z2+m+n2−l,\begin{split}J[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{1+\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\\
E[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\\
F[m,n](z)&=\sum_{l}\frac{J_{l}[m,n]}{z^{2+\frac{m+n}{2}-l}}\,,\end{split}

(4.30)


where  l ll runs over  \mathbb{Z}+\frac{m+n}{2} ℤ+m+n2\mathbb{Z}+\frac{m+n}{2}. In terms of these, the vacuum vector at  z=0 z=0z=0 satisfies





 \begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
E_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
F_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\,.\end{split} Jl​[m,n]​|v0⟩=0​ for ​2​l+m+n<0El​[m,n]​|v0⟩=0​ for ​2​l+m+n<0Fl​[m,n]​|v0⟩=0​ for ​2​l+m+n<0.\begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
E_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
F_{l}[m,n]|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\,.\end{split}

(4.31)


These relations define a module for the mode algebra of the chiral algebra. More formally, the relations (4.31) define a left ideal in the mode algebra of the chiral algebra, and quotienting by this left ideal gives the desired module.




What is not obvious, however, is that this module is of the desired size. Let us explain what we mean by this. A (topological) basis for the mode algebra of the chiral algebra is given by ordered products of the generators  J_{l}[m,n] Jl​[m,n]J_{l}[m,n],  E_{l}[m,n] El​[m,n]E_{l}[m,n],  F_{l}[m,n] Fl​[m,n]F_{l}[m,n] in some ordering prescription. We would like a basis of the module to be given, in a similar way, by products of the generators





 \begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\,.\end{split} Jl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0El​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0Fl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0.\begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\,.\end{split}

(4.32)


These products should be taken in some chosen ordering for the currents listed above.




It could, however, happen that the module is too small, or even zero. This can occur if the commutator of two generators satisfying the equality (4.31) is a product of expressions which satisfy the opposite inequalities (4.32), forcing certain composite states to be zero.




In the Appendix D, we will verify that the module is indeed of the correct size.





4.3 Microscopic definition of the boundary conditions




So far, we have defined the boundary conditions for the chiral algebra by prescribing the poles that elements of the BRST cohomology acquire. In this section we will see how to define the boundary condition before we take BRST cohomology, by considering the behaviour of the fundamental fields  I,X I,XI,X and the  \mathrm{b}-\mathrm{c} b−c\mathrm{b}-\mathrm{c} ghosts.




Recall that we require  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n] to have a pole at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty of order  m+n+1 m+n+1m+n+1, whereas  E[m,n] E⁡[m,n]E[m,n] has a pole of order  m+n m+nm+n and  F[m,n] F⁡[m,n]F[m,n] has a pole of order  m+n+2 m+n+2m+n+2. The poles in  E,F E,FE,F are easy to obtain: we simply allow the  X XX fields to have a first order pole at  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty.




The pole for  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n] is more challenging to obtain, because we would need to give  I II a pole of order  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}. Fortunately, as Davide Gaiotto explained to us, it is possible to give  I II a pole of order  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}, by giving a kind of Ramond puncture2121
              21
              
              
              
            This is an example of the notion of a twisted module known from vertex algebra literature [90]. at  z=0 z=0z=0,  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty.




Normally, a Ramond puncture is defined by choosing a square root of the canonical bundle on a punctured surface that does not extend across the puncture. Because we want a Ramond-like puncture only for the  I II fields but not for the  X XX fields, we will do something slightly different.




Our Ramond-like puncture is defined by considering the system on  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times}, and placing a defect on a line stretching between  0 00 and  \infty ∞\infty. Along this defect, the field  I II has a branch cut and changes sign. The other fields are unaffected.




Once we have this branch cut, it makes sense to give  I II a pole of order  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}. We also give  X XX a pole of order  1 11, and the  \mathrm{b} b\mathrm{b}-ghost a pole of order  2 22.




Let us describe this more formally in terms of modes. The field  I II has modes  I_{l} IlI_{l} of integer spin (suppressing  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) and  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) indices), as do  \mathrm{b},\mathrm{c} b,c\mathrm{b},\mathrm{c}. The field  X XX has modes of half-integer spin. The vector  |v_{0}\rangle |v0⟩|v_{0}\rangle is annihilated by the modes:





 \begin{split}I_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<0\\
X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<-\frac{1}{2}\\
\mathrm{b}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<-1\\
\mathrm{c}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<0\end{split} Il​|v0⟩=0​ for ​l<0Xlα˙​|v0⟩=0​ for ​l<−12bl​|v0⟩=0​ for ​l<−1cl​|v0⟩=0​ for ​l<0\begin{split}I_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<0\\
X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<-\frac{1}{2}\\
\mathrm{b}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<-1\\
\mathrm{c}_{l}|v_{0}\rangle&=0\text{ for }l<0\end{split}

(4.33)


For this to make sense, we need to know that the ideal of elements annihilating  |v_{0}\rangle |v0⟩|v_{0}\rangle is closed under the BRST operator. The BRST operator is schematically of the form





 \begin{split}QI_{l}&=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}I_{s}\,,\qquad QX^{\dot{\alpha}}_{l}=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\\
Q\mathrm{c}_{l}&=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}c_{s}\,,\qquad Q\mathrm{b}_{l}=\sum_{r+s=l}\left(:\!I_{r}I_{s}\!:+\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}:\!X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{r}X^{\dot{\beta}}_{s}\!:\right).\end{split} Q​Il=∑r+s=lcr​Is,Q​Xlα˙=∑r+s=lcr​Xsα˙Q​cl=∑r+s=lcrcs,Qbl=∑r+s=l(:IrIs:+ϵα˙​β˙:Xα˙rXβ˙s:).\begin{split}QI_{l}&=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}I_{s}\,,\qquad QX^{\dot{\alpha}}_{l}=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\\
Q\mathrm{c}_{l}&=\sum_{r+s=l}\mathrm{c}_{r}c_{s}\,,\qquad Q\mathrm{b}_{l}=\sum_{r+s=l}\left(:\!I_{r}I_{s}\!:+\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}:\!X^{\dot{\alpha}}_{r}X^{\dot{\beta}}_{s}\!:\right).\end{split}

(4.34)


It is easy to check that if the left hand side of this expression annihilates  |v_{0}\rangle |v0⟩|v_{0}\rangle, then so does the right hand side.
With these boundary conditions, we have the desired poles in the currents  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n],  E[m,n] E⁡[m,n]E[m,n],  F[m,n] F⁡[m,n]F[m,n]. For instance, the lowest spin component of  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n] that survives on the boundary is of the form





 I_{0}(X^{\dot{1}}_{-\frac{1}{2}})^{r}(X^{\dot{2}}_{-\frac{1}{2}})^{s}I_{-1} I0​(X−121˙)r​(X−122˙)s​I−1I_{0}(X^{\dot{1}}_{-\frac{1}{2}})^{r}(X^{\dot{2}}_{-\frac{1}{2}})^{s}I_{-1}

(4.35)


which is of spin  -\frac{1}{2}(r+s) −12​(r+s)-\frac{1}{2}(r+s). This is what we would find from a current of spin  1+\frac{1}{2}(r+s) 1+12​(r+s)1+\frac{1}{2}(r+s) with a pole of order  r+s+1 r+s+1r+s+1.




We verify in the appendix C.1 that, at large  N NN, the BRST cohomology of this microscopic description of the module reproduces the description given earlier.





4.4 Conformal blocks of the chiral algebra with defects




Given a chiral algebra, perhaps with defects, we say a conformal block is a way of defining correlation functions of local operators in a way consistent with all OPEs, and with all boundary behaviour determined by the defects.




In this section we will find that the chiral algebra has an infinite-dimensional space of conformal blocks, which we will identify with the Hilbert space of the 4d system on a small  S^{3} S3S^{3}. We only compute the conformal blocks at infinite  N NN. In 4d, we compute the Hilbert space via an ansatz that matches it with the space of local operators in the free theory. It is important to note that there is no Hamiltonian in our analysis: it is purely kinematic.




Let us first explain why we would expect it to be true. In [33], it was shown that for a theory on flat space coming from a holomorphic theory on twistor space, conformal blocks for the celestial chiral algebra are isomorphic to local operators. This was derived both by a formal QFT argument, and an explicit calculation (see also [91]).




By the state-operator correspondence, the space of local operators in the 4d theory is the Hilbert space on  S^{3} S3S^{3}. We therefore need to show how to generalize the argument of [33] to a 4d geometry which is not flat. As in [33], we will give both a formal argument and an explicit calculation.




Since our chiral algebra is generated by the towers  J,E,F J,E,FJ,E,F, correlation functions are determined by the correlation functions with only  J,E,F J,E,FJ,E,F insertions. We will determine the conformal blocks by analyzing the ambiguity in the correlation functions of these insertions.




As a warm-up, let us first consider correlators of the operator  J=J[0,0] J=J⁡[0,0]J=J[0,0]. This is a usual Kac-Moody current for  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8). If we do not change the boundary conditions, then the correlators





 \left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{n})\right\rangle ⟨J⁡(z1)​…​J​(zn)⟩\left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{n})\right\rangle

(4.36)


are meromorphic functions of  z_{1},\dots,z_{n} z1,…,znz_{1},\dots,z_{n}. They have poles only at  z_{i}=z_{j} zi=zjz_{i}=z_{j}, and the polar part is determined by the OPE. Because  J JJ is of spin  1 11, the correlator has a second-order zero at  z_{i}=\infty zi=∞z_{i}=\infty. This  n nn-point function is completely determined by the  (n-1) (n−1)(n-1)-point function. The only ambiguity in defining the correlation function is present in the definition of the zero-point function  \left\langle 1\right\rangle ⟨1⟩\left\langle 1\right\rangle, which can be an arbitrary constant.




This means that in this case the vector space of conformal blocks is one dimensional.2222
              22
              
              
              
            The reader may notice that the correlation functions are over-determined. This is related to the fact that the Kac-Moody algebra has derived conformal blocks, in the sense introduced by Beilinson-Drinfeld [92].




Let us see what changes when we allow  J(z) J⁡(z)J(z) to have a first order pole at zero and infinity. The pole at infinity is to be taken in addition to the second order zero prescribed by the fact that  J JJ is of spin  1 11, so that in total we have a first order zero at  \infty ∞\infty.




Suppose, in this case, we have defined the  k kk-point function





 \left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{k})\right\rangle ⟨J⁡(z1)​…​J​(zk)⟩\left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{k})\right\rangle

(4.37)


for  k<n k<nk<n, in a way consistent with the OPE. Let us consider the ambiguity in defining the  n nn-point function.




The poles in the correlator  \left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{n})\right\rangle ⟨J⁡(z1)​…​J​(zn)⟩\left\langle J(z_{1})\dots J(z_{n})\right\rangle at  z_{i}=z_{j} zi=zjz_{i}=z_{j} are determined by the OPEs and the  k kk-point correlators for  k<n k<nk<n. This means that the  n nn-point function is determined up to the addition of a meromorphic function  \mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}) ℱ⁡(z1,…,zn)\mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}) which is regular at  z_{i}=z_{j} zi=zjz_{i}=z_{j}. The function  \mathcal{F} ℱ\mathcal{F} can have a first order pole at  z_{i}=0 zi=0z_{i}=0 and must have a first order zero at  z_{i}=\infty zi=∞z_{i}=\infty.
This means that  \mathcal{F} ℱ\mathcal{F} is proportional to





 \mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\propto\frac{1}{z_{1}\dots z_{n}}. ℱ⁡(z1,…,zn)∝1z1​…​zn.\mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\propto\frac{1}{z_{1}\dots z_{n}}.

(4.38)





In this case, we see that for each  n nn, we are free to add an arbitrary multiple of  z_{1}^{-1}\cdots z_{n}^{-1} z1−1⋯zn−1z_{1}^{-1}\cdots z_{n}^{-1} to the correlation function. This expression can depend also on the Lie algebra indices of the current, but in a symmetric way. We find that the full space of conformal blocks is the symmetric algebra





 \operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}\mathfrak{g}=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{n}\mathfrak{g} Sym∗𝔤=⊕n≥0Symn𝔤\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}\mathfrak{g}=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{n}\mathfrak{g}

(4.39)





Now let us turn to the conformal blocks of our full chiral algebra generated by  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s],  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s]. It is natural to expect, following the analysis above, that the ambiguity in defining the  n nn-point correlation function – which one can call the  n nn-point conformal block – is the  n nnth symmetric power of the  1 11-point conformal block. If so, the computation of the conformal blocks amounts to computing the  1 11-point conformal block, which is quite easy. Now let us check this property:



Proposition 4.1.



The full space of conformal blocks is isomorphic to the symmetric algebra on the conformal blocks for the one-point function.




Proof.



The proof is similar to the proof in the case of the Kac-Moody algebra, except that we cannot simply work by induction on the number of insertions. This is because in the larger algebra, the OPE of two operators  E,F,J E,F,JE,F,J will in general be expressed in terms of normally ordered products of a number of such operators. The  (n-1) (n−1)(n-1)-point function where one of the insertions is a normally ordered product can be expressed in terms of a  k kk-point function without normally ordered products, where  k\geq n k≥nk\geq n. Therefore the inductive argument will not work.




Instead, we need to introduce a measure of complexity of the operators which decreases when we take the normally ordered product. We say the width of a single-trace operator is the number of fundamental fields  I,X I,XI,X required to build it. Thus,  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s],  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s] have width  r+s+2 r+s+2r+s+2 and  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s] has width  r+s r+sr+s. The OPE between single-trace operators of width  l_{1},l_{2} l1,l2l_{1},l_{2} is a sum of expressions of width  \leq l_{1}+l_{2}-2 ≤l1+l2−2\leq l_{1}+l_{2}-2.




Suppose we are studying the correlation functions of  n nn single-trace operators  E,F,J E,F,JE,F,J of total width  l ll. Suppose by induction we have already defined a consistent set of correlation functions with  k kk insertions of single-trace operators of total width  m mm where  k+m<n+l k+m<n+lk+m<n+l. Then, the  n nn-point function of total width  l ll is defined up to the addition of a function  \mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}) ℱ⁡(z1,…,zn)\mathcal{F}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}) which is regular at  z_{i}=z_{j} zi=zjz_{i}=z_{j}. Thus,  \mathcal{F} ℱ\mathcal{F} is a sum of products of the monomials  z_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots z_{n}^{k_{n}} z1k1​…​znknz_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots z_{n}^{k_{n}} where the allowed values of  k_{1},\dots,k_{n} k1,…,knk_{1},\dots,k_{n} are determined by the spins of the fields and the boundary conditions – that is, by the conformal blocks of the  1 11-point functions.
∎





Finally, the one-point conformal blocks are easy to determine. We will write the one-point conformal blocks as a representation of the symmetry group of the system, which is  \mathrm{SO}(8)\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times} SO⁡(8)×SL2​(ℂ)×ℂ×\mathrm{SO}(8)\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}. Here  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times} SL2​(ℂ)×ℂ×\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times} is the complexification of the isometry group of Burns space, and this group acts on twistor space in a way fixing the boundary divisors. In terms of the currents  E,F,J E,F,JE,F,J,  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} is the rotation of the  z zz-plane. The currents  E[m,n] E⁡[m,n]E[m,n] for  m+n m+nm+n fixed transform in the irreducible spin  (m+n)/2 (m+n)/2(m+n)/2 representation of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and similarly for  F[m,n] F⁡[m,n]F[m,n],  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n]. Finally,  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8) only acts on  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n], which transforms in the adjoint representation.




Let  J[r] J⁡[r]J[r],  E[r] E⁡[r]E[r],  F[r] F⁡[r]F[r] denote the collection of currents  J[m,n],E[m,n],F[m,n] J⁡[m,n],E⁡[m,n],F⁡[m,n]J[m,n],E[m,n],F[m,n] with  m+n=r m+n=rm+n=r. Let  S_{+} S+S_{+} denote the fundamental representation of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). Let  S_{-} S−S_{-} denote the two-dimensional representation of  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} with weights  (\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}) (12,−12)(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}). (The terminology is because these representations come from the spin representations of  \operatorname{Spin}(4) Spin⁡(4)\operatorname{Spin}(4)).




Then, the one-point function of  J[r] J⁡[r]J[r],  E[r] E⁡[r]E[r],  F[r] F⁡[r]F[r] live in the following representations:





 \begin{split}J[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}\\
E[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\\
F[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\end{split} J⁡[r]∈Symr⁡S+⊗Symr⁡S−⊗𝔰​𝔬8E⁡[r]∈Symr⁡S+⊗Symr⁡S−F⁡[r]∈Symr⁡S+⊗Symr⁡S−\begin{split}J[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}\\
E[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\\
F[r]&\in\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{r}S_{-}\end{split}

(4.40)


In order to compare with what we find for the field theory on Burns space, it will be useful to write this in terms of sections of vector bundles on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. We find the one-point conformal blocks of the  E,F,J E,F,JE,F,J towers are:





 \begin{split}J&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathfrak{so}_{8}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\\
E&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\\
F&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1))).\end{split} J∈H0​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝔰​𝔬8⊗Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(1)))E∈H0​(ℂ​ℙ1,Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(1)))F∈H0​(ℂ​ℙ1,Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(1))).\begin{split}J&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathfrak{so}_{8}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\\
E&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\\
F&\in H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1))).\end{split}

(4.41)






4.5 Comparison of conformal blocks with the Hilbert space in four dimensions




We want to show that the space of conformal blocks of the chiral algebra is isomorphic to the Hilbert space of the four-dimensional dual theory on a small  3 33-sphere surrounding the “core” of Burns space. In the coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} where the Burns space Kähler potential is





 \left\|u\right\|^{2}+N\log\left\|u\right\|^{2} ‖u‖2+N​log⁡‖u‖2\left\|u\right\|^{2}+N\log\left\|u\right\|^{2}

(4.42)


the relevant  S^{3} S3S^{3} is just that where  \left\|u\right\|^{2}=\epsilon ‖u‖2=ϵ\left\|u\right\|^{2}=\epsilon,  \epsilon ϵ\epsilon small. On flat space, the Hilbert space for this  S^{3} S3S^{3} is the space of local operators. On Burns space, where the apparent singularity in the metric has been resolved by blowing up the origin, this Hilbert space represents the space of operators wrapping the  S^{2} S2S^{2} that replaces the origin.




We note that this Hilbert space does not have a Hamiltonian, because a neighbourhood of this  S^{3} S3S^{3} does not have a time-translation symmetry.




We will show that the Hilbert space and the space of conformal blocks are isomorphic as representations of the  \mathrm{SO}(8)\times\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) SO⁡(8)×SU⁡(2)×U⁡(1)\mathrm{SO}(8)\times\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) symmetry algebra present on both systems. As we explained earlier, the interpretation of this is the following. To define the correlation functions of the chiral algebra, we need to choose a conformal block. To define the scattering amplitudes on Burns geometry, we are free to modify the theory by placing an operator in the center of Burns space. Our analysis shows that this additional data is the same in each case.




The Hilbert space is built in the usual way. First, we build the phase space of the model, and then we apply geometric quantization. Since there is no Hamiltonian, the phase space is simply a symplectic manifold defined from the Cauchy data. We will model the Cauchy data as simply that of a collection of free scalars in  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8). One might object that by doing this we will be missing more global features, such as Skyrmions, which are states with topological charge in  \pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8)) π3​(SO​(8))\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8)). However, we will show shortly that Skyrmions do not arise in our model.




The phase space is also insensitive to the metric. Therefore there is no change if we work with the flat metric, rather than the Burns metric. Since the free theory is conformally invariant, this Hilbert space can then be computed by the state-operator correspondence: it is the same as the space of local operators in the free theory.




Finally, the space of local operators in the free theory can be computed using twistor space and matched with what we found above from studying conformal blocks. (This is a version of the argument given in [33] relating conformal blocks to local operators). The Hilbert space is the symmetric algebra on the single-particle Hilbert space, which is identified with the space of local operators which are linear functionals in the fields. To prove the correspondence with conformal blocks, we therefore need to match the space of those local operators which are linear in the fields, with the one-point conformal blocks.




The space of local operators which are linear on the fields is the dual vector space to the vector space of solutions to the equations of motion of the free theory. For a free scalar field valued in  \mathfrak{so}_{8} 𝔰​𝔬8\mathfrak{so}_{8}, the space of solutions to the equations of motion is (by the Penrose transform)





 H^{1}(\mathbb{PT},\mathscr{O}(-2))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}. H1​(ℙ​𝕋,𝒪⁡(−2))⊗𝔰​𝔬8.H^{1}(\mathbb{PT},\mathscr{O}(-2))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}.

(4.43)


By pushing forward the structure sheaf of  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT} to  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}, this is the same as





 H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-2)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}. H1​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝒪⁡(−2)⊗Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(−1)))⊗𝔰​𝔬8.H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-2)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}.

(4.44)


Serre duality tells us that the dual vector space of this is





 H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-2)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}=H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8} H1​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝒪⁡(−2)⊗Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(−1)))∨⊗𝔰​𝔬8=H0​(ℂ​ℙ1,Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(1)))⊗𝔰​𝔬8H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-2)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}=H^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)))\otimes\mathfrak{so}_{8}

(4.45)


which is exactly the one-point conformal blocks of the  J JJ-tower of currents.




A similar analysis applies to the closed string fields. The space of field configurations  \phi ϕ\phi satisfying the fourth-order equation  \triangle\triangle\phi=0 △​△​ϕ=0\triangle\triangle\phi=0 is the same as the space of pairs of solutions to the ordinary Laplace equation  \triangle\phi_{i}=0 △​ϕi=0\triangle\phi_{i}=0,  i=1,2 i=1,2i=1,2. If we take  \phi ϕ\phi up to the addition of a constant, then we take  \phi_{1} ϕ1\phi_{1} up to the addition of a constant. This means that the space of linear local operators for the closed string fields is





 H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-1)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{>0}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))\oplus H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-1)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1))) H1​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝒪⁡(−1)⊗Sym>0⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(−1)))⊕H1​(ℂ​ℙ1,𝒪⁡(−1)⊗Sym∗⁡(S+⊗𝒪⁡(−1)))H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-1)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{>0}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))\oplus H^{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathscr{O}(-1)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}(S_{+}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)))

(4.46)


By Serre duality, this matches exactly with the contribution of the  E EE and  F FF towers to the one-point conformal blocks. The fact that the first factor above contains only the positive symmetric powers matches the fact that there is no  E[0,0] E⁡[0,0]E[0,0] current (recall from (4.17) that  E[0,0]=1 E⁡[0,0]=1E[0,0]=1).



Absence of Skyrmions.




Finally, we should mention why we do not need to consider topologically nontrivial states, i.e. Skyrmions, in the open string sector. It turns out that they can not arise in WZW4, as we will see from a twistor space analysis.




Consider the twistor space of  \mathbb{R}^{4}-0 ℝ4−0\mathbb{R}^{4}-0. This is the complement of the zero section in  \mathbb{PT} ℙ​𝕋\mathbb{PT}, and so is a fibration over  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} with fibers  \mathbb{C}^{2}-0 ℂ2−0\mathbb{C}^{2}-0. Twistor space has two patches, lying over the loci where  z\neq 0 z≠0z\neq 0 and  z\neq\infty z≠∞z\neq\infty in  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. Over these patches twistor space is  \mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0) ℂ×(ℂ2−0)\mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0). A field configuration on these patches is a holomorphic bundle, and every such bundle is topologically trivial.




A topologically nontrivial field configuration on spacetime would come equipped with topologically nontrivial gluing data on the overlap  \mathbb{C}^{\times}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0) ℂ××(ℂ2−0)\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0) of the two patches. The gluing data is a holomorphic map





 \mathbb{C}^{\times}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0)\to\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C}). ℂ××(ℂ2−0)→SO⁡(8,ℂ).\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}-0)\to\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C}).



Associated to this is a class in  \pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C})) π3​(SO​(8,ℂ))\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C})), obtained by restricting this gluing map to  S^{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}-0 S3⊂ℂ2−0S^{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}-0. This class is the topological charge of the Skyrmion in spacetime. Our goal is to show that this class is zero.




 \mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C}) SO⁡(8,ℂ)\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C}) is an affine algebraic variety, and hence a closed subvariety of some  \mathbb{C}^{n} ℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}. Hartog’s theorem tells us that any holomorphic map from  \mathbb{C}^{2}-0 ℂ2−0\mathbb{C}^{2}-0 to  \mathbb{C}^{n} ℂn\mathbb{C}^{n} extends to a map from all of  \mathbb{C}^{2} ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. Therefore, the gluing data gives a topologically trivial class in  \pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C})) π3​(SO​(8,ℂ))\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(8,\mathbb{C})).







5 Where does celestial CFT live?




Up till now, our analysis has been fairly top-down and string theoretic. But for a genuine holographic interpretation, we must also connect to the plethora of bottom-up approaches developed in the literature [9]. To do this, we need to construct a physical picture of the holographic boundary where the celestial dual lives. In this section, we make precise the identification of this boundary as a boundary of the twistor space of Burns space.




This is accomplished by exhibiting the twistor space of Burns space as AdS {}_{3}\times S^{3} 3×S3{}_{3}\times S^{3}. The boundary of this twistorial AdS3 bulk will act as our Euclidean proxy for the celestial sphere. This is where the boundary data for solutions of linearized free field equations lives. In our case, this spherical boundary also gets appended with two extra copies of the non-negative reals  [0,\infty] [0,∞][0,\infty] living at its north and south poles. At the level of celestial CFT, these play the role of point defects living at the poles. Thereafter, the rough idea will be to foliate AdS3 by twistor lines.  S^{2} S2S^{2} compactification along the twistor lines yields Burns space. This converts familiar AdS {}_{3}\times S^{3} 3×S3{}_{3}\times S^{3} holography into “exotic” celestial holography!




5.1 Looking for AdS3




As we saw in section 3.2, the twistor space of Burns space is obtained from the complete flag variety  \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}(1,2,3) 𝔽=𝔽⁡(1,2,3)\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}(1,2,3) of points in lines in  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} by subtracting a single copy of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. This led us to conclude that Burns space itself is diffeomorphic to  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} minus a point. It is the  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} viewpoint that most naturally helps us understand the boundary structure of our holographic duality. The removed point corresponds precisely to spatial infinity  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty on Burns space (in the sense of Euclidean signature one-point compactifications).




Recall that the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} was the set of points  ([V^{k}],[W_{k}])\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2} ([Vk],[Wk])⊂ℂ​ℙ2×ℂ​ℙ2([V^{k}],[W_{k}])\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2} satisfying  V\cdot W=0 V⋅W=0V\cdot W=0. This was the twistor space of  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}, which we coordinatized by homogeneous coordinates  [U^{k}] [Uk][U^{k}]. Complex conjugation was denoted  U^{k}\mapsto\bar{U}_{k}\equiv\overline{U^{k}} Uk↦U¯k≡Uk¯U^{k}\mapsto\bar{U}_{k}\equiv\overline{U^{k}}. The twistor line corresponding to  [U^{k}] [Uk][U^{k}] was cut out by the equations  V\cdot W=U\cdot W=V\cdot\bar{U}=0 V⋅W=U⋅W=V⋅U¯=0V\cdot W=U\cdot W=V\cdot\bar{U}=0. In what follows, we will use the notation  V^{k}=(V^{\dot{\alpha}},V^{3}) Vk=(Vα˙,V3)V^{k}=(V^{\dot{\alpha}},V^{3}),  W_{k}=(W_{\dot{\alpha}},W_{3}) Wk=(Wα˙,W3)W_{k}=(W_{\dot{\alpha}},W_{3}),  U^{k}=(U^{\dot{\alpha}},U^{3}) Uk=(Uα˙,U3)U^{k}=(U^{\dot{\alpha}},U^{3}) etc. for convenience.




Introduce affine coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}}=U^{\dot{\alpha}}/U^{3} uα˙=Uα˙/U3u^{\dot{\alpha}}=U^{\dot{\alpha}}/U^{3} on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}. To obtain Burns space in these coordinates, one performs the diffeomorphism  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2}. Being an inversion, this maps the origin  u^{\dot{\alpha}}=0 uα˙=0u^{\dot{\alpha}}=0 of this affine patch to the spatial infinity of Burns space. In homogeneous coordinates, this is what we called the point at infinity  [U^{k}]=[0,0,1] [Uk]=[0,0,1][U^{k}]=[0,0,1]. When constructing Burns space, we excised this point from  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}. To study its conformal boundary, it will prove more useful to compactify Burns space into  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} by adding back this point. Doing this, we can instead treat  [0,0,1] [0,0,1][0,0,1] as the spacetime boundary. In the flag variety, the twistor line associated to  [0,0,1] [0,0,1][0,0,1] is the copy of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} given by2323
              23
              
              
              
            Not to be confused with the notation for the chiral algebra field  I_{ri} Ir​iI_{ri}.





 I=\{V^{3}=W_{3}=0\}\,. I={V3=W3=0}.I=\{V^{3}=W_{3}=0\}\,.

(5.1)


We call this the line at infinity.




The brane backreaction generates a copy of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) cut out by  [v\,w]=N [v​w]=N[v\,w]=N. The boundary of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) would be the set of points  (v,w)\in\mathbb{C}^{4} (v,w)∈ℂ4(v,w)\in\mathbb{C}^{4} satisfying  [v\,w]=0 [v​w]=0[v\,w]=0. We can append these to  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) by an appropriate compactification. In our setup,  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is compactified by embedding it in the flag variety via the map  V^{\dot{\alpha}}=-v^{\dot{\alpha}}V^{3}/\sqrt{N} Vα˙=−vα˙V3/NV^{\dot{\alpha}}=-v^{\dot{\alpha}}V^{3}/\sqrt{N},  W_{\dot{\alpha}}=w_{\dot{\alpha}}W_{3}/\sqrt{N} Wα˙=wα˙​W3/NW_{\dot{\alpha}}=w_{\dot{\alpha}}W_{3}/\sqrt{N} given in (3.23). The image of this map is the open set  V^{3}W_{3}\neq 0 V3​W3≠0V^{3}W_{3}\neq 0. The complement of this image in  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} is the boundary divisor  V^{3}W_{3}=0 V3​W3=0V^{3}W_{3}=0. This is a union of the following sets:





 D_{0}=\{W_{3}=0\}\,,\qquad D_{\infty}=\{V^{3}=0\}\,. D0={W3=0},D∞={V3=0}.D_{0}=\{W_{3}=0\}\,,\qquad D_{\infty}=\{V^{3}=0\}\,.

(5.2)


Each of these occurs with multiplicity one.  D_{0} D0D_{0} and  D_{\infty} D∞D_{\infty} are each a copy of the blow-up of  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} at a point. Their intersection equals the line at infinity:  D_{0}\cap D_{\infty}=I D0∩D∞=ID_{0}\cap D_{\infty}=I.




The twistor space of Burns space is given by  Z_{N}=\mathbb{F}-I=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\cup(D_{0}\cup D_{\infty}-I) ZN=𝔽−I=SL2​(ℂ)∪(D0∪D∞−I)Z_{N}=\mathbb{F}-I=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\cup(D_{0}\cup D_{\infty}-I). From the 4d perspective, the divisors  D_{0}-I D0−ID_{0}-I,  D_{\infty}-I D∞−ID_{\infty}-I of  Z_{N} ZNZ_{N} are not part of asymptotic infinity. Each of them maps isomorphically onto  \widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} ℂ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}, the complex manifold underlying Burns space.




In [16], the authors compactified the deformed conifold  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) to a quadric in  \mathbb{CP}^{4} ℂ​ℙ4\mathbb{CP}^{4}. The resulting geometry had an  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)×SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) symmetry. Quotienting out an  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) subgroup of one of the copies of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) led to Euclidean AdS {}_{3}\simeq\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{SU}(2) ≃3SL2(ℂ)/SU(2){}_{3}\simeq\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{SU}(2). Their holographic dual lived on the boundary of this AdS3.




In our current setting, we have instead compactified the  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) by embedding it in the flag variety. The  \mathrm{U}(1)_{\text{left}}\times\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\text{right}} U​(1)left×SU​(2)right\mathrm{U}(1)_{\text{left}}\times\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\text{right}} isometries of Burns space lift to an action of the complexification  \mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) ℂ××SL2​(ℂ)\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) on the flag variety:





 ([V],[W])\mapsto\bigl([L^{\dot{\alpha}}{}_{\dot{\beta}}V^{\dot{\beta}},s^{-1}V^{3}]\,,\;[L_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}W_{\dot{\beta}},sW_{3}]\bigr)\,,\quad L\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\;s\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\,. ([V],[W])↦([Lα˙​Vβ˙β˙,s−1​V3],[Lα˙​Wβ˙β˙,s​W3]),L∈SL2​(ℂ),s∈ℂ×.([V],[W])\mapsto\bigl([L^{\dot{\alpha}}{}_{\dot{\beta}}V^{\dot{\beta}},s^{-1}V^{3}]\,,\;[L_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}W_{\dot{\beta}},sW_{3}]\bigr)\,,\quad L\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\;s\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\,.

(5.3)


The  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} is our defect conformal group. We want to quotient  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} by  \mathrm{SU}(2)\subset\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SU​(2)⊂SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SU}(2)\subset\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) and show that the resulting geometry is again AdS3. We would also like to visualize the projection of the twistor lines onto this AdS3.




On the  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) patch  V^{3}W_{3}\neq 0 V3​W3≠0V^{3}W_{3}\neq 0, the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) action is free and has  S^{3} S3S^{3} orbits. Quotienting out  S^{3} S3S^{3} gives AdS3 (without its boundary) as the orbit space. Poincaré coordinates on this AdS3 can be constructed out of the natural  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) invariants in the game. Using the conventions of [16], we introduce the Poincaré coordinates  x\in\mathbb{CP}^{1} x∈ℂ​ℙ1x\in\mathbb{CP}^{1},  \varrho>0 ϱ>0\varrho>0,





 \begin{split}x&=\frac{[\hat{w}\,v]}{\|w\|^{2}}=-\frac{W_{3}\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}V_{\dot{\alpha}}}{V^{3}\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,,\\
\varrho&=\frac{1}{\|w\|^{2}}=\frac{|W_{3}|^{2}}{N\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,.\end{split} x=[w^​v]‖w‖2=−W3​W^α˙​Vα˙V3​W^α˙​Wα˙,ϱ=1‖w‖2=|W3|2N​W^α˙​Wα˙.\begin{split}x&=\frac{[\hat{w}\,v]}{\|w\|^{2}}=-\frac{W_{3}\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}V_{\dot{\alpha}}}{V^{3}\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,,\\
\varrho&=\frac{1}{\|w\|^{2}}=\frac{|W_{3}|^{2}}{N\hat{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,.\end{split}

(5.4)


The boundary of AdS3 would usually be the copy of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} obtained by taking a union of the limits  \varrho\to 0 ϱ→0\varrho\to 0 and  \varrho\to\infty ϱ→∞\varrho\to\infty.




The boundary divisors  D_{0} D0D_{0} and  D_{\infty} D∞D_{\infty} project down to copies of  [0,\infty] [0,∞][0,\infty] in  \mathbb{F}/\mathrm{SU}(2) 𝔽/SU⁡(2)\mathbb{F}/\mathrm{SU}(2), so they cannot cover the  S^{2} S2S^{2} boundary of AdS3. We would have naively expected the line at infinity  I II to then map to the boundary of AdS3. A similar projection does give the correct boundary of AdS3 in the case of the  \mathbb{CP}^{4} ℂ​ℙ4\mathbb{CP}^{4} compactification of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) [16]. But unfortunately, in our flag variety compactification,  I II is the orbit of the point  ([1,0,0],[0,1,0])\in\mathbb{F} ([1,0,0],[0,1,0])∈𝔽([1,0,0],[0,1,0])\in\mathbb{F} under the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) action. So the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient collapses it to a point. Hence, the line at infinity cannot by itself be the boundary that we are looking for. This is consistent with the fact that  I II is the fixed point set of the left-handed  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} action. It is only acted on by the right-handed  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) symmetries which constitute the flavor symmetry of our dual chiral algebra, whereas we expect the  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} to be the nontrivial defect conformal symmetry of our chiral algebra sphere.




This analysis shows that, unlike the  \mathbb{CP}^{4} ℂ​ℙ4\mathbb{CP}^{4} compactification, the flag variety compactification of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) simply does not contain the boundary divisor relevant to a conventional notion of holography. Nonetheless, it turns out that this situation can be easily ameliorated. The correct boundary is instead found by blowing up  I II.





5.2 The boundary of twistor space




We begin by blowing up the line at infinity  I\subset\mathbb{F} I⊂𝔽I\subset\mathbb{F} to obtain the variety2424
              24
              
              
              
            This blowup is not a twistor space because it is compact and Kähler, and according to a theorem of Hitchin [51], the only compact Kähler twistor spaces are  \mathbb{CP}^{3} ℂ​ℙ3\mathbb{CP}^{3} and the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}. We thank Claude LeBrun for the warning! Nonetheless, it is an important building block in the Donaldson-Friedman construction of twistor spaces of connected sums of  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} [93].





 \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}=\text{Bl}_{I}\mathbb{F}\,. 𝔽~=BlI​𝔽.\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}=\text{Bl}_{I}\mathbb{F}\,.

(5.5)


Away from  V^{3}=W_{3}=0 V3=W3=0V^{3}=W_{3}=0, this is biholomorphic to  \mathbb{F}-I 𝔽−I\mathbb{F}-I. But the line at infinity has now been replaced by the exceptional divisor





 E=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2}\,. E=ℂ​ℙ1×ℂ​ℙ1=I×𝒞​𝒮2.E=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2}\,.

(5.6)


The first  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} is a copy of the line at infinity. The second  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} has been suggestively named  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2}, as it will turn out to be the correct boundary on which our holographic dual lives. In this sense, we interpret it as our Euclidean analogue of the “celestial sphere”.  E EE consists of a union of projective lines  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z ℂ​ℙ1×z\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z, one for each point  z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2} z∈𝒞​𝒮2z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2}.2525
              25
              
              
              
            In the terminology familiar from work on scattering amplitudes,  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} is the sphere of undotted spinor-helicity variables  \lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z) λα=(1,z)\lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z), while the deprojectivization of  I II is “Fourier conjugate” to the 2-plane of dotted spinor-helicity variables  \tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} λ~α˙\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} (one expects this to be a precise correspondence in split signature).




 \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} is a compactification of the twistor space of the Burns geometry that will encode the asymptotic structure at infinity.
The complement of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) in  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} now consists of three divisors. Two of them are the preimages  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0},  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty} of the divisors  D_{0},D_{\infty} D0,D∞D_{0},D_{\infty} by the blow-up morphism  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{F} 𝔽~→𝔽\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{F}, but now they no longer intersect. The third divisor is  E EE, which intersects  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0} along  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times 0 ℂ​ℙ1×0\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times 0 and  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty} along  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\infty ℂ​ℙ1×∞\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\infty.




One may define the blow-up in coordinates by working in affine patches. Because  V^{\dot{1}}W_{\dot{1}}+V^{\dot{2}}W_{\dot{2}}=0 V1˙​W1˙+V2˙​W2˙=0V^{\dot{1}}W_{\dot{1}}+V^{\dot{2}}W_{\dot{2}}=0 along  V^{3}=W_{3}=0 V3=W3=0V^{3}=W_{3}=0, the line  I II is contained within the union of the two patches  \{V^{\dot{2}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{1}}\neq 0\} {V2˙≠0,W1˙≠0}\{V^{\dot{2}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{1}}\neq 0\} and  \{V^{\dot{1}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{2}}\neq 0\} {V1˙≠0,W2˙≠0}\{V^{\dot{1}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{2}}\neq 0\} of  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}. Let us work in the first of these. In this patch, the blow-up is described by the set of points  ([V],[W],z)\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1} ([V],[W],z)∈ℂ​ℙ2×ℂ​ℙ2×ℂ​ℙ1([V],[W],z)\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1} satisfying





 V\cdot W=0\,,\qquad\frac{W_{3}}{W_{\dot{1}}}=z\,\frac{V^{3}}{V^{\dot{2}}}\,. V⋅W=0,W3W1˙=z​V3V2˙.V\cdot W=0\,,\qquad\frac{W_{3}}{W_{\dot{1}}}=z\,\frac{V^{3}}{V^{\dot{2}}}\,.

(5.7)


The  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} action  (V^{3},W_{3})\mapsto(s^{-1}V^{3},sW_{3}) (V3,W3)↦(s−1​V3,s​W3)(V^{3},W_{3})\mapsto(s^{-1}V^{3},sW_{3}) extends to  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} as the  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times} defect conformal symmetry  z\mapsto s^{2}z z↦s2​zz\mapsto s^{2}z.




The line at infinity  I II is acted on by the right-handed  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) symmetry as usual. Since the right-handed  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) action collapses  I II to a point, the exceptional divisor  E EE projects down to  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} under the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient. In the patch under consideration,  V^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}=0 Vα˙​Wα˙=0V^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\dot{\alpha}}=0 is solved to find  V^{\dot{\alpha}}=-V^{\dot{2}}W^{\dot{\alpha}}/W_{\dot{1}} Vα˙=−V2˙Wα˙/W1˙V^{\dot{\alpha}}=-V^{\dot{2}}W^{\dot{\alpha}}/W_{\dot{1}}. Using this alongside  V^{3}=W_{3}=0 V3=W3=0V^{3}=W_{3}=0, the Poincaré coordinates (5.4) reduce to





 x=z\,,\qquad\varrho=0 x=z,ϱ=0x=z\,,\qquad\varrho=0

(5.8)


on the image of  E EE in the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient. A similar analysis may be performed in the other patch  \{V^{\dot{1}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{2}}\neq 0\} {V1˙≠0,W2˙≠0}\{V^{\dot{1}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{2}}\neq 0\} by setting  W_{3}/W_{\dot{2}}=-\tilde{z}\,V^{3}/V^{\dot{1}} W3/W2˙=−z~V3/V1˙W_{3}/W_{\dot{2}}=-\tilde{z}\,V^{3}/V^{\dot{1}} for some  \tilde{z}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1} z~∈ℂ​ℙ1\tilde{z}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}, where the sign is fixed by demanding that  \tilde{z}=z z~=z\tilde{z}=z along  I II on the overlap of the two patches.




This confirms that the boundary of AdS3 coincides with the celestial sphere  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} created by the blow-up. So  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} is the  z zz-plane where the chiral algebra lives. In this way, twisted holography makes precise the bottom-up intuition that celestial conformal field theory lives on a 2-sphere at infinity [94]. In our Euclidean setting, although we cannot access the standard notion of a spacetime celestial sphere, we have just shown that our holographic plane is nevertheless a 2-sphere that acts as the boundary of the twistor space of Burns space.




Using these facts, we may recast Burns holography as an example of AdS3 holography. To see this explicitly, we need to understand how compactification of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} SL2​(ℂ)≃AdS3×S3\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} along the twistor lines affects the AdS3 factor.




Let us rewrite the twistor lines corresponding to the  \mathbb{C}^{2}-0 ℂ2−0\mathbb{C}^{2}-0 patch of Burns space in compact notation,





 v^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+\sigma\vartheta\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad w^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}+\sigma^{-1}\vartheta u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\vartheta\vcentcolon=\sqrt{1+\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}}\,. vα˙​(σ)=uα˙+σ​ϑ​u^α˙,wα˙​(σ)=u^α˙+σ−1​ϑ​uα˙,ϑ:=1+N‖u‖2.v^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=u^{\dot{\alpha}}+\sigma\vartheta\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad w^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}+\sigma^{-1}\vartheta u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\vartheta\vcentcolon=\sqrt{1+\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}}\,.

(5.9)


The quantity  \vartheta ϑ\vartheta is the fourth root of the metric determinant of the Burns metric. Under a quotient by the right-handed rotations  (v,w)\mapsto(Lv,Lw) (v,w)↦(L​v,L​w)(v,w)\mapsto(Lv,Lw),  L\in\mathrm{SU}(2) L∈SU⁡(2)L\in\mathrm{SU}(2), a curve associated to  u uu is identified with the curve associated to  Lu L​uLu. So in the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient, the curves do not drop in dimension, but all the curves along any  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) orbit get identified with each other. Every curve in the quotient corresponds to an  S^{3} S3S^{3} worth of curves in  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).




We can compute their images in the orbit space AdS {}_{3}=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{SU}(2) =3SL2(ℂ)/SU(2){}_{3}=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{SU}(2). In the Poincaré coordinates introduced in (5.4), the curves project down to





 x(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma\vartheta(1+|\sigma|^{2})}{\vartheta^{2}+|\sigma|^{2}}\,,\qquad\varrho(\sigma)=\frac{|\sigma|^{2}}{\|u\|^{2}(\vartheta^{2}+|\sigma|^{2})}\,. x⁡(σ)=σ​ϑ​(1+|σ|2)ϑ2+|σ|2,ϱ⁡(σ)=|σ|2‖u‖2​(ϑ2+|σ|2).x(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma\vartheta(1+|\sigma|^{2})}{\vartheta^{2}+|\sigma|^{2}}\,,\qquad\varrho(\sigma)=\frac{|\sigma|^{2}}{\|u\|^{2}(\vartheta^{2}+|\sigma|^{2})}\,.

(5.10)


Plugging in  \sigma=0 σ=0\sigma=0, we observe that each of these curves starts at  x=0 x=0x=0,  \varrho=0 ϱ=0\varrho=0. Setting  \sigma=\infty σ=∞\sigma=\infty shows that each curve also ends at  x=\infty x=∞x=\infty, with the curve corresponding to  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} asymptoting to  \varrho=\|u\|^{-2} ϱ=‖u‖−2\varrho=\|u\|^{-2}.




These curves are best visualized by working in the Poincaré disk coordinates





 (y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\left(\frac{x+\bar{x}}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}},\frac{\mathrm{i}(\bar{x}-x)}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}},\frac{|x|^{2}+N^{2}\varrho^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}}\right)\,. (y1,y2,y3)=(x+x¯|x|2+(N​ϱ+1)2,i⁡(x¯−x)|x|2+(N​ϱ+1)2,|x|2+N2​ϱ2−1|x|2+(N​ϱ+1)2).(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\left(\frac{x+\bar{x}}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}},\frac{\mathrm{i}(\bar{x}-x)}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}},\frac{|x|^{2}+N^{2}\varrho^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}+(N\varrho+1)^{2}}\right)\,.

(5.11)


For positive  N NN, this change of coordinates maps AdS3 to the unit ball  y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{3}^{2}<1 y12+y22+y32<1y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{3}^{2}<1. The equations (5.10) are now recognized as parametrizing the spheroids





 \vartheta^{2}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})+y_{3}^{2}=1 ϑ2​(y12+y22)+y32=1\vartheta^{2}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})+y_{3}^{2}=1

(5.12)


with  1/\vartheta 1/ϑ1/\vartheta acting as the half-length of two of the principal axes.




Similarly, setting  u^{\dot{\alpha}}=t\,\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙=t​ζα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}}=t\,\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}}, the twistor lines associated to points  [\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{1} [ζα˙]∈ℂ​ℙ1[\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{1} on the exceptional divisor  t=0 t=0t=0 of Burns space take the form





 v^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\frac{\sqrt{N}\hat{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|\zeta\|}\,\sigma\,,\qquad w^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\frac{\sqrt{N}\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|\zeta\|}\,\sigma^{-1}\,. vα˙​(σ)=N​ζ^α˙‖ζ‖​σ,wα˙​(σ)=N​ζα˙‖ζ‖​σ−1.v^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\frac{\sqrt{N}\hat{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|\zeta\|}\,\sigma\,,\qquad w^{\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)=\frac{\sqrt{N}\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|\zeta\|}\,\sigma^{-1}\,.

(5.13)


All points on a 2-sphere can be mapped to each other by its rotational symmetry, so this exceptional divisor lies in a single  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) orbit. Consequently, all of these twistor lines get identified with each other. In Poincaré coordinates, they descend to a single one-real-dimensional line in the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient which reads





 x(\sigma)=0\,,\qquad\varrho(\sigma)=\frac{|\sigma|^{2}}{N}\,. x⁡(σ)=0,ϱ⁡(σ)=|σ|2N.x(\sigma)=0\,,\qquad\varrho(\sigma)=\frac{|\sigma|^{2}}{N}\,.

(5.14)


This line shoots straight across the Poincaré disk.

 Refer to caption 
Figure 3: A foliation of the interior of AdS3 by the twistor lines of Burns space in the Poincaré disk model. All curves stretch from  x=0 x=0x=0 on the left to the point  x=\infty x=∞x=\infty on the right. The boundary of AdS3 is not a twistor line but is instead identified with  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2} via the transformation  x=z,\varrho=0 x=z,ϱ=0x=z,\varrho=0.




Putting these curves together, one finds a foliation of the entire interior of AdS3. This creates the image of an onion-like foliation of the Poincaré disk as displayed in figure 3. The construction of our holographic duality consists of compactification along the twistor lines. This compactifies the interior of AdS3 down to a copy of  \mathbb{R}_{+}\equiv(0,\infty) ℝ+≡(0,∞)\mathbb{R}_{+}\equiv(0,\infty) parametrized by  \|u\| ‖u‖\|u\|. Globally, the B-model on  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} SL2​(ℂ)≃AdS3×S3\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times S^{3} compactifies down to a theory on  \widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} ℂ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}. The final outcome is a duality between WZW4 + Mabuchi gravity on Burns space, and our holographic chiral algebra living on the AdS3 boundary that consists of the “celestial sphere”  \mathcal{CS}^{2} 𝒞​𝒮2\mathcal{CS}^{2}.




We emphasize that this procedure is essentially the opposite of de Boer and Solodukhin’s approach to flat holography. In [95], they conjectured that flat holography might arise from foliating Minkowski spacetime by AdS3 and dS3 slices and “uplifting” AdS3 holography to embedding space. Our procedure instead starts with a relatively standard holographic duality in AdS {}_{3}\times S^{3} 3×S3{}_{3}\times S^{3} and compactifies AdS3 along a foliation by Riemann spheres. In this way, it compactifies AdS3/CFT2 to a celestial holographic duality. Nevertheless, in agreement with de Boer and Solodukhin, we have shown that the holographic dual of our four-dimensional theory does indeed live on a two-dimensional sphere.




Finally, to complete the construction of  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}/\mathrm{SU}(2) 𝔽~/SU⁡(2)\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}/\mathrm{SU}(2), let us also mention that the divisors  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0} and  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty} project down to copies of  [0,\infty] [0,∞][0,\infty] that respectively live entirely at the points  x=0 x=0x=0 and  x=\infty x=∞x=\infty on the AdS3 boundary. They partially resemble a real blow-up of these boundary points, in the sense of replacing  x=0,\infty x=0,∞x=0,\infty at the boundary with copies of  [0,\infty] [0,∞][0,\infty] in the above picture. For example, to zoom into  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0} in the quotient, we replace the quotient by the set of points  (x,\varrho,t)\in\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times[0,\infty] (x,ϱ,t)∈AdS3×[0,∞](x,\varrho,t)\in\mathrm{AdS}_{3}\times[0,\infty] satisfying  |x|^{2}=t\varrho |x|2=t​ϱ|x|^{2}=t\varrho. Away from  x=\varrho=0 x=ϱ=0x=\varrho=0, this is diffeomorphic to AdS {}_{3}-\{x=\varrho=0\} −3{x=ϱ=0}{}_{3}-\{x=\varrho=0\}. But just as for a blow-up, the idea is that the point  x=\varrho=0 x=ϱ=0x=\varrho=0 is replaced by a copy of  [0,\infty] [0,∞][0,\infty] with coordinate  t tt. A similar analysis works for  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty} if one uses coordinates adapted to  x=\infty x=∞x=\infty.




An important point is that before the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) quotient, all twistor lines were disjoint. But from figure 3, the projections of the twistor lines to  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}/\mathrm{SU}(2) 𝔽~/SU⁡(2)\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}/\mathrm{SU}(2) naively appear to all intersect at  x=0,x=\infty x=0,x=∞x=0,x=\infty. They are only seen to be disjoint in the quotient geometry after accounting for the projections of the divisors  \overline{D}_{0},\overline{D}_{\infty} D¯0,D¯∞\overline{D}_{0},\overline{D}_{\infty}. E.g., the projected twistor line (5.10) intersects  x=\varrho=0 x=ϱ=0x=\varrho=0 at  t=\lim_{\sigma\to 0}|x(\sigma)|^{2}/\varrho(\sigma)=\|u\|^{2} t=limσ→0|x⁡(σ)|2/ϱ⁡(σ)=‖u‖2t=\lim_{\sigma\to 0}|x(\sigma)|^{2}/\varrho(\sigma)=\|u\|^{2}. The extra divisors might support extra sources or intial data of interest, but we leave their study to future work.






6 The holographic dictionary on twistor space




Celestial holography has greatly benefited from the study of extrapolate dictionaries in the past [96, 97]. Before we start building a holographic dictionary between Burns space states and celestial operators, in this section we will flesh out an extrapolate dictionary directly on twistor space. This crucially uses the AdS {}_{3}\times S^{3} 3×S3{}_{3}\times S^{3} geometry of the twistor space of Burns space that was described in the previous section. We will identify operators living on the boundary of AdS3 with states in the bulk of twistor space. The twistor space states are always in one-to-one correspondence with spacetime states via the Penrose transform.




Quite dramatically, this will enable a proof that at tree level, the  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) SU⁡(2)×U⁡(1)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) isometries of Burns space get enhanced to a full  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)×SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) Lorentz symmetry (and in fact its complexification) in the scattering amplitudes of our bulk theory. At loop level we do not provide a rigorous proof of this statement. However, we conjecture that it remains true at loop level, and our tree level argument provides very strong evidence. This enhancement will be concretely observed in the amplitudes computed in section 9, but proving it by direct calculation on Burns space turns out to be nigh-on impossible.




6.1 Relating two different compactifications




Let us recall the holographic dictionary of [16]. The bulk geometry is  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), which we coordinatize as the set of pairs of vectors  v^{\dot{\alpha}} vα˙v^{\dot{\alpha}},  w^{\dot{\alpha}} wα˙w^{\dot{\alpha}} satisfying  [v\,w]=N [v​w]=N[v\,w]=N.




The whole space has a symmetry of  \mathrm{SL}^{+}_{2}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} SL2+×SL2−\mathrm{SL}^{+}_{2}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-}, acting on the left and on the right. This is a copy of the Lorentz group. If we build a four-vector  X^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} Xα​α˙X^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} with





 X^{1\dot{\alpha}}=v^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\quad X^{2\dot{\alpha}}=w^{\dot{\alpha}} X1​α˙=vα˙,X2​α˙=wα˙X^{1\dot{\alpha}}=v^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\quad X^{2\dot{\alpha}}=w^{\dot{\alpha}}

(6.1)


then  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{+} SL2+\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{+} rotates the dotted indices of  X^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} Xα​α˙X^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} and  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} SL2−\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} rotates the undotted indices. In terms of the group manifold  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{+} SL2+\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{+} acts by left multiplication and  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} SL2−\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} acts by right multiplication. The Cartan of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} SL2−\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{-} acts on  v^{\dot{\alpha}} vα˙v^{\dot{\alpha}} with weight  1 11 and  w^{\dot{\alpha}} wα˙w^{\dot{\alpha}} with weight  -1 −1-1.




In [16] the group manifold was compactified to the quadric





 \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\{[v^{\dot{\alpha}},w^{\dot{\alpha}},t]\mid[v\,w]=Nt^{2}\}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{4}. SL¯2​(ℂ)={[vα˙,wα˙,t]∣[v​w]=N​t2}⊂ℂ​ℙ4.\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\{[v^{\dot{\alpha}},w^{\dot{\alpha}},t]\mid[v\,w]=Nt^{2}\}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{4}.

(6.2)


 \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is embedded as the patch  t=1 t=1t=1. The boundary divisor  t=0 t=0t=0 is a quadric in  \mathbb{CP}^{3} ℂ​ℙ3\mathbb{CP}^{3}, so is a  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} ℂ​ℙ+1×ℂ​ℙ−1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}. The spheres  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{\pm} ℂ​ℙ±1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{\pm} are acted on by  \mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm} SL2±\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}. The boundary chiral algebra consists of operators which live at a point  z\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} z∈ℂ​ℙ−1z\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}, and wrap  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+} ℂ​ℙ+1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}. The holographic chiral algebra is defined to be the set of such boundary operators equipped with the OPE in the  z zz-plane (i.e. the  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} ℂ​ℙ−1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} direction).




As explained in the previous section, the twistor construction leads us to a different compactification of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), that inside the blow-up  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}=\text{Bl}_{I}\mathbb{F} 𝔽~=BlI​𝔽\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}=\text{Bl}_{I}\mathbb{F} of the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} along the line at infinity  I II. In that case, the complement of the interior locus  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) consists of three divisors  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0},  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty}, and  E EE. The first two divisors are each a copy of  \widetilde{\mathbb{CP}}{}^{2} ℂ​ℙ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{CP}}{}^{2}, the blow-up of  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} at a point. They are not part of the holographic boundary but instead behave like surface defects. The third divisor  E=\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2} E=ℂ​ℙ+1×ℂ​ℙ−1=I×𝒞​𝒮2E=\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2} is the holographic boundary. It admits an action of the  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) SU⁡(2)×U⁡(1)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1) isometries of Burns space, where  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) acts on  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}=I ℂ​ℙ+1=I\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}=I and  \mathrm{U}(1) U⁡(1)\mathrm{U}(1) on  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}=\mathcal{CS}^{2} ℂ​ℙ−1=𝒞​𝒮2\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-}=\mathcal{CS}^{2}. The second sphere  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} ℂ​ℙ−1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} is the  z zz-plane that supports the celestial CFT.




There is a rational map2626

            That is, a map from a dense open subset of  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} to  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) that need not extend to all of  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}. of algebraic varieties





 \mathbb{F}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) 𝔽→SL¯2​(ℂ)\mathbb{F}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(6.3)


defined by





 \big([V^{\dot{\alpha}},V^{3}],[W_{\dot{\alpha}},W_{3}]\big)\mapsto[V^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{3},W^{\dot{\alpha}}V^{3},V^{3}W_{3}]. ([Vα˙,V3],[Wα˙,W3])↦[Vα˙​W3,Wα˙​V3,V3​W3].\big([V^{\dot{\alpha}},V^{3}],[W_{\dot{\alpha}},W_{3}]\big)\mapsto[V^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{3},W^{\dot{\alpha}}V^{3},V^{3}W_{3}].

(6.4)


This is an isomorphism on the interior  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). It is regular on the loci  \{V^{3}=0,W_{3}\neq 0\} {V3=0,W3≠0}\{V^{3}=0,W_{3}\neq 0\} and  \{W_{3}=0,V^{3}\neq 0\} {W3=0,V3≠0}\{W_{3}=0,V^{3}\neq 0\}. However, it has singularities when  V^{3}=W_{3}=0 V3=W3=0V^{3}=W_{3}=0. This is because on the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F} the coordinates  V^{\dot{\alpha}} Vα˙V^{\dot{\alpha}} and  W^{\dot{\alpha}} Wα˙W^{\dot{\alpha}} are defined up to separate rescalings by  \mathbb{C}^{\times} ℂ×\mathbb{C}^{\times}, whereas on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) only one rescaling is used, scaling  V^{\dot{\alpha}},W^{\dot{\alpha}} Vα˙,Wα˙V^{\dot{\alpha}},W^{\dot{\alpha}} at the same time. Thus, when  V^{3},W_{3}\to 0 V3,W3→0V^{3},W_{3}\to 0, the relative coefficient of  V^{\dot{\alpha}} Vα˙V^{\dot{\alpha}} and  W^{\dot{\alpha}} Wα˙W^{\dot{\alpha}} in (6.4) is undetermined.




This becomes a regular map (i.e. everywhere well-defined) if we lift it to the blow-up, giving us a map





 \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,. 𝔽~→SL¯2​(ℂ).\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,.

(6.5)


This maps the exceptional divisor  E EE to the boundary divisor  \mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} ℂ​ℙ+1×ℂ​ℙ−1\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{+}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{-} of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), while collapsing  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0} and  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty} down to copies of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. The fiber of the map  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) 𝔽~→SL¯2​(ℂ)\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is a point, except over the locus  z=0 z=0z=0,  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty in the boundary where the fiber is a copy of  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}.




Using this map, we can modify the holographic dictionary of [16]. This dictionary was defined by considering boundary conditions for the fields, and then modifying them on a curve  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z ℂ​ℙ1×z\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z in the boundary of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). We can implement the same procedure on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}, again modifying the boundary conditions on a  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z ℂ​ℙ1×z\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z.





6.2 Building an extrapolate dictionary




To do this in detail, we need to specify the boundary conditions on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} and relate them to those on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).




Let us first discuss the open string field. This is required to vanish on the boundary divisor of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). If we take such a  (0,1) (0,1)(0,1)-form on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and pull it back to  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}, it still vanishes on the boundary divisors of  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}. So we can impose the same boundary condition on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.




For the closed string fields, things are slightly more tricky. The closed string field is a  (2,1) (2,1)(2,1) form, which in [16] was allowed to have logarithmic poles on the boundary divisor.
