However, this was not entirely essential, and it is perhaps better to use the stronger boundary condition that requires that the closed string field is a regular  (2,1) (2,1)(2,1)-form on the boundary. This boundary condition was discussed in [15]. If we use this boundary condition for the closed string field, then a field on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) obviously pulls back to one on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.




In this way, we can mimic the story of [16], and define the holographic correlation functions to be the path integral of the theory where the boundary conditions are modified along a  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} in the boundary. The  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} must live in the divisor  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2} ℂ​ℙ1×ℂ​ℙ1=I×𝒞​𝒮2\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}=I\times\mathcal{CS}^{2}, at a fixed value of  z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2} z∈𝒞​𝒮2z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2}. The modification of the boundary conditions encode the choice of single-trace local operators in the dual CFT.




As discussed in [16], sections 7 and 8, modifications of the boundary condition are the same as boundary operators placed along  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z ℂ​ℙ1×z\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z. On  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}, these boundary operators (in the open string sector) are given by integrals of the holomorphic Chern-Simons gauge field  \mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-D)) 𝒜∈Ω0,1​(𝔽~,𝔤⊗𝒪⁡(−D))\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-D)) along  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z\subset E ℂ​ℙ1×z⊂E\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z\subset E, where  D=\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty}\cup E D=D¯0∪D¯∞∪ED=\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty}\cup E is the total boundary divisor. In other words, they are holomorphic Wilson lines wrapping the line at infinity  I II at a point  z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2} z∈𝒞​𝒮2z\in\mathcal{CS}^{2}.



Dictionary on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) for open string fields.




Before we build these, let us revisit the procedure of specifying such boundary operators on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). Focus on the patch  w^{\dot{2}}\neq 0 w2˙≠0w^{\dot{2}}\neq 0 without loss of generality. We let  n=t/w^{\dot{2}} n=t/w2˙n=t/w^{\dot{2}} denote a coordinate normal to the boundary  \partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1} ∂SL¯2​(ℂ)=ℂ​ℙ1×ℂ​ℙ1\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}. Let  y,z y,zy,z be coordinates on this  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1×ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}, where  y=w^{\dot{1}}/w^{\dot{2}} y=w1˙/w2˙y=w^{\dot{1}}/w^{\dot{2}} and  z=-v^{\dot{2}}/w^{\dot{2}} z=−v2˙/w2˙z=-v^{\dot{2}}/w^{\dot{2}}. The normal bundle to  \partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) ∂SL¯2​(ℂ)\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is  \mathscr{O}(1,1) 𝒪⁡(1,1)\mathscr{O}(1,1). So the normal vector  \partial_{n} ∂n\partial_{n} has a first order zero at  y=\infty y=∞y=\infty and at  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty.




Suppose we study the B-model on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). As a boundary condition, we assume that the holomorphic Chern-Simons gauge field  \mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{so}_{8}) 𝒜∈Ω0,1​(SL¯2​(ℂ),𝔰​𝔬8)\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{so}_{8}) vanishes at  n=0 n=0n=0. One can build boundary local operators by expressions like





 \int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{l}\,\partial_{n}^{k}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots ∫ℂ​ℙ1×zyl​∂nk𝒜​𝑑y+⋯\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{l}\,\partial_{n}^{k}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots

(6.6)


where  l\leq k-2 l≤k−2l\leq k-2 in order to ensure the measure has no pole at  y=\infty y=∞y=\infty. The expression as written is invariant under linearized gauge transformations. The ellipsis indicates expressions we need to add on to ensure invariance under non-linear gauge transformations. Because our boundary conditions force gauge transformations to vanish at  n=0 n=0n=0, there are a finite number of such terms; they will not be relevant for our discussion.




The normal vector  \partial_{n} ∂n\partial_{n} has spin  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2} under rotations of the  z zz-plane. Therefore the expression (6.6) has spin  k/2 k/2k/2. Further, because  l ll ranges from  0 00 to  k-2 k−2k-2, it lives in a representation of spin  (k-2)/2 (k−2)/2(k-2)/2 of the  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) rotating the dotted indices. In [16] the identification





 J[l,k-2-l]=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{l}\,\partial_{n}^{k}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots J⁡[l,k−2−l]=∫ℂ​ℙ1×zyl​∂nk𝒜​𝑑y+⋯J[l,k-2-l]=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{l}\,\partial_{n}^{k}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots

(6.7)


was made (up to normalization). The point is that both sides have the same quantum numbers. This provides an extrapolate dictionary for twisted holography on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). We wish to pull this back to a dictionary on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.




Dictionary on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} for open string fields.




How does this analysis change when we consider the blow up  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}? To answer this question, we need to understand how the normal vector  \partial_{n} ∂n\partial_{n} to the boundary divisor of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) behaves on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.




The boundary of  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} consists of three divisors. Two of them,  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0},  \overline{D}_{\infty} D¯∞\overline{D}_{\infty}, come from the boundary of the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}. The third is the exceptional divisor of the blow up, which we called  E EE.




The map





 \pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) π:𝔽~→SL¯2​(ℂ)\pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(6.8)


has the feature that





 \pi^{-1}\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty}\cup E π−1​∂SL¯2​(ℂ)=D¯0∪D¯∞∪E\pi^{-1}\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty}\cup E

(6.9)


where on the right hand side all three divisors appear with multiplicity one. For the exceptional divisor  E EE, this is clear, because  E EE maps isomorphically onto the boundary divisor of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). For the other divisors, we note that according to (6.4), the coordinate  t tt in (6.2) whose vanishing cuts out the boundary divisor of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) corresponds to the function  V^{3}W_{3} V3​W3V^{3}W_{3} on the flag variety  \mathbb{F} 𝔽\mathbb{F}, whose zero locus is  \overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty} D¯0∪D¯∞\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty} with multiplicity one.




Let us work in a local patch near the boundary of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) with coordinates  y,z,n y,z,ny,z,n as above, where we work in the region near  z=0 z=0z=0,  n=0 n=0n=0. Similarly, on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}, we can work in a local patch near the exceptional divisor  E EE, with coordinates  y,z,\widetilde{n} y,z,n~y,z,\widetilde{n} where  \widetilde{n}=0 n~=0\widetilde{n}=0 is the exceptional divisor. Pulling back the  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) coordinates  y=w^{\dot{1}}/w^{\dot{2}} y=w1˙/w2˙y=w^{\dot{1}}/w^{\dot{2}},  z=-v^{\dot{2}}/w^{\dot{2}} z=−v2˙/w2˙z=-v^{\dot{2}}/w^{\dot{2}} by the map (6.4) yields the corresponding coordinates  y=W^{\dot{1}}/W^{\dot{2}} y=W1˙/W2˙y=W^{\dot{1}}/W^{\dot{2}},  z=-V^{\dot{2}}W_{3}/W^{\dot{2}}V^{3} z=−V2˙W3/W2˙V3z=-V^{\dot{2}}W_{3}/W^{\dot{2}}V^{3} on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}. Because  W^{\dot{2}}=-W_{\dot{1}} W2˙=−W1˙W^{\dot{2}}=-W_{\dot{1}}, the latter coincides with the coordinate  z zz defined through the blow-up equations (5.7). In these coordinates, the divisor  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0} is the locus  z=0 z=0z=0.




The locus  n=0 n=0n=0 on our patch of  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) must correspond to the locus where either  \widetilde{n}=0 n~=0\widetilde{n}=0 or  z=0 z=0z=0 on our patch of  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} (with multiplicity one). This is because the limit  \widetilde{n}\to 0 n~→0\widetilde{n}\to 0 at fixed  z zz picks out  E EE, while  z\to 0 z→0z\to 0 at fixed  \widetilde{n} n~\widetilde{n} picks out  \overline{D}_{0} D¯0\overline{D}_{0}. Thus, the map between the varieties takes the form





 n=\widetilde{n}z\,. n=n~​z.n=\widetilde{n}z\,.

(6.10)


Alternatively, we can take this as the definition of  \widetilde{n} n~\widetilde{n}. In homogeneous coordinates, one finds that  \widetilde{n}=-V^{3}/V^{\dot{2}} n~=−V3/V2˙\widetilde{n}=-V^{3}/V^{\dot{2}}. In the patch  \{V^{\dot{2}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{1}}\neq 0\} {V2˙≠0,W1˙≠0}\{V^{\dot{2}}\neq 0,W_{\dot{1}}\neq 0\}, sending  V^{3}\to 0 V3→0V^{3}\to 0 while keeping  z zz fixed requires simultaneously sending  W_{3}\to 0 W3→0W_{3}\to 0, which lands on exactly the divisor  E EE.




On the locus  \widetilde{n}=0 n~=0\widetilde{n}=0, we therefore have





 \partial_{n}=z^{-1}\partial_{\widetilde{n}}\,. ∂n=z−1∂n~.\partial_{n}=z^{-1}\partial_{\widetilde{n}}\,.

(6.11)


Now consider the pullback of the expression for  J[m,n] J⁡[m,n]J[m,n]:





 J[m,n](z)=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{m}z^{-m-n-2}\,\partial_{\widetilde{n}}^{m+n+2}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots J⁡[m,n]​(z)=∫ℂ​ℙ1×zym​z−m−n−2​∂n~m+n+2𝒜​𝑑y+⋯J[m,n](z)=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}y^{m}z^{-m-n-2}\,\partial_{\widetilde{n}}^{m+n+2}\mathcal{A}\,\mathrm{d}y+\cdots

(6.12)


Noting that our boundary conditions for holomorphic Chern-Simons give  \mathcal{A} 𝒜\mathcal{A} a zero at  z=0 z=0z=0, we see that  J[m,n](z) J​[m,n]​(z)J[m,n](z) has a pole of order  m+n+1 m+n+1m+n+1 at  z=0 z=0z=0, exactly as desired.




Dictionary for closed string fields.




Let us now do the corresponding analysis for the closed string field, which is a closed  (2,1) (2,1)(2,1) form  \eta η\eta. In the coordinates  y,n,z y,n,zy,n,z above,  \eta η\eta has three components  \eta_{yn} ηy​n\eta_{yn},  \eta_{nz} ηn​z\eta_{nz},  \eta_{zy} ηz​y\eta_{zy} related by





 \partial_{z}\eta_{yn}+\partial_{y}\eta_{nz}+\partial_{n}\eta_{zy}=0. ∂zηy​n+∂yηn​z+∂nηz​y=0.\partial_{z}\eta_{yn}+\partial_{y}\eta_{nz}+\partial_{n}\eta_{zy}=0.

(6.13)


This equation tells us that only two of the three components will give us independent operators – say  \eta_{nz} ηn​z\eta_{nz} and  \eta_{yn} ηy​n\eta_{yn}. Quantities built by integrating  \eta_{zy} ηz​y\eta_{zy} over the  y yy-plane will generally2727
                27
                
                
                
              There is a single operator built from  \eta_{zy} ηz​y\eta_{zy} which is independent, namely  \int_{y}\iota_{\partial_{z}}\eta ∫yι∂z​η\int_{y}\iota_{\partial_{z}}\eta. This is of spin  1 11 and is  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) invariant.  not be independent, as they will have an  n nn-derivative and so can be transformed into expressions built from  \eta_{yn} ηy​n\eta_{yn} and  \eta_{zy} ηz​y\eta_{zy}.




The boundary operators we can build are then the integrals





 \begin{split}E[r,s]&=\int_{y}y^{r}\,\iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\eta+\cdots\\
F[r,s]&=\int_{y}\mathrm{d}y\,y^{r}\,\partial_{n}^{r+s+1}\iota_{\partial_{z}}\iota_{\partial_{n}}\eta+\cdots\end{split} E⁡[r,s]=∫yyr​ι∂n​∂nr+s−1η+⋯F⁡[r,s]=∫yd​y​yr​∂nr+s+1ι∂z​ι∂n​η+⋯\begin{split}E[r,s]&=\int_{y}y^{r}\,\iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\eta+\cdots\\
F[r,s]&=\int_{y}\mathrm{d}y\,y^{r}\,\partial_{n}^{r+s+1}\iota_{\partial_{z}}\iota_{\partial_{n}}\eta+\cdots\end{split}

(6.14)


where  \iota_{\xi}\eta\equiv\xi\,\lrcorner\,\eta ιξ​η≡ξ​⌟​η\iota_{\xi}\eta\equiv\xi\,\lrcorner\,\eta denotes interior product with a vector  \xi ξ\xi. In each equation, we integrate over  n=0 n=0n=0 at a fixed value of  z zz. In the expression for  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s], we must have  r+s>0 r+s>0r+s>0, which is consistent with  E[0,0] E⁡[0,0]E[0,0] being the identity operator. The fact that  \iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\eta ι∂n​∂nr+s−1η\iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\eta is a  1 11-form in the  y yy-plane vanishing to order  r+s r+sr+s at  y=\infty y=∞y=\infty guarantees that the integrand has no poles. Because  \partial_{n} ∂n\partial_{n} is of spin  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}, this expression has spin  \frac{1}{2}(r+s) 12​(r+s)\frac{1}{2}(r+s), giving us the correct quantum numbers for  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s] as defined on the CFT side.




Similarly, the expression for  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] has spin  \frac{1}{2}(r+s)+2 12​(r+s)+2\frac{1}{2}(r+s)+2, and so has the same quantum numbers as the CFT operator.




As we see from equations (6.14), when we work on the flag variety and replace  \partial_{n} ∂n\partial_{n} by  z^{-1}\partial_{\widetilde{n}} z−1∂n~z^{-1}\partial_{\widetilde{n}},  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s] has a pole at  z=0 z=0z=0 of order  r+s r+sr+s, and  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] has a pole of order  r+s+2 r+s+2r+s+2, exactly as desired.






6.3 Matching correlators on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} and  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})




One can ask whether the holographic correlators defined using the compactification  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} coincide with those for the compactification  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). In this section we will prove this statement for all tree level holographic correlators, and argue that at loop level the statement remains true.




Consider placing boundary operators such as those in (6.7) at points  z_{i} ziz_{i} where  z_{i}\neq 0,\infty zi≠0,∞z_{i}\neq 0,\infty. These source linear fields in the topological string on  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and the CFT correlation function is given by the topological string scattering amplitude of these field configurations.




It turns out that the fields sourced by these operators are the same whether we construct it using the  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) boundary condition or the  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} boundary condition. Let us phrase this result as a theorem:



Theorem 2.



There is a unique (up to gauge equivalence) field configuration on either  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} or  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) sourced by any boundary operator  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s],  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] placed at  z\neq 0,\infty z≠0,∞z\neq 0,\infty on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}. Further, the field configuration on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} is gauge-equivalent to the field configuration pulled back from  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).




Proof.



Let us first check this for the open string states. We will show that the field sourced by a boundary operator  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s] on either  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) or  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} is unique up to gauge equivalence. The ambiguity in finding a solution to the equations of motion with a given source term, up to gauge equivalence, is an element of Dolbeault  H^{1} H1H^{1} with coefficient in the sheaf of functions vanishing on the boundary. The obstruction to finding a global field sourcing a given boundary operator is an element of Dolbeault  H^{2} H2H^{2}. Thus, it suffices to know that





 \begin{split}H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))&=0\\
H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathscr{O}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E))&=0\,.\end{split} H∗∂¯(SL¯2(ℂ),𝒪(−∂SL¯2(ℂ)))=0H∂¯∗​(𝔽~,𝒪⁡(−D¯0−D¯∞−E))=0.\begin{split}H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))&=0\\
H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathscr{O}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E))&=0\,.\end{split}

(6.15)


We check this (easy) vanishing result in Appendix E.




For the closed string fields, the argument is a little more complicated. We let  \Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}} Ωcl2\Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}} be the complex of sheaves  \Omega^{2}\xrightarrow{\partial}\Omega^{3} Ω2→∂Ω3\Omega^{2}\xrightarrow{\partial}\Omega^{3}. The closed string fields live in





 H^{1}(X,\Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}}) H1​(X,Ωcl2)H^{1}(X,\Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}})

(6.16)


where  X XX is either  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} or  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). This is the same as





 H^{2,1}(X)\oplus H^{3,0}(X) H2,1​(X)⊕H3,0​(X)H^{2,1}(X)\oplus H^{3,0}(X)

(6.17)


which is easily seen to be zero, because in either case  H^{3}(X)=0 H3​(X)=0H^{3}(X)=0. This tells us that, if a field sourced by a boundary operator exists, it is necessarily unique up to gauge transformations.




The obstruction to existing lives in





 H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}})=H^{2,2}(X)\oplus H^{3,1}(X) H2​(X,Ωcl2)=H2,2​(X)⊕H3,1​(X)H^{2}(X,\Omega^{2}_{\text{cl}})=H^{2,2}(X)\oplus H^{3,1}(X)

(6.18)


(and  H^{3,1}(X)=0 H3,1​(X)=0H^{3,1}(X)=0). By the Lefschetz hyperplane theorem and Poincaré duality,





 H^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathbb{C} H2,2​(SL¯2​(ℂ))=ℂH^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathbb{C}

(6.19)


with a basis given by the location  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z ℂ​ℙ1×z\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z where we insert a boundary operator. This group contains the obstruction to finding a field sourced by a boundary operator. Similarly, one can show that  H^{2,2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}})=\mathbb{C}^{3} H2,2​(𝔽~)=ℂ3H^{2,2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}})=\mathbb{C}^{3}.




To complete the proof, we need to check that the obstruction vanishes for the boundary operators  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] in equation (6.14). Let us perform the analysis for  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s]; the  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] case is similar.




Using the kinetic term  \partial^{-1}\eta\wedge\bar{\partial}\eta ∂−1η∧∂¯​η\partial^{-1}\eta\wedge\bar{\partial}\eta, we see that the field sourced by  E[r,s](z) E​[r,s]​(z)E[r,s](z) in (6.14) satisfies





 \bar{\partial}\eta=\partial\left(y^{r}\iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\bar{\delta}_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}\right)\,. ∂¯​η=∂(yr​ι∂n​∂nr+s−1δ¯ℂ​ℙ1×z).\bar{\partial}\eta=\partial\left(y^{r}\iota_{\partial_{n}}\partial_{n}^{r+s-1}\bar{\delta}_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z}\right)\,.

(6.20)


This can be solved as long as the  (2,2) (2,2)(2,2) form on the right hand side is zero in  H^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) H2,2​(SL¯2​(ℂ))H^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})). This is automatic, however, by Hodge theory: the map





 H^{2,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) H2,1​(SL¯2​(ℂ))→∂H2,2​(SL¯2​(ℂ))H^{2,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{2,2}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

(6.21)


is zero, and the right hand side of (6.20) is in the image of this map.




This argument applies on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} in the same way, so that in each case there is a unique field configuration up to gauge equivalence sourced by a given operator. Uniqueness implies that the pullback of the field sourced by an operator on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is (up to a gauge transformation) the field sourced by an operator on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.
∎





This almost immediately tells us that holographic correlators are the same whether computed on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) or on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}. Let us explain this in detail for the open string sector; the argument in the closed string and mixed sectors is identical.




Let us start with the holographic two-point function of  J[r,s](z_{1}) J​[r,s]​(z1)J[r,s](z_{1}) with  J[s,r](z_{2}) J​[s,r]​(z2)J[s,r](z_{2}). Let  \mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1}) ℱ​[r,s]​(z1)\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1}) be the field sourced by  J[r,s](z_{1}) J​[r,s]​(z1)J[r,s](z_{1}) on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). Then, on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), the holographic two-point function is





 \left\langle J[r,s](z_{1})J[s,r](z_{2})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2}}\mathrm{d}y\;y^{s}\,\partial_{n}^{s+m+2}\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1})\,. ⟨J⁡[r,s]​(z1)​J​[s,r]​(z2)⟩SL¯2​(ℂ)=∫ℂ​ℙ1×z2d​y​ys​∂ns+m+2ℱ⁡[r,s]​(z1).\left\langle J[r,s](z_{1})J[s,r](z_{2})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2}}\mathrm{d}y\;y^{s}\,\partial_{n}^{s+m+2}\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1})\,.

(6.22)


In this expression, we have inserted the field configuration  \mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1}) ℱ​[r,s]​(z1)\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1}) into the linear term of the operator  J[s,r](z_{2}) J​[s,r]​(z2)J[s,r](z_{2}) defined in equation (6.7). (Here, we are not concerned with details of normalization: only the structure of the expression is needed to compare the results on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} and on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})).




The corresponding expression on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} is almost exactly the same, except that everything has been pulled back along the map  \pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) π:𝔽~→SL¯2​(ℂ)\pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). We get





 \left\langle J[r,s](z_{1})J[s,r](z_{2})\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2}}\mathrm{d}y\;y^{s}\,\partial_{n}^{s+m+2}\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1})\,. ⟨J⁡[r,s]​(z1)​J​[s,r]​(z2)⟩𝔽~=∫ℂ​ℙ1×z2d​y​ys​∂ns+m+2π∗​ℱ​[r,s]​(z1).\left\langle J[r,s](z_{1})J[s,r](z_{2})\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2}}\mathrm{d}y\;y^{s}\,\partial_{n}^{s+m+2}\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r,s](z_{1})\,.

(6.23)


Since the map  \pi π\pi is an isomorphism on the  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2} ℂ​ℙ1×z2\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{2} we are considering, these expressions are the same.




Next, let us consider the  3 33-point function of open string operators placed at  z_{1},z_{2},z_{3} z1,z2,z3z_{1},z_{2},z_{3} where  z_{i}\neq 0 zi≠0z_{i}\neq 0,  z_{i}\neq\infty zi≠∞z_{i}\neq\infty. We can take the operators to be  J[r_{i},s_{i}] J⁡[ri,si]J[r_{i},s_{i}], as in equation (6.7). We let  \mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) ℱ⁡[ri,si]​(zi)\mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) be the fields they source. These are field configurations on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}):





 \mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i})\in\overline{\Omega}^{0,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{so}_{8}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))\,. ℱ[ri,si](zi)∈Ω¯0,1(SL¯2(ℂ),𝔰𝔬8⊗𝒪(−∂SL¯2(ℂ))).\mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i})\in\overline{\Omega}^{0,1}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{so}_{8}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))\,.

(6.24)


The notation  \overline{\Omega}^{0,1} Ω¯0,1\overline{\Omega}^{0,1} indicates that these are distributional  (0,1) (0,1)(0,1) forms. It is important that these distributions are regular away from  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{i} ℂ​ℙ1×zi\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{i} in the boundary. These field configurations are uniquely specified by the gauge, linearized equations of motion, boundary conditions, and source term.




The holographic  3 33-point function defined in  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is





 \left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\\
=\int_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\,\Omega ⟨J⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​J​[r2,s2]​(z2)​J​[r3,s3]​(z3)⟩SL¯2​(ℂ)=∫SL¯2​(ℂ)ℱ⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2)​ℱ​[r3,s3]​(z3)​Ω\left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\\
=\int_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\,\Omega

(6.25)


where  \Omega Ω\Omega is the meromorphic volume form on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).




The holographic  3 33-point function on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} is defined in exactly the same way:





 \left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\\
=\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\,\pi^{\ast}\Omega\,. ⟨J⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​J​[r2,s2]​(z2)​J​[r3,s3]​(z3)⟩𝔽~=∫𝔽~π∗​ℱ​[r1,s1]​(z1)​π∗​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2)​π∗​ℱ​[r3,s3]​(z3)​π∗​Ω.\left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\right\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\\
=\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\,\pi^{\ast}\Omega\,.

(6.26)


All quantitites in the integrand, including the meromorphic volume form, have been pulled back to  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}.




It is almost evident that the expresions (6.25) and (6.26) are the same. If the  \mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) ℱ⁡[ri,si]​(zi)\mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) were smooth, then both expressions are given by absolutely convergent integrals on the interior  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and so they must coincide.




A slight subtlety is engendered by the fact that  \mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) ℱ⁡[ri,si]​(zi)\mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) has distributional singularities in the boundary on the locus  \mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{i} ℂ​ℙ1×zi\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times z_{i}. This is not a serious problem: each integral can be computed on the open region





 U=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}-\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty} U=𝔽~−D¯0∪D¯∞U=\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}-\overline{D}_{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\infty}

(6.27)


where the locus  z=0,\infty z=0,∞z=0,\infty in the boundary has been removed. The map  \pi π\pi is an isomorphism from  U UU onto its image in  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), and the integrands in (6.26) and (6.25) are quantities that are smooth and absolutely convergent away from a compact set in  U UU. It follows that the integrals agree.




Now let us generalize this to show that the holographic  n nn-point functions at tree level coincide whether we use  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} or  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), as long as the insertions are of operators with  z\neq 0,\infty z≠0,∞z\neq 0,\infty. We will present the details for the  4 44-point function; the general case is similar. Consider operators  J[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) J⁡[ri,si]​(zi)J[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) sourcing fields  \mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) ℱ⁡[ri,si]​(zi)\mathcal{F}[r_{i},s_{i}](z_{i}) on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), as above. The four-point function





 \left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})J[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})} ⟨J⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​J​[r2,s2]​(z2)​J​[r3,s3]​(z3)​J​[r4,s4]​(z4)⟩SL¯2​(ℂ)\left\langle J[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})J[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})J[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})J[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\right\rangle_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}

(6.28)


has, as usual,  3 33 channels. We will focus on the contribution of one channel, and see that the computation of this channel on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) or  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} yields the same result.




In one channel, the four-point function is





 \int_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\bigl\{\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\bigr\}\,\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\mathcal{F}[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\,\Omega\,. ∫SL¯2​(ℂ)∂¯−1​{ℱ⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2)}​ℱ​[r3,s3]​(z3)​ℱ​[r4,s4]​(z4)​Ω.\int_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\bigl\{\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\bigr\}\,\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\mathcal{F}[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\,\Omega\,.

(6.29)


The four-point function on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} in the same channel is





 \int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\bigl\{\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\bigr\}\,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\,\pi^{*}\Omega\,. ∫𝔽~∂¯−1​{π∗​ℱ​[r1,s1]​(z1)​π∗​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2)}​π∗​ℱ​[r3,s3]​(z3)​π∗​ℱ​[r4,s4]​(z4)​π∗​Ω.\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\bigl\{\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\bigr\}\,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{3},s_{3}](z_{3})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{4},s_{4}](z_{4})\,\pi^{*}\Omega\,.

(6.30)


The two integrals will coincide, as long as we can show that





 \bar{\partial}^{-1}\left(\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\right)=\pi^{\ast}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\right). ∂¯−1​(π∗​ℱ​[r1,s1]​(z1)​π∗​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2))=π∗​∂¯−1​(ℱ⁡[r1,s1]​(z1)​ℱ​[r2,s2]​(z2)).\bar{\partial}^{-1}\left(\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\right)=\pi^{\ast}\bar{\partial}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}[r_{1},s_{1}](z_{1})\mathcal{F}[r_{2},s_{2}](z_{2})\right).

(6.31)


Here, by  \bar{\partial}^{-1} ∂¯−1\bar{\partial}^{-1} we mean any solution of the  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} equation with a source; we have seen that any two solutions are gauge equivalent.




This equation is easily seen to hold by applying  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} to both sides. The point is that  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial} commutes with  \pi^{\ast} π∗\pi^{\ast}, as long as  \pi^{\ast} π∗\pi^{\ast} is only applied to distributions whose singularities lie in the locus where the map  \pi π\pi is a diffeomorphism.




In this way we see that the four-point functions on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) and on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} match channel by channel. It is straightforward to generalize this to show that holographic  n nn-point functions at tree level coincide whether we use  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} or  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), as long as the insertions are of operators with  z\neq 0,\infty z≠0,∞z\neq 0,\infty.




It is also natural to conjecture:



Conjecture 1.



The holographic correlators for insertions at  z\neq 0,\infty z≠0,∞z\neq 0,\infty for  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} and  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) coincide at loop level as well as tree level.





One can argue for this as follows. As we have seen, the states sourced by boundary operators are the same whether we work on  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}} or on  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). The holographic correlator is the scattering amplitude of these states on the non-compact geometry  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). This scattering amplitude should be independent of the boundary structure, except for the region of the boundary on which the states we are scattering localize. Therefore, the scattering amplitude should be the same whether we use  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) or  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}. Of course, a careful proof of this conjecture would require one to find counterterms at loop level that respect such enhanced symmetries.




This conjecture has some quite remarkable consequences. Firstly, by the Penrose transform, it is immediate that the holographic correlators on twistor space are the same as scattering amplitudes of WZW4 plus Mabuchi gravity on Burns space. After all, the Penrose transform of the states sourced by the boundary operators on twistor space are single-particle states on Burns space, and the topological string Lagrangian on twistor space corresponds as we have seen to the Lagrangian on Burns space.
Now, on twistor space, if we use the compactification  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), the holographic dual chiral algebra has no defects. It is simply the BRST reduction of certain symplectic bosons by  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N). At infinite  N NN, this algebra has no conformal blocks.2828
              28
              
              
              
            This is because all the operators  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s],  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] have positive spin. As we saw in Proposition 4.1, the full space of conformal blocks is the symmetric algebra of the space of possible one-point functions. For operators of positive spin, one-point functions must vanish, as they have no poles and yet must vanish at  \infty ∞\infty.  This means correlation functions are uniquely defined.




We see that conjecture 1 now implies the following:



Conjecture 2.



At all orders in  1/N 1/N1/N, the scattering amplitudes for WZW4 plus Mabuchi gravity on Burns space coincide with the (uniquely-defined) correlators for the large  N NN chiral algebra with no defects.





At tree level, this is a theorem. This conjecture implies a remarkable simplicity in the scattering amplitudes on Burns space: after all, correlators of the large  N NN chiral algebra are essentially combinatorial in nature, since it is the BRST reduction of a free theory.




At tree level, this result implies that all  + ++ amplitudes for Yang-Mills theory on Burns space are precisely the correlators of open string states of the planar chiral algebra.




There are a few more important statements one can prove at tree level. In [16], it was shown in the context of the type II topological B-model that one can match:

1. 



Single-trace bosonic modes in the planar chiral algebra which preserve the vacuum at  0 00 and  \infty ∞\infty. This Lie algebra, sometimes called the wedge algebra, is the Lie algebra of modes which preserve all planar correlators.

2. 



The Lie algebra  \operatorname{Vect}_{0}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) Vect0⁡(SL2​(ℂ))\operatorname{Vect}_{0}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) of divergence free holomorphic vector fields on  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). These are infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) which preserve the complex structure and volume form. As such, they are symmetries of all holographic correlators at tree level.



In [16] it was shown that all planar correlation functions in both boundary and bulk theories are essentially completely determined by these symmetries.




A small variant of the arguments of [16] shows that, in the type I topological string, in both bulk and boundary systems, we have similar infinite dimensional symmetry algebras arising as symmetries of planar correlators. In the open string sector, it is the Lie algebra of holomorphic maps from  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) to  \mathfrak{so}_{8} 𝔰​𝔬8\mathfrak{so}_{8}, and in the closed string sector it is again the Lie algebra of divergence-free holomorphic vector fields on  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).




This tells us that scattering amplitudes on Burns space have not just the  \mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)×SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2) symmetry mentioned above, but an infinite-dimensional enhancement to  \operatorname{Vect}_{0}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) Vect0⁡(SL2​(ℂ))\operatorname{Vect}_{0}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})).






7 States in the 4d theory




To test our holographic duality in the planar limit, in the sections that follow, we will describe a correspondence between scattering states on Burns space and single trace, gauge invariant operators of the large  N NN chiral algebra. The former will be built as solutions of linearized field equations on Burns space, while the latter have been listed in section 4 by means of BRST cohomology.




7.1 States and propagator of WZW4




To compute scattering amplitudes, one starts with solutions of linearized free field equations to build a space of states of the bulk theory and proceeds to include interactions between the states perturbatively via propagators and interaction vertices. Such linearized solutions are commonly known as scattering wavefunctions. To perform this analysis for the “positive helicity gluon” states of the WZW4 model, we will solve the Laplace equation for the adjoint-valued scalar  \phi ϕ\phi defined in (2.29). Demanding plane wave asymptotics, we discover the remarkably compact solutions displayed in (7.29) below. The corresponding linearized spin 1 wavefunction may be found by applying  A=-\bar{\partial}\phi A=−∂¯​ϕA=-\bar{\partial}\phi. In the latter half of this section, we will also derive the Green’s function of the Laplacian on Burns space, which acts as the scalar propagator for  \phi ϕ\phi. The result for this is equation (7.46).



Wavefunctions in WZW4.




As seen from (2.31), the linearized free field equation of the adjoint-valued scalar  \phi ϕ\phi is  \omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=0 ω∧∂∂¯​ϕ=0\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=0. This is equivalent to the Laplace equation:





 \triangle\phi=4\mathrm{i}\,\frac{\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi}{\omega^{2}}=0\,, △​ϕ=4​i​ω∧∂∂¯​ϕω2=0,\triangle\phi=4\mathrm{i}\,\frac{\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi}{\omega^{2}}=0\,,

(7.1)


having recognized the commonly used expression for the Laplacian on Kähler 4-manifolds. The Laplacian associated to the Burns metric (3.38) has a simple explicit expression,





 \triangle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{|g|}}\bigg(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\bigg)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,, △=2|g|​(ϵα˙​β˙+N​uα˙​u^β˙‖u‖4)​∂2∂uα˙​∂u^β˙,\triangle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{|g|}}\bigg(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\bigg)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,,

(7.2)


where  \sqrt{|g|}=1+N/\|u\|^{2} |g|=1+N/‖u‖2\sqrt{|g|}=1+N/\|u\|^{2} is the square root of the metric determinant. This inherits the  \mathrm{U}(2) U⁡(2)\mathrm{U}(2) symmetries of Burns space.




In what follows, our strategy will be to construct solutions  \phi ϕ\phi that are expressible as formal Taylor series in  N NN with summands spanning an “integer basis” of polynomial solutions. Our polynomial solutions will be the spacetime analogue of the discrete basis of states of holomorphic Chern Simons theory on the deformed conifold found in [16]. We will start with constructing such a polynomial basis, then assemble infinite linear combinations of the polynomial solutions that resum into a wavefunction with plane-wave-like asymptotics. This is somewhat in the spirit of recent work on celestial holography [98], though our polynomial solutions won’t correspond to soft modes by themselves. Instead, they provide a Burns space analogue of the integer basis of flat space states introduced in [99], which were themselves analogous to quasinormal modes in de Sitter space.




Of course, in practice there are many ways to solve such a Laplace equation. For example, neglecting the prefactor of  \sqrt{|g|} |g|\sqrt{|g|} that drops from the Laplace equation, the Burns space Laplacian (7.2) splits cleanly into a flat space Laplacian plus an order  N NN deformation. Therefore, a more systematic approach would be to decompose  \phi ϕ\phi into a flat space solution plus an  N NN dependent correction, and solve for the latter perturbatively in  N NN using the Green’s function of the flat space Laplacian. This works in greater generality but can be computationally cumbersome and relatively unintuitive. Another alternative would be to apply the Penrose transform [100, 48, 101], which we anticipate to be a powerful tool but leave to future work.




Let us also remark that the perturbative expansions in “small”  N NN that we employ below may appear unconventional at first, as we want to take the large  N NN limit at the end of the day. However, they will generally either be finite polynomials in  N NN, or will take the form of Taylor expansions in  N NN that can be explicitly resummed into expressions analytic in  N NN. For instance, our brane backreaction was already seen to be polynomial in  N NN, both at the level of the twistor complex structure (3.5) and the Burns metric (3.38). Subsequently, the coordinate transformation  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙↦N​uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}} mentioned in (3.40) can be applied to systematically map our small  N NN perturbation theory to a  1/\sqrt{N} 1/N1/\sqrt{N} expansion. The fact that this works appears to be a simplifying characteristic of twisted holography – as opposed to standard holography – and has been key to the Koszul duality based determinations of backreaction effects in past work on AdS3 [17]. We will see this happen concretely when we come to the computation of scattering amplitudes.




For now, let us start by looking for solutions that are polynomials in the complex coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙,u^α˙u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}, diagonalize the left-handed  \mathrm{U}(1) U⁡(1)\mathrm{U}(1) isometry generated by the Killing vector  u\cdot\partial_{u}-\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}} u⋅∂u−u^⋅∂u^u\cdot\partial_{u}-\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}}, and transform in irreps of the unbroken right-handed  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) isometries. To discover such solutions, let us momentarily return to flat space. On  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, solutions of the Laplace equation with the above properties are given by the totally symmetrized polynomials





 u^{(\dot{\alpha}_{1}}\cdots u^{\dot{\alpha}_{k}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}_{1}}\cdots\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}_{l})}\,,\qquad k,l\in\mathbb{N}\,. u(α˙1CLOSE⋯uα˙ku^β˙1⋯u^OPENβ˙l),k,l∈ℕ.u^{(\dot{\alpha}_{1}}\cdots u^{\dot{\alpha}_{k}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}_{1}}\cdots\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}_{l})}\,,\qquad k,l\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(7.3)


Since  u^{\dot{\alpha}}=x^{1\dot{\alpha}} uα˙=x1​α˙u^{\dot{\alpha}}=x^{1\dot{\alpha}} and  \hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}=x^{2\dot{\alpha}} u^α˙=x2​α˙\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}=x^{2\dot{\alpha}} transform in the same spin  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2} representation of the right-handed  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2), this set of wavefunctions spans the spin  (k+l)/2 (k+l)/2(k+l)/2 irrep of  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2). For identical values of  k+l k+lk+l, solutions with differing  k,l k,lk,l are distinguished from each other by their eigenvalue  k-l k−lk-l under  u\cdot\partial_{u}-\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}} u⋅∂u−u^⋅∂u^u\cdot\partial_{u}-\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}}.




To verify that the expressions in (7.3) are annihilated by the flat space Laplacian





 \triangle_{0}\equiv 2\,\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,, △0≡2​ϵα˙​β˙​∂2∂uα˙​∂u^β˙,\triangle_{0}\equiv 2\,\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,,

(7.4)


it is useful to introduce an auxiliary spinor  \tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}^{2} λ~α˙∈ℂ2\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}^{2} and democratically contract it into all the  k+l k+lk+l indices of (7.3). This yields a complex wavefunction





 [u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l} [u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}

(7.5)


which satisfies the flat space Laplace equation because  \triangle_{0}([u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l})\propto[\tilde{\lambda}\,\tilde{\lambda}]=0 △0​([u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l)∝[λ~​λ~]=0\triangle_{0}([u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l})\propto[\tilde{\lambda}\,\tilde{\lambda}]=0. Unsurprisingly, it will turn out that this auxiliary spinor is the same as the spinor-helicity variable  \tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} λ~α˙\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} that enters expressions for scattering amplitudes [102].




It is now a straightforward exercise to correct these order-by-order in the backreaction to obtain the analogous solutions on Burns space. We demand that the corrections transform in the same representations of  \mathrm{U}(2) U⁡(2)\mathrm{U}(2) as (7.5), so the corrections can necessarily only differ from (7.5) by  \mathrm{U}(2) U⁡(2)\mathrm{U}(2) invariant factors. The most basic  \mathrm{U}(2) U⁡(2)\mathrm{U}(2) invariant is the Euclidean norm of the coordinates,  \|u\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,x^{2} ‖u‖2=12​x2\|u\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,x^{2}, so the new solution should equal (7.5) times a factor depending on  \|u\| ‖u‖\|u\|.




These considerations – along with a bit of hindsight – motivate us to use the ansatz





 \phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}](x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}F(\chi)\,,\qquad\chi\vcentcolon=\frac{\|u\|^{2}}{N}\,, ϕk,l​[λ~]​(x)=𝖳a​[u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l​F​(χ),χ:=‖u‖2N,\phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}](x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}F(\chi)\,,\qquad\chi\vcentcolon=\frac{\|u\|^{2}}{N}\,,

(7.6)


where  \mathsf{T}_{a} 𝖳a\mathsf{T}_{a} is a generator of the Lie algebra  \mathfrak{g} 𝔤\mathfrak{g} occurring in WZW4. Plugging this into the Laplace equation on Burns space, we successfully extract an ODE for  F(\chi) F⁡(χ)F(\chi) that is sufficient for our ansatz to work,





 \chi^{2}(\chi+1)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}F}{\mathrm{d}\chi^{2}}+\chi\big((k+l+2)(\chi+1)-1\big)\frac{\mathrm{d}F}{\mathrm{d}\chi}+klF=0\,. χ2​(χ+1)​d2​Fd​χ2+χ⁡((k+l+2)​(χ+1)−1)​d​Fd​χ+k​l​F=0.\chi^{2}(\chi+1)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}F}{\mathrm{d}\chi^{2}}+\chi\big((k+l+2)(\chi+1)-1\big)\frac{\mathrm{d}F}{\mathrm{d}\chi}+klF=0\,.

(7.7)


When  k,l\in\mathbb{N} k,l∈ℕk,l\in\mathbb{N}, this ODE can be solved in terms of Jacobi polynomials,





 F(\chi)=\begin{cases}C\chi^{-l}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)\,,\qquad k\geq l\,,\\
\vskip-11.99998pt\\
C\chi^{-k}P_{k}^{l-k,0}(1+2\chi)\,,\qquad k<l\,,\end{cases} F⁡(χ)={C​χ−l​Plk−l,0​(1+2​χ),k≥l,C​χ−k​Pkl−k,0​(1+2​χ),k<l,F(\chi)=\begin{cases}C\chi^{-l}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)\,,\qquad k\geq l\,,\\
\vskip-11.99998pt\\
C\chi^{-k}P_{k}^{l-k,0}(1+2\chi)\,,\qquad k<l\,,\end{cases}

(7.8)


up to a constant of integration  C CC. As we are working with a second order ODE, in principle we also find a second set of solutions. But they involve  \log\chi log⁡χ\log\chi, so are not well-behaved in the  \|u\|\to 0 ‖u‖→0\|u\|\to 0 limit. We suspect that they are Burns space analogues of the shadow transformed massless wavefunctions studied in [11]. We leave their inclusion to future work.




The Jacobi polynomials  P_{n}^{a,b}(t) Pna,b​(t)P_{n}^{a,b}(t) are a well-known class of classical orthogonal polynomials. The ones occurring here are given by





 \begin{split}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)&=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\frac{(k+j)!}{(k-l+j)!}\,\frac{\chi^{j}}{j!(l-j)!}\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\,\chi^{l-j}\,,\end{split} Plk−l,0​(1+2​χ)=∑j=0l(k+j)!(k−l+j)!​χjj!​(l−j)!=∑j=0l(k+l−j)!j!​(k−j)!​(l−j)!​χl−j,\begin{split}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)&=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\frac{(k+j)!}{(k-l+j)!}\,\frac{\chi^{j}}{j!(l-j)!}\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\,\chi^{l-j}\,,\end{split}

(7.9)


for  k\geq l k≥lk\geq l, with the case  k<l k<lk<l obtained by exchanging  k kk and  l ll. Jacobi polynomials famously enter the Wigner D-matrices of  \mathrm{SU}(2) SU⁡(2)\mathrm{SU}(2) representation theory [103], so their appearance here is not a total surprise. Curiously, the specific polynomials  P_{l}^{k-l,0} Plk−l,0P_{l}^{k-l,0} are also related to the Zernike polynomials  R_{p}^{q} RpqR_{p}^{q} used in optical imaging, e.g.,





 R_{k+l}^{k-l}(\sqrt{-\chi})=(-1)^{l}(-\chi)^{\frac{k-l}{2}}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)\,. Rk+lk−l​(−χ)=(−1)l​(−χ)k−l2​Plk−l,0​(1+2​χ).R_{k+l}^{k-l}(\sqrt{-\chi})=(-1)^{l}(-\chi)^{\frac{k-l}{2}}P_{l}^{k-l,0}(1+2\chi)\,.

(7.10)


It is tempting to speculate that Burns space must literally feel like a lens or a holographic medium for the positive helicity photons or gluons traversing through it!




To constrain the integration constant, we impose the boundary condition that our solution reduces to the flat space value (7.5) as  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty. At the level of  F(\chi) F⁡(χ)F(\chi), this is the boundary condition





 \lim_{\chi\to\infty}F(\chi)=1\,. limχ→∞F⁡(χ)=1.\lim_{\chi\to\infty}F(\chi)=1\,.

(7.11)


It is easily solved for  C CC to find





 C=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\,, C=k!​l!(k+l)!,C=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\,,

(7.12)


valid for either  k\geq l k≥lk\geq l or  k<l k<lk<l. With this choice, the full solutions also happen to admit a uniform expression for all values of  k,l\in\mathbb{N} k,l∈ℕk,l\in\mathbb{N},





 \phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}](x)=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k,l)}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\left(\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{j}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,\mathsf{T}_{a}\,, ϕk,l​[λ~]​(x)=k!​l!(k+l)!​∑j=0min⁡(k,l)(k+l−j)!j!​(k−j)!​(l−j)!​(N‖u‖2)j​[u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l​𝖳a,\phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}](x)=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k,l)}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\left(\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{j}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,\mathsf{T}_{a}\,,

(7.13)


having reinstated  \chi=\|u\|^{2}/N χ=‖u‖2/N\chi=\|u\|^{2}/N. As promised, this reduces to (7.5) asymptotically as  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty or in the limit  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0 of no backreaction.




This result gives us another example of the phenomenon where the backreaction turns out to be polynomial in  N NN, at least at the level of such building blocks. We now come to more involved wavefunctions that are Taylor series in  N NN but asymptotically behave like the familiar momentum eigenstates from flat space.




The most commonly used scattering wavefunctions in flat space are momentum eigenstates labeled by a null momentum  p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} pα​α˙p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}},





 \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,,\qquad p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,. ei​p⋅x,pα​α˙=λα​λ~α˙.\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,,\qquad p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(7.14)


As shown here, the nullity constraint  p^{2}=0 p2=0p^{2}=0 is trivialized in terms of a pair of constant spinors  \lambda_{\alpha} λα\lambda_{\alpha},  \tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} λ~α˙\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} of opposite chirality. These are known variously as momentum spinors or spinor-helicity variables. In Euclidean signature, any null momentum must be complex, so the spinor-helicity variables are also generically complex-valued. They are defined up to little group scaling,





 (\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}})\sim(s\lambda_{\alpha},s^{-1}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}})\,,\qquad s\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}. (λα,λ~α˙)∼(s​λα,s−1​λ~α˙),s∈ℂ×.(\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}})\sim(s\lambda_{\alpha},s^{-1}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}})\,,\qquad s\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

(7.15)


When taken up to an overall energy scale, they act as homogeneous coordinates on the complexified celestial sphere.




To compute scattering amplitudes on Burns space, we want to construct solutions that asymptote to such plane wave states.
We will do this by expanding  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} ei​p⋅x\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} as a double series in  [u\,\tilde{\lambda}] [u​λ~][u\,\tilde{\lambda}] and  [\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}] [u^​λ~][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}] and turning on the backreaction term by term. Starting with





 \begin{split}p\cdot x&=p_{1\dot{\alpha}}x^{1\dot{\alpha}}+p_{2\dot{\alpha}}x^{2\dot{\alpha}}\\
&=\lambda_{1}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]+\lambda_{2}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]\,,\end{split} p⋅x=p1​α˙​x1​α˙+p2​α˙​x2​α˙=λ1​[u​λ~]+λ2​[u^​λ~],\begin{split}p\cdot x&=p_{1\dot{\alpha}}x^{1\dot{\alpha}}+p_{2\dot{\alpha}}x^{2\dot{\alpha}}\\
&=\lambda_{1}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]+\lambda_{2}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]\,,\end{split}

(7.16)


we can Taylor expand





 \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}=\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{k+l}\lambda_{1}^{k}\lambda_{2}^{l}}{k!\,l!}\,[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,. ei​p⋅x=∑k,l≥0ik+l​λ1k​λ2lk!​l!​[u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l.\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}=\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{k+l}\lambda_{1}^{k}\lambda_{2}^{l}}{k!\,l!}\,[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,.

(7.17)


To deform this into a wavefunction for the adjoint-valued scalar on Burns space, all we need to do is replace each factor of  [u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l} [u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l} by the polynomial solution  \phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}] ϕk,l​[λ~]\phi_{k,l}[\tilde{\lambda}] found in (7.13) and resum the resulting series,





 \phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{k+l}\lambda_{1}^{k}\lambda_{2}^{l}}{(k+l)!}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k,l)}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\left(\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{j}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,. ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​∑k,l≥0ik+l​λ1k​λ2l(k+l)!​∑j=0min⁡(k,l)(k+l−j)!j!​(k−j)!​(l−j)!​(N‖u‖2)j​[u​λ~]k​[u^​λ~]l.\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{k+l}\lambda_{1}^{k}\lambda_{2}^{l}}{(k+l)!}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k,l)}\frac{(k+l-j)!}{j!(k-j)!(l-j)!}\left(\frac{N}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{j}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{k}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]^{l}\,.

(7.18)


Exchanging the sum over  j jj with the sums over  k,l k,lk,l, then shifting  k\mapsto k+j k↦k+jk\mapsto k+j,  l\mapsto l+j l↦l+jl\mapsto l+j, we can reduce this to sums of hypergeometric type,





 \begin{split}\phi(x)&=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{(k+l+j)!}{(k+l+2j)!}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}])^{k}(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}])^{l}}{k!\,l!}\\
&=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{(n+j)!}{(n+2j)!}\frac{(\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)^{n}}{n!}\,.\end{split} ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​∑j≥01j!​(−N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)j​∑k,l≥0(k+l+j)!(k+l+2​j)!​(i​λ1​[u​λ~])k​(i​λ2​[u^​λ~])lk!​l!=𝖳a​∑j≥01j!​(−N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)j​∑n≥0(n+j)!(n+2​j)!​(i​p⋅x)nn!.\begin{split}\phi(x)&=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{(k+l+j)!}{(k+l+2j)!}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}[u\,\tilde{\lambda}])^{k}(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}[\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}])^{l}}{k!\,l!}\\
&=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{(n+j)!}{(n+2j)!}\frac{(\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)^{n}}{n!}\,.\end{split}

(7.19)


To get the second line, we’ve partially performed the  k,l k,lk,l sum using the binomial theorem and are left with an effective sum over the combination  n=k+l n=k+ln=k+l. The sum over  n nn yields confluent hypergeometric functions,





 \phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{(2j)!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}{}_{1}F_{1}(j+1,2j+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)\,. ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​∑j≥01(2​j)!​(−N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)j​F11​(j+1,2​j+1|i​p⋅x).\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{(2j)!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}{}_{1}F_{1}(j+1,2j+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)\,.

(7.20)


This is the form of the wavefunction that we will most commonly use for practical calculations. As  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0, only the  j=0 j=0j=0 term survives which reduces to a plane wave due to the identity  {}_{1}F_{1}(1,1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} F11​(1,1|i​p⋅x)=ei​p⋅x{}_{1}F_{1}(1,1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}.




As anticipated, our wavefunction has taken the form of a formal Taylor series in  N NN. But we can keep going and completely resum this series to find a result that is analytic in  N NN as well as the spinor-helicity variables. To do this, we first recast the confluent hypergeometric functions occurring in (7.20) in terms of spherical Bessel functions,





 {}_{1}F_{1}\!\left(j+1,\,2j+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(2j)!}{j!}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}}{(p\cdot x)^{j-1}}\left\{j_{j-1}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\mathrm{i}\,j_{j}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,. F11​(j+1, 2​j+1|i​p⋅x)=12​(2​j)!j!​ei​p⋅x2(p⋅x)j−1​{jj−1​(p⋅x2)+i​jj​(p⋅x2)}.{}_{1}F_{1}\!\left(j+1,\,2j+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p\cdot x\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(2j)!}{j!}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}}{(p\cdot x)^{j-1}}\left\{j_{j-1}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\mathrm{i}\,j_{j}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,.

(7.21)


This breaks (7.20) into a pair of sums,





 \phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left(S_{1}+\mathrm{i}\,S_{2}\right)\,. ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​ei​p⋅x2​(S1+i​S2).\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left(S_{1}+\mathrm{i}\,S_{2}\right)\,.

(7.22)


The first term in the brackets here is the sum





 S_{1}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{p\cdot x}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\,j_{j-1}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\,. S1=p⋅x2​∑j≥01j!​(−N​λ1​λ2p⋅x​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)j​jj−1​(p⋅x2).S_{1}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{p\cdot x}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\,j_{j-1}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\,.

(7.23)


Using the generating function of spherical Bessel functions,





 \frac{1}{y}\,\cos\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\,j_{j-1}(y)\,, 1y​cos⁡y2−2​y​t=∑j≥0tjj!​jj−1​(y),\frac{1}{y}\,\cos\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\,j_{j-1}(y)\,,

(7.24)


we can resum  S_{1} S1S_{1} to find a simple, closed-form expression





 S_{1}=\cos\sqrt{\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}}\,. S1=cos⁡(p⋅x)24+N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2.S_{1}=\cos\sqrt{\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}}\,.

(7.25)


The second term is the sum





 S_{2}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{p\cdot x}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\,j_{j}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\,. S2=p⋅x2​∑j≥01j!​(−N​λ1​λ2p⋅x​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)j​jj​(p⋅x2).S_{2}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}\biggl(-\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{p\cdot x}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{j}\,j_{j}\biggl(\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\,.

(7.26)


Differentiating (7.24) with respect to  t tt generates the identity





 \frac{\sin\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}}{\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\,j_{j}(y)\,. sin⁡y2−2​y​ty2−2​y​t=∑j≥0tjj!​jj​(y).\frac{\sin\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}}{\sqrt{y^{2}-2yt}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\,j_{j}(y)\,.

(7.27)


Using this, we can resum  S_{2} S2S_{2} to find





 S_{2}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\bigg(\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sin\sqrt{\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}}\;. S2=p⋅x2​((p⋅x)24+N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2)−12​sin⁡(p⋅x)24+N​λ1​λ2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2.S_{2}=\frac{p\cdot x}{2}\bigg(\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sin\sqrt{\frac{(p\cdot x)^{2}}{4}+N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}}\;.

(7.28)


Note that neither  S_{1} S1S_{1} nor  S_{2} S2S_{2} have branch cuts since both  \cos(\theta) cos⁡(θ)\cos(\theta) and  \sin(\theta)/\theta sin⁡(θ)/θ\sin(\theta)/\theta are even functions of  \theta θ\theta. So we can analytically continue to all values of the argument of the square root.




Putting (7.22), (7.25) and (7.28) together gives us our “quasi-momentum eigenstate” in closed form,





 \phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,, ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​ei​p⋅x2​{cos⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)+iψ​sin⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)},\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,,

(7.29)


where we have introduced the dressing factor





 \psi(x)=\sqrt{1+\frac{4N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{(p\cdot x)^{2}}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}} ψ⁡(x)=1+4​N​λ1​λ2(p⋅x)2​[u​λ~]​[u^​λ~]‖u‖2\psi(x)=\sqrt{1+\frac{4N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{(p\cdot x)^{2}}\frac{[u\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{u}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|u\|^{2}}}

(7.30)


as a convenient abbreviation. As  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0 or  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty, we see that  \psi\to 1 ψ→1\psi\to 1 and our state asymptotes to  \mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} 𝖳a​ei​p⋅x\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}. By substituting  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}})=(t\,\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}},\bar{t}\,\hat{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}}) xα​α˙=(uα˙,u^α˙)=(t​ζα˙,t¯​ζ^α˙)x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}})=(t\,\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}},\bar{t}\,\hat{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}}), where  \zeta^{\dot{\alpha}} ζα˙\zeta^{\dot{\alpha}} are homogeneous coordinates on  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}, it is easy to check that this solution is also finite in the  t\to 0 t→0t\to 0 limit,





 \lim_{t\to 0}\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\cos\sqrt{\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}[\zeta\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{\zeta}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|\zeta\|^{2}}}\,. limt→0ϕ⁡(x)=𝖳a​cos⁡N​λ1​λ2​[ζ​λ~]​[ζ^​λ~]‖ζ‖2.\lim_{t\to 0}\phi(x)=\mathsf{T}_{a}\cos\sqrt{\frac{N\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}[\zeta\,\tilde{\lambda}][\hat{\zeta}\,\tilde{\lambda}]}{\|\zeta\|^{2}}}\,.

(7.31)


That is, it extends to  \widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} ℂ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}, the blow-up of  \mathbb{C}^{2} ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} at the origin. The dressing (7.30) and wavefunction (7.29) are also invariant under the little group scalings (7.15), so we have found a 3-complex parameter family of solutions analogous to plane waves in flat space.




These are the states that we will scatter on Burns space. They are the asymptotically flat analogues of AdS bulk-to-boundary propagators. We will also be able to establish a dictionary matching these states to gauge invariant operators in our dual chiral algebra. That we find such a simple, closed-form solution of the wave equation is a testament to the simplicity that comes with working on self-dual backgrounds; see [104, 105, 35] for other recent examples of this phenomenon. In fact, it would be desirable to find a simpler, group theoretic derivation of (7.29), somewhat akin to Witten’s derivation of AdS bulk-to-boundary propagators from conformal inversions of power-law wavefunctions [44].




A puzzle regarding normalizable modes.




For completeness, let us also mention a curiosity associated to our solutions. Contrary to expectations, we have noticed that states like (7.29) are not the only solutions asymptotic to  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} ei​p⋅x\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}. One can find further solutions of the Burns space Laplace equation that are not captured by our analysis.




For example, we have discovered that (7.29) can actually be split into a linear combination of two “simpler” solutions,





 \phi=\phi_{+}+\phi_{-}\,. ϕ=ϕ++ϕ−.\phi=\phi_{+}+\phi_{-}\,.

(7.32)


 \phi_{\pm} ϕ±\phi_{\pm} are found by replacing the sines and cosines by exponentials,





 \displaystyle\phi_{+}(x) ϕ+​(x)\displaystyle\phi_{+}(x)
 \displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\frac{\psi+1}{2\psi}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(1+\psi)p\cdot x}\,, =𝖳a​ψ+12​ψ​ei2​(1+ψ)​p⋅x,\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\frac{\psi+1}{2\psi}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(1+\psi)p\cdot x}\,,

(7.33)



 \displaystyle\phi_{-}(x) ϕ−​(x)\displaystyle\phi_{-}(x)
 \displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\frac{\psi-1}{2\psi}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(1-\psi)p\cdot x}\,. =𝖳a​ψ−12​ψ​ei2​(1−ψ)​p⋅x.\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\frac{\psi-1}{2\psi}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(1-\psi)p\cdot x}\,.

(7.34)


It can be verified by direct calculation that both of these separately solve the Burns space Laplace equation.




The first mode  \phi_{+} ϕ+\phi_{+} asymptotes to  \mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} 𝖳a​ei​p⋅x\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} as  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty or  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0 and behaves like another Burns space analogue of a momentum eigenstate. But interestingly, the second solution  \phi_{-} ϕ−\phi_{-} decays to  0 00 as  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty or  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0. So we can take any solution  \phi ϕ\phi and add to it any amount of  \phi_{-} ϕ−\phi_{-} without affecting its asymptotic boundary conditions.




We will be unable to determine any operators dual to the  \phi_{\pm} ϕ±\phi_{\pm} modes in this work. The main reason for this is that unlike (7.29), these states acquire a branch cut in the argument of the square root that enters  \psi ψ\psi, thereby lacking analyticity and any clear twistorial interpretation. The branch cut only disappears in the combination  \phi_{+}+\phi_{-} ϕ++ϕ−\phi_{+}+\phi_{-}. It would be interesting if the  \phi_{-} ϕ−\phi_{-} states were analogous to the normalizable modes encountered in AdS, although we do not expect them to arise from twistor space. More generally, it would be very interesting to complete such early considerations into a full-fledged harmonic analysis of linearized field equations on Burns space.




Green’s function of the Laplacian.




There exists a beautiful trick to derive solutions and Green’s functions of conformally invariant equations on Burns space: simply map the equations to  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} using the conformal diffeomorphism  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} discussed in (3.53). This allows us to recycle much of the literature on solving such PDEs on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} and apply it to Burns space.




On any 4-manifold, the conformally covariant completion of the Laplacian is the Yamabe operator,





 \mathsf{Y}_{g}=\triangle-\frac{R}{6}\,, 𝖸g=△−R6,\mathsf{Y}_{g}=\triangle-\frac{R}{6}\,,

(7.35)


also known as the conformal Laplacian. It satisfies the conformal transformation law





 \mathsf{Y}_{f^{2}g}(f^{-1}\phi)=f^{-3}\mathsf{Y}_{g}\phi 𝖸f2​g​(f−1​ϕ)=f−3​𝖸g​ϕ\mathsf{Y}_{f^{2}g}(f^{-1}\phi)=f^{-3}\mathsf{Y}_{g}\phi

(7.36)


for all positive conformal rescalings  g\mapsto f^{2}g g↦f2​gg\mapsto f^{2}g. On a scalar-flat geometry like the Burns metric, the Yamabe operator coincides with the ordinary Laplacian. On  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}, the scalar curvature of the Fubini-Study metric is  R=24 R=24R=24, so the Yamabe operator becomes  \triangle-4 △−4\triangle-4. Hence, solutions of  \triangle\phi=0 △​ϕ=0\triangle\phi=0 on Burns space can be found by instead solving  (\triangle-4)\phi=0 (△−4)​ϕ=0(\triangle-4)\phi=0 on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}.




Denote the Burns and Fubini-Study metrics by  g_{\text{B}} gBg_{\text{B}} and  g_{\text{FS}} gFSg_{\text{FS}} respectively. We saw in (3.53) that the inversion diffeomorphism  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} from  \widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} ℂ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} to  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} minus a point induces the conformal equivalence





 g_{\text{B}}=f^{2}g_{\text{FS}}\,,\qquad f=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2}) gB=f2​gFS,f=2​N​(1+‖u‖−2)g_{\text{B}}=f^{2}g_{\text{FS}}\,,\qquad f=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2})

(7.37)


where  f ff has been expressed in the  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} coordinates obtained after the inversion. These are affine coordinates on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}, in which the Fubini-Study metric reads





 g_{\text{FS}}=\frac{\|\mathrm{d}u\|^{2}+|[u\,\mathrm{d}u]|^{2}}{(1+\|u\|^{2})^{2}}\,. gFS=‖d​u‖2+|[u​d​u]|2(1+‖u‖2)2.g_{\text{FS}}=\frac{\|\mathrm{d}u\|^{2}+|[u\,\mathrm{d}u]|^{2}}{(1+\|u\|^{2})^{2}}\,.

(7.38)


A systematic procedure to construct Green’s functions of wave equations on projective spaces is described in [106]. The main idea is to express the Green’s function in terms of the geodesic distance. Let us review this briefly for the case of the Yamabe operator.




Let  u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}},u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} u1α˙,u2α˙u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}},u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} be two points on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}. Using the Fubini-Study metric, the geodesic distance between them is calculated to be





 \text{dist}(u_{1},u_{2})=\cos^{-1}\sqrt{L}\,, dist​(u1,u2)=cos−1⁡L,\text{dist}(u_{1},u_{2})=\cos^{-1}\sqrt{L}\,,

(7.39)


expressed in terms of the quantity





 L=\frac{|1+\bar{u}^{1}_{1}u_{2}^{\dot{1}}+\bar{u}_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{\dot{2}}|^{2}}{(1+\|u_{1}\|^{2})(1+\|u_{2}\|^{2})}=\frac{(1+[\hat{u}_{1}u_{2}])(1+[\hat{u}_{2}u_{1}])}{(1+\|u_{1}\|^{2})(1+\|u_{2}\|^{2})} L=|1+u¯11​u21˙+u¯12​u22˙|2(1+‖u1‖2)​(1+‖u2‖2)=(1+[u^1​u2])​(1+[u^2​u1])(1+‖u1‖2)​(1+‖u2‖2)L=\frac{|1+\bar{u}^{1}_{1}u_{2}^{\dot{1}}+\bar{u}_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{\dot{2}}|^{2}}{(1+\|u_{1}\|^{2})(1+\|u_{2}\|^{2})}=\frac{(1+[\hat{u}_{1}u_{2}])(1+[\hat{u}_{2}u_{1}])}{(1+\|u_{1}\|^{2})(1+\|u_{2}\|^{2})}

(7.40)


where  \hat{u}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}}=(-\bar{u}_{i}^{2},\bar{u}_{i}^{1}) u^iα˙=(−u¯i2,u¯i1)\hat{u}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}}=(-\bar{u}_{i}^{2},\bar{u}_{i}^{1}) as before. Motivated by this, if we use the ansatz





 G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})\equiv\mathcal{G}(L) GFS​(u1,u2)≡𝒢⁡(L)G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})\equiv\mathcal{G}(L)

(7.41)


for the Green’s function, then away from  u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} u1α˙=u2α˙u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} the conformally coupled Laplace equation  (\triangle_{u_{1}}-4)G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=0 (△u1−4)​GFS​(u1,u2)=0(\triangle_{u_{1}}-4)G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=0 reduces to an ODE,





 L(L-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{G}}{\mathrm{d}L^{2}}+(3L-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}}{\mathrm{d}L}+\mathcal{G}=0\,. L⁡(L−1)​d2​𝒢d​L2+(3​L−1)​d​𝒢d​L+𝒢=0.L(L-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{G}}{\mathrm{d}L^{2}}+(3L-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}}{\mathrm{d}L}+\mathcal{G}=0\,.

(7.42)


This has the general solution





 \mathcal{G}(L)=\frac{C_{1}}{1-L}+\frac{C_{2}\log L}{1-L}\,, 𝒢⁡(L)=C11−L+C2​log⁡L1−L,\mathcal{G}(L)=\frac{C_{1}}{1-L}+\frac{C_{2}\log L}{1-L}\,,

(7.43)


with  C_{1},C_{2} C1,C2C_{1},C_{2} being constants of integration.




The only singularity that a genuine Green’s function can have is the short distance singularity at  L=1 L=1L=1 when  u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} u1α˙=u2α˙u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}}. So we drop the logarithmic solution which is non-singular at  L=1 L=1L=1 but has an unphysical singularity at  L=0 L=0L=0. Choosing the normalization  C_{1}=-1/4\pi^{2} C1=−1/4π2C_{1}=-1/4\pi^{2} then ensures that it solves  (\triangle_{u_{1}}-4)G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=|g_{\text{FS}}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{4}(u_{1}-u_{2}) (△u1−4)​GFS​(u1,u2)=|gFS|−12​δ4​(u1−u2)(\triangle_{u_{1}}-4)G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=|g_{\text{FS}}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{4}(u_{1}-u_{2}). This gives us the Green’s function of the Yamabe operator associated to the Fubini-Study metric,





 G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1-L}\,. GFS​(u1,u2)=−14​π2​11−L.G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1-L}\,.

(7.44)


This was originally found in [74] and used to study transition amplitudes of conformally coupled scalars traversing through spacetime foam built from  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} vacuum bubbles. Due to conformal covariance, this happens to be very closely related to scattering of ordinary scalars on Burns space.




To return to Burns space, one simply dresses the conformally coupled scalar with the conformal factor  f(u)=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2}) f⁡(u)=2​N​(1+‖u‖−2)f(u)=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2}) in accordance with the transformation law (7.36). Following this, one undoes the conformal diffeomorphism. Being an inversion, the inverse of this diffeomorphism is again  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2}. So, to obtain the Green’s function of the Laplacian on Burns space, we start with the conformally rescaled scalar solution





 \frac{G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})}{f(u_{1})f(u_{2})} GFS​(u1,u2)f⁡(u1)​f​(u2)\frac{G_{\text{FS}}(u_{1},u_{2})}{f(u_{1})f(u_{2})}

(7.45)


and map  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2}. Dressing the result with a color-trace, we land on the propagator for the adjoint-valued scalar  \phi ϕ\phi on Burns space,





 G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b})}{8\pi^{2}}\left(\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|^{2}+\frac{N|[u_{1}u_{2}]|^{2}}{\|u_{1}\|^{2}\|u_{2}\|^{2}}\right)^{-1}\,, Ga​b​(u1,u2)=−tr⁡(𝖳a​𝖳b)8​π2​(‖u1−u2‖2+N​|[u1​u2]|2‖u1‖2​‖u2‖2)−1,G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b})}{8\pi^{2}}\left(\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|^{2}+\frac{N|[u_{1}u_{2}]|^{2}}{\|u_{1}\|^{2}\|u_{2}\|^{2}}\right)^{-1}\,,

(7.46)


where the factor of  |[u_{1}u_{2}]|^{2}=[u_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{1}\hat{u}_{2}] |[u1​u2]|2=[u1​u2]​[u^1​u^2]|[u_{1}u_{2}]|^{2}=[u_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{1}\hat{u}_{2}] has been obtained by writing  \|u_{i}\|^{2}=[\hat{u}_{i}u_{i}] ‖ui‖2=[u^i​ui]\|u_{i}\|^{2}=[\hat{u}_{i}u_{i}] and applying Schouten’s identity  [\hat{u}_{1}u_{1}][\hat{u}_{2}u_{2}]-[\hat{u}_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{2}u_{1}]=[u_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{1}\hat{u}_{2}] [u^1​u1]​[u^2​u2]−[u^1​u2]​[u^2​u1]=[u1​u2]​[u^1​u^2][\hat{u}_{1}u_{1}][\hat{u}_{2}u_{2}]-[\hat{u}_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{2}u_{1}]=[u_{1}u_{2}][\hat{u}_{1}\hat{u}_{2}].




In the limit  u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}\to u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} u1α˙→u2α˙u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}\to u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}}, (7.46) has the expected short distance singularity





 G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})\sim-\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b})}{4\pi^{2}}\,\bigl(g_{\mu\nu}(x_{2})x_{12}^{\mu}x_{12}^{\nu}\bigr)^{-1}+\text{non-singular in }x_{12}^{\mu}\,, Ga​b​(u1,u2)∼−tr⁡(𝖳a​𝖳b)4​π2​(gμ​ν​(x2)​x12μ​x12ν)−1+non-singular in ​x12μ,G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})\sim-\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b})}{4\pi^{2}}\,\bigl(g_{\mu\nu}(x_{2})x_{12}^{\mu}x_{12}^{\nu}\bigr)^{-1}+\text{non-singular in }x_{12}^{\mu}\,,

(7.47)


where  g gg now denotes the Burns metric,  x_{12}^{\mu}\vcentcolon=x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu} x12μ:=x1μ−x2μx_{12}^{\mu}\vcentcolon=x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu}, and we have reinstated the original double null coordinates  x_{i}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}}) xiα​α˙=(uiα˙,u^iα˙)x_{i}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}}) using (2.6). Since it solves the Laplace equation everywhere other than  u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}} u1α˙=u2α˙u_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}}=u_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}}, this boundary condition is then sufficient to conclude that it solves





 \triangle_{u_{1}}G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\,\delta^{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b}) △u1​Ga​b​(u1,u2)=1|g|​δ4​(x1−x2)​tr​(𝖳a​𝖳b)\triangle_{u_{1}}G_{ab}(u_{1},u_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\,\delta^{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a}\mathsf{T}_{b})

(7.48)


on Burns space, where the metric determinant can be evaluated at either point as usual.






7.2 States of Mabuchi gravity




Similarly, we can determine the scattering states of our gravitational sector. The linearized field equation of the Kähler scalar perturbation  \rho ρ\rho follows from varying just the quadratic terms in (2.49),





 \triangle^{2}\rho-\frac{8\mathrm{i}P\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho}{\omega^{2}}=0\,. △2​ρ−8​i​P∧∂∂¯​ρω2=0.\triangle^{2}\rho-\frac{8\mathrm{i}P\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\rho}{\omega^{2}}=0\,.

(7.49)


This involves the Paneitz operator: a conformally covariant fourth order operator which reduces to the squared Laplacian on  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} [107]; see [108] for a review. On a general 4-manifold with Riemannian metric  g gg, it can be expressed in terms of the Laplacian  \triangle △\triangle, the Levi-Civita connection  \nabla_{\mu} ∇μ\nabla_{\mu}, and the Ricci and scalar curvatures  R_{\mu\nu} Rμ​νR_{\mu\nu},  R RR as





 \mathsf{P}_{g}=\triangle^{2}+2\,\nabla_{\mu}\biggl(R^{\mu\nu}-\frac{R}{3}\,g^{\mu\nu}\biggr)\nabla_{\nu}\,. 𝖯g=△2+2​∇μ(Rμ​ν−R3​gμ​ν)​∇ν.\mathsf{P}_{g}=\triangle^{2}+2\,\nabla_{\mu}\biggl(R^{\mu\nu}-\frac{R}{3}\,g^{\mu\nu}\biggr)\nabla_{\nu}\,.

(7.50)


Under a conformal transformation  g\mapsto f^{2}g g↦f2​gg\mapsto f^{2}g, it obeys
