 \mathsf{P}_{f^{2}g}\rho=f^{-4}\mathsf{P}_{g}\rho\,. 𝖯f2​g​ρ=f−4​𝖯g​ρ.\mathsf{P}_{f^{2}g}\rho=f^{-4}\mathsf{P}_{g}\rho\,.

(7.51)


The linearized equation (7.49) is precisely the conformally invariant equation





 \mathsf{P}_{g}\rho=0 𝖯g​ρ=0\mathsf{P}_{g}\rho=0

(7.52)


specialized to a scalar-flat Kähler 4-manifold with Kähler form  \omega ω\omega, Ricci form  P PP and Laplacian  \triangle △\triangle.



Wavefunctions for Kähler perturbations.




In flat space, (7.49) reduces to  \triangle_{0}^{2}\rho=0 △02​ρ=0\triangle_{0}^{2}\rho=0. This is trivially solved by the usual momentum eigenstate  \rho=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} ρ=ei​p⋅x\rho=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} when  p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} pα​α˙p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} is null. But, being a fourth order equation, it also admits an additional solution  \rho=x^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x} ρ=x2​ei​p⋅x\rho=x^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}. Our gravitational modes will be described by the analogous solutions on Burns space.




The procedure for solving (7.49) is technically identical to the case of the Laplace equation dealt with in the previous section. The Laplacian on Burns space was displayed in (7.2), while the Kähler and Ricci forms can be found in (3.36) and (3.44) respectively. But this presentation of the linearized field equation is somewhat cumbersome to work with. In fact, it will prove much wiser to start with the Paneitz operator on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} and conformally transform it back to Burns space to obtain a more tractable version of (7.49).




Since the Fubini-Study metric (7.38) is Einstein, its Ricci curvature is pure trace





 R^{\mu\nu}=6g_{\text{FS}}^{\mu\nu}\,. Rμ​ν=6​gFSμ​ν.R^{\mu\nu}=6g_{\text{FS}}^{\mu\nu}\,.

(7.53)


As mentioned before, the corresponding scalar curvature is  R=24 R=24R=24. Therefore, the Paneitz operator on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} is very simple and can be expressed in terms of its Yamabe operator,






 \displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{FS}}}=\triangle^{2}-4\triangle=(\triangle-4)(\triangle-4+4) 𝖯gFS=△2−4​△=(△−4)​(△−4+4)\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{FS}}}=\triangle^{2}-4\triangle=(\triangle-4)(\triangle-4+4)





 \displaystyle\implies\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{FS}}}=\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{FS}}}\bigl(\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{FS}}}+4\bigr)\,. ⟹𝖯gFS=𝖸gFS​(𝖸gFS+4).\displaystyle\implies\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{FS}}}=\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{FS}}}\bigl(\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{FS}}}+4\bigr)\,.

(7.54)


Following (7.37), denote the conformal equivalence between the Burns and Fubini-Study metrics by  g_{\text{B}}=f^{2}g_{\text{FS}} gB=f2​gFSg_{\text{B}}=f^{2}g_{\text{FS}}. In the coordinates  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}, we had  f=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2}) f=2​N​(1+‖u‖−2)f=\sqrt{2N}(1+\|u\|^{-2}). Under the inverse map  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} uα˙↦N​uα˙/‖u‖2u^{\dot{\alpha}}\mapsto\sqrt{N}u^{\dot{\alpha}}/\|u\|^{2} from  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} to Burns space, this maps to





 f=\sqrt{2N}\left(1+\frac{\|u\|^{2}}{N}\right)\,. f=2​N​(1+‖u‖2N).f=\sqrt{2N}\left(1+\frac{\|u\|^{2}}{N}\right)\,.

(7.55)


Next, the left hand side of (7.54) can be mapped to Burns space using the conformal transformation law for the Paneitz operator, equation (7.51), whereas its right hand side can be independently transformed using the transformation law of the Yamabe operator, equation (7.36). This leads to the identity





 f^{4}\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{B}}}\rho=f^{3}\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{B}}}\!\left\{f^{-1}\cdot f^{3}\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{B}}}(f^{-1}\rho)+4f^{-1}\rho\right\}\,. f4​𝖯gB​ρ=f3​𝖸gB​{f−1⋅f3​𝖸gB​(f−1​ρ)+4​f−1​ρ}.f^{4}\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{B}}}\rho=f^{3}\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{B}}}\!\left\{f^{-1}\cdot f^{3}\mathsf{Y}_{g_{\text{B}}}(f^{-1}\rho)+4f^{-1}\rho\right\}\,.

(7.56)


Since the Yamabe operator on Burns space is just the Laplacian, this produces a simpler, factorized expression for the free field equation (7.49):





 \mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{B}}}\rho=\frac{1}{f}\,\triangle\bigg[f^{2}\triangle\bigg(\frac{\rho}{f}\bigg)+\frac{4\rho}{f}\bigg]=0 𝖯gB​ρ=1f​△​[f2​△​(ρf)+4​ρf]=0\mathsf{P}_{g_{\text{B}}}\rho=\frac{1}{f}\,\triangle\bigg[f^{2}\triangle\bigg(\frac{\rho}{f}\bigg)+\frac{4\rho}{f}\bigg]=0

(7.57)


with  f ff given by (7.55). Of course, (7.57) can also be directly verified to match (7.49).




Generating solutions to this is now straightforward. We rewrite (7.57) as a pair of second order PDEs in terms of  \rho ρ\rho and an auxiliary scalar field  \tilde{\rho} ρ~\tilde{\rho},






 \displaystyle f^{2}\triangle\bigg(\frac{\rho}{f}\bigg)+\frac{4\rho}{f}=\tilde{\rho}\,, f2​△​(ρf)+4​ρf=ρ~,\displaystyle f^{2}\triangle\bigg(\frac{\rho}{f}\bigg)+\frac{4\rho}{f}=\tilde{\rho}\,,

(7.58)




 \displaystyle\triangle\tilde{\rho}=0\,. △​ρ~=0.\displaystyle\triangle\tilde{\rho}=0\,.

(7.59)


To find quasi-momentum eigenstates for  \rho ρ\rho, it is natural to take  \tilde{\rho} ρ~\tilde{\rho} to be the solution of the Laplace equation discovered in (7.29),





 \tilde{\rho}(x)=4\sqrt{2N}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,, ρ~​(x)=4​2​N​ei​p⋅x2​{cos⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)+iψ​sin⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)},\tilde{\rho}(x)=4\sqrt{2N}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,,

(7.60)


having dressed it with a judicious normalization while dropping the color factor  \mathsf{T}_{a} 𝖳a\mathsf{T}_{a}. Plugging this into the second order PDE (7.58) should give rise to a pair of solutions for  \rho ρ\rho. At least one of these can be found trivially: if  \tilde{\rho} ρ~\tilde{\rho} solves the Laplace equation (7.59), then the profile  \rho=f\tilde{\rho}/4 ρ=f​ρ~/4\rho=f\tilde{\rho}/4 automatically solves (7.58). This shows that

8 The holographic dictionary on spacetime




Precision tests of any holographic duality require a holographic dictionary: a map from states of the bulk theory to operators of the boundary dual. Once such a map is set up, holography tells us that scattering amplitudes of bulk states equal correlators of the boundary operators. In the previous sections, we have described both the gauge invariant local operators of our chiral algebra in the large  N NN limit, as well as wavefunctions of gluon and Kähler perturbations on Burns space. In this section, we will link them with a holographic dictionary.




8.1 Open string modes




On one hand, string states of the B-model on twistor space descend to states of the WZW4 model and Mabuchi gravity on spacetime. On the other hand, holography relates them to chiral algebra operators. Combining these facts, we can associate spacetime states with their dual operators.




Classically, the group-valued field  \mathsf{g}=\mathrm{e}^{\phi} 𝗀=eϕ\mathsf{g}=\mathrm{e}^{\phi} of the WZW4 model encodes the gauge field  A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1} A=−∂¯​𝗀​𝗀−1A=-\bar{\partial}\mathsf{g}\,\mathsf{g}^{-1} on spacetime. In linear theory, scalar wavefunctions for  \phi ϕ\phi give rise to positive helicity gluon wavefunctions on Burns space,  A=-\bar{\partial}\phi+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{2}) A=−∂¯​ϕ+O⁡(ϕ2)A=-\bar{\partial}\phi+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{2}). In the flat space limit  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0, they should reduce to the positive helicity gluon momentum eigenstates





 A=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\lambda_{1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,\mathsf{T}_{a} A=−i​λ~α˙​d​u^α˙λ1​ei​p⋅x​𝖳aA=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}{\lambda_{1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,\mathsf{T}_{a}

(8.1)


where  p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} pα​α˙=λα​λ~α˙p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} is a flat space null momentum, and  \mathsf{T}_{a} 𝖳a\mathsf{T}_{a} is a color factor as before. Such a state scales with weight  -2 −2-2 under little group scalings (7.15), as desired from positive helicity gluons. The self-dual part of its linearized field strength is canonically normalized,





 \tilde{F}_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\partial_{\alpha(\dot{\alpha}}A^{\alpha}{}_{\dot{\beta})}=\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,\mathsf{T}_{a}\,. F~α˙​β˙=∂α⁡(α˙CLOSEAαOPENβ˙)=λ~α˙λ~β˙ei​p⋅x𝖳a.\tilde{F}_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\partial_{\alpha(\dot{\alpha}}A^{\alpha}{}_{\dot{\beta})}=\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\beta}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,\mathsf{T}_{a}\,.

(8.2)


The flat space solution of  \triangle_{0}\phi=0 △0​ϕ=0\triangle_{0}\phi=0 that gives rise to this spin 1 wavefunction via  A=-\bar{\partial}\phi A=−∂¯​ϕA=-\bar{\partial}\phi has the profile

(8.3)


Therefore, on Burns space, we find it natural to normalize the quasi-momentum eigenstates such that they reduce to (8.3) as  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0 or  \|u\|\to\infty ‖u‖→∞\|u\|\to\infty:





 \phi(x)=\phi_{a}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\equiv\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,, ϕ⁡(x)=ϕa​(x|z,λ~)≡𝖳a​ei​p⋅x2λ1​λ2​{cos⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)+iψ​sin⁡(ψ​p⋅x2)},\phi(x)=\phi_{a}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\equiv\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}{2}}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\left\{\cos\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\psi}\,\sin\biggl(\frac{\psi\,p\cdot x}{2}\biggr)\right\}\,,

(8.4)


where the dressing factor  \psi ψ\psi was written out in (7.30). Just as for  A AA, these states for  \phi ϕ\phi (in flat as well as Burns space) scale with little group weight  -2 −2-2, which provides a useful check of many amplitude calculations.




For our holographic dictionary, we propose to associate the gauge invariant currents  J_{rs}[k,l](z) Jr​s​[k,l]​(z)J_{rs}[k,l](z) displayed in (4.17) to elements in a soft expansion of (8.4). It is easiest to describe this by fixing the little group freedom to set





 \lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z)\,,\qquad\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\omega(1,\bar{z})\,. λα=(1,z),λ~α˙=ω⁡(1,z¯).\lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z)\,,\qquad\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\omega(1,\bar{z})\,.

A soft expansion of (8.4) is a Taylor expansion in  \omega,\bar{z} ω,z¯\omega,\bar{z} of the form3131

 \begin{split}\phi_{a}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})&=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\omega^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p}\frac{\bar{z}^{p-k}}{k!(p-k)!}\,\phi[k,p-k](x|z)\,\mathsf{T}_{a}\\

These give a taste of how positive helicity soft gluons get deformed when backreaction is turned on. Twisted holography again ensures that the deformations of such soft modes are polynomial in  N NN. It is also curious to note that deformations only start from the sub-subleading order. The leading and subleading soft gluon wavefunctions receive no backreaction!




Trading adjoint indices  a aa for composite indices  rs r​srs of the antisymmetric square of the fundamental of  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8), we observe that the currents  J_{rs}(z)=I_{r}(X^{\dot{1}})^{(k}(X^{\dot{2}})^{l)}I_{s} Jr​s​(z)=Ir​(X1˙)(kCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENl)​IsJ_{rs}(z)=I_{r}(X^{\dot{1}})^{(k}(X^{\dot{2}})^{l)}I_{s} are in one-to-one correspondence with these soft gluon wavefunctions. It then seems natural to identify the holographic dictionary to be





 \phi[k,l](x|z)\,\mathsf{T}_{rs}\quad\overset{?}{\longleftrightarrow}\quad J_{rs}[k,l](z)\,. ϕ⁡[k,l]​(x|z)​𝖳r​s⟷?Jr​s​[k,l]​(z).\phi[k,l](x|z)\,\mathsf{T}_{rs}\quad\overset{?}{\longleftrightarrow}\quad J_{rs}[k,l](z)\,.

(8.8)


This happens to be a bit naive, since we are allowing the currents on the right to have poles of order up to  k+l+1 k+l+1k+l+1 in a general 2d vacuum, whereas the wavefunctions on the left clearly only have simple poles in  z zz. The latter are displayed in (8.7).




A simple way to account for this is to cancel the poles on the right by multiplying with a factor of  z^{k+l} zk+lz^{k+l}. This leads us to propose the dictionary3232

 \begin{split}J_{rs}(z,\tilde{\lambda})&=\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{1}}{}^{k}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{2}}{}^{l}}{k!\,l!}\,z^{k+l}\,J_{rs}[k,l](z)\\

The soft gluon dictionary then implies that this operator must be dual to the hard gluon wavefunction in the bulk:





 \phi_{rs}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad J_{rs}(z,\tilde{\lambda})\,. ϕr​s(x|z,λ~)⟷Jr​s(z,λ~).\phi_{rs}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad J_{rs}(z,\tilde{\lambda})\,.

This dictionary will identify scattering amplitudes of hard states on the left with correlators of the operators on the right in the large  N NN limit.




We expect that a very similar dictionary will continue to hold also at finite  N NN, although the basis of gauge invariant operators at finite  N NN may need to be reevaluated to account for trace relations.




We also remark that what we have called soft gluon currents  J_{rs}[k,l] Jr​s​[k,l]J_{rs}[k,l] are not immediately the same as the  S SS-algebra currents studied by Strominger [5]. There is an important distinction to be made with regards their conformal weights: the  S SS-algebra currents have mostly negative conformal weights (see also [33]), whereas the  J[k,l] J⁡[k,l]J[k,l] have positive weights. In our CCFT dual, the currents  J[k,l]=I(X^{\dot{1}})^{(k}(X^{\dot{2}})^{l)}I J⁡[k,l]=I​(X1˙)(kCLOSE​(X2˙)OPENl)​IJ[k,l]=I(X^{\dot{1}})^{(k}(X^{\dot{2}})^{l)}I have weights  h=\frac{1}{2}(k+l+2) h=12​(k+l+2)h=\frac{1}{2}(k+l+2). In the presence of our defects, these can be mapped to currents  z^{k+l+1}J[k,l] zk+l+1​J​[k,l]z^{k+l+1}J[k,l] that transform as primaries of weight  h=-\frac{1}{2}(k+l+2) h=−12​(k+l+2)h=-\frac{1}{2}(k+l+2) under the defect conformal group  z\mapsto az z↦a​zz\mapsto az,  a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times} a∈ℂ×a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}. It is these currents that enter the soft expansion of the hard modes, and it is these currents that can genuinely be identified as Burns space analogues of the  S SS-algebra currents by a simple relabeling:





 S^{p}_{m}(z)=z^{2p-1}J[p-1+m,p-1-m](z)\,,\qquad p\in\left\{1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots\right\}\,,\;|m|\leq p-1\,. Smp​(z)=z2​p−1​J​[p−1+m,p−1−m]​(z),p∈{1,32,2,⋯},|m|≤p−1.S^{p}_{m}(z)=z^{2p-1}J[p-1+m,p-1-m](z)\,,\qquad p\in\left\{1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots\right\}\,,\;|m|\leq p-1\,.

(8.12)


The presence of the defects means that the OPEs of  S^{p}_{m} SmpS^{p}_{m} can now contain increasingly singular terms in  z_{ij} zi​jz_{ij}, as long as we compensate for the scaling dimension by multiplying with appropriate factors of  z_{i},z_{j} zi,zjz_{i},z_{j} in the numerator. In particular, the chiral algebra of our currents will be able to display interesting central terms.





8.2 Closed string modes




In previous sections, we have also determined the wavefunctions for Kähler perturbations of Mabuchi gravity. Their dual operators can be built out of the  E[k,l] E⁡[k,l]E[k,l] and  F[k,l] F⁡[k,l]F[k,l] currents of equation (4.17).




We choose to describe this dictionary in the case where we do not set the center of mass modes  E[1,0]=\text{Tr}\,X^{\dot{1}} E⁡[1,0]=Tr​X1˙E[1,0]=\text{Tr}\,X^{\dot{1}} and  E[0,1]=\text{Tr}\,X^{\dot{2}} E⁡[0,1]=Tr​X2˙E[0,1]=\text{Tr}\,X^{\dot{2}} to zero. As discussed below equation (4.2), this is analogous to working with  \mathrm{U}(N) U⁡(N)\mathrm{U}(N) rather than  \mathrm{SU}(N) SU⁡(N)\mathrm{SU}(N) gauge group in Maldacena’s AdS5/CFT4 duality. Doing this will allow us to match three point functions like  \langle E[1,0]J[0,1]J[0,0]\rangle ⟨E⁡[1,0]​J​[0,1]​J​[0,0]⟩\langle E[1,0]J[0,1]J[0,0]\rangle, etc. to 4d amplitudes, thereby providing the simplest test of the gravitational sector of our duality in section 9.3.




On  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, positive helicity metric perturbations in gravity are conventionally given little group weight  -4 −4-4. The associated Kähler potential perturbations must then also have the same little group weight. For example, the Kähler potential perturbation around flat space,





 \rho=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}}\,, ρ=ei​p⋅xλ12​λ22,\rho=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}}\,,

(8.13)


solves  \triangle_{0}^{2}\rho=0 △02​ρ=0\triangle_{0}^{2}\rho=0 (and in fact  \triangle_{0}\rho=0 △0​ρ=0\triangle_{0}\rho=0) and gives rise to a standard positive helicity graviton wavefunction on  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4},





 h=2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}=-\frac{2\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\,[\tilde{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}u][\tilde{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}]\,. h=2​∂2ρ∂uα˙​∂u^β˙​d​uα˙​d​u^β˙=−2​ei​p⋅xλ1​λ2​[λ~​d​u]​[λ~​d​u^].h=2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}=-\frac{2\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\,[\tilde{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}u][\tilde{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}]\,.

(8.14)

We associate the currents  E[k,l] E⁡[k,l]E[k,l] to soft modes of  \rho^{E} ρE\rho^{E}, and  F[k,l] F⁡[k,l]F[k,l] to the soft modes of  \rho^{F} ρF\rho^{F}. Since  E[k,l] E⁡[k,l]E[k,l] can have poles of order  k+l k+lk+l, while  \rho^{E} ρE\rho^{E} by construction only has a second order pole in  z zz, we postulate that  z^{k+l-2}E[k,l] zk+l−2​E​[k,l]z^{k+l-2}E[k,l] are dual to soft modes of  \rho^{E} ρE\rho^{E}. The operators dual to the corresponding hard states are then built from summing the soft operators:





 \begin{split}E(z,\tilde{\lambda})&=-\frac{1}{z^{2}}+\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{1}}{}^{k}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{2}}{}^{l}}{k!\,l!}\,z^{k+l-2}E[k,l](z)\\

 \displaystyle\rho^{E}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad E(z,\tilde{\lambda})\,, ρE(x|z,λ~)⟷E(z,λ~),\displaystyle\rho^{E}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda})\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad E(z,\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(8.20)


i.e., correlators of the gravitational operators on the right should reproduce amplitudes of Mabuchi gravity on Burns space.




In section 9.3, we will content ourselves with testing this dictionary by computing an amplitude involving two gluons and one  \rho^{E} ρE\rho^{E} Kähler perturbation. The dictionary for  \rho^{F} ρF\rho^{F} is also a conjecture that we find natural, but tests of our duality that involve the  \rho^{F} ρF\rho^{F} states are left to another occasion.






9 Tests of the duality




Past work on celestial holography has produced a treasure trove of bottom-up correspondences between perturbative QFT on 4d asymptotically flat spacetimes and correlators of putative 2d CFTs. With our top-down model of holography on Burns space, we have completed this into a full-fledged duality by providing independent definitions of the bulk and boundary theories. In the sections that follow, we show that our proposed dual chiral algebra explicitly reproduces some simple low-lying amplitudes of WZW4 + Mabuchi gravity on Burns space.




Unlike in flat space, these amplitudes are non-vanishing on Burns space. They vanish on flat space because momentum eigenstates on flat space uplift to states on twistor space that are localized on different fibers of  \mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} 𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)→ℂ​ℙ1\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} (see [109] for the explicit form of such states). Because they are separated in the  z zz-plane, they cannot interact with each other through the local interactions present in the holomorphic Chern-Simons or BCOV twistor actions. In contrast, the twistor space of Burns space no longer fibers over  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}, and the states will scatter non-trivially for generic kinematics.




We begin by computing examples of 2-gluon tree amplitudes in WZW4. These give rise to a central extension for the celestial chiral algebra [4, 5, 33] of positive helicity gluons on Burns space. We also compute collinear limits of 3-gluon amplitudes at leading and subleading order in the brane backreaction, and show that they match the OPE coefficients of the dual chiral algebra.3333
            33
            
            
            
          A direct calculation of the 3-gluon tree amplitude of WZW4 on Burns space to leading order in the backreaction can also be found in the supplemental material to
[36]. We will not repeat it here, but its collinear limit agrees with the OPE that we report. A similar calculation is also performed for the 3-point scattering of two gluons and one gravitational  E EE-mode to leading order.




9.1 2-point amplitudes in WZW4




Scattering amplitudes are the primary physical observables of perturbative gauge theory and gravity in asymptotically flat spacetimes. It is well-known that in Minkowski space, tree level amplitudes of massless particles are analytic functions of spinor-helicity variables. So they can be analytically continued to split signature, where the spinor-helicity variables  \lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} λα,λ~α˙\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}} can be real and independent, or to Euclidean signature where they are necessarily complex due to the absence of real null momenta. As we are working on a Euclidean signature, self-dual spacetime, we will define scattering amplitudes on Burns space via similar analytic continuation.




A preliminary understanding of amplitudes on Euclidean signature instanton backgrounds has been developed in [110, 32, 74]. Alternatively, one can think of our Euclidean signature amplitudes as the analogue of holographic correlators computed by summing Witten diagrams in Euclidean AdS3, which is how such observables were originally computed in earlier examples of twisted holography [16, 17].



Boundary computation of 2-point amplitude.




The first test that we present is a match between the 2-point amplitude of the WZW4 model on Burns space and a 2-point correlator in the boundary dual. Before we dive into the computation on Burns space, let us start by describing the calculation in the dual chiral algebra. This happens to be drastically simpler than the calculation in the bulk because the boundary dual is the BRST reduction of a free theory.




Let us work with the standard basis of  \mathfrak{so}(k) 𝔰​𝔬​(k)\mathfrak{so}(k) provided by the antisymmetric matrices





 (\mathsf{T}_{pq})_{e}{}^{f}=\delta_{pe}\delta_{q}{}^{f}-\delta_{p}{}^{f}\delta_{qe}\,,\qquad p,q,e,f\in\{1,\dots,k\}\,. (𝖳p​q)e=fδp​eδq−fδpδq​ef,p,q,e,f∈{1,…,k}.(\mathsf{T}_{pq})_{e}{}^{f}=\delta_{pe}\delta_{q}{}^{f}-\delta_{p}{}^{f}\delta_{qe}\,,\qquad p,q,e,f\in\{1,\dots,k\}\,.

(9.1)


For us, the case of interest is  k=8 k=8k=8. But since we are working in the planar limit, our calculations will be agnostic to the value of  k kk. The  \mathfrak{so}(k) 𝔰​𝔬​(k)\mathfrak{so}(k) algebra is given by





 [\mathsf{T}_{pq},\mathsf{T}_{rs}]\equiv f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}\mathsf{T}_{tu}=\delta_{qr}\mathsf{T}_{ps}-\delta_{pr}\mathsf{T}_{qs}-\delta_{qs}\mathsf{T}_{pr}+\delta_{ps}\mathsf{T}_{qr}\,, [𝖳p​q,𝖳r​s]≡fp​q,r​s​𝖳t​ut​u=δq​r​𝖳p​s−δp​r​𝖳q​s−δq​s​𝖳p​r+δp​s​𝖳q​r,[\mathsf{T}_{pq},\mathsf{T}_{rs}]\equiv f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}\mathsf{T}_{tu}=\delta_{qr}\mathsf{T}_{ps}-\delta_{pr}\mathsf{T}_{qs}-\delta_{qs}\mathsf{T}_{pr}+\delta_{ps}\mathsf{T}_{qr}\,,

(9.2)


while the trace in its fundamental representation may be evaluated to find





 \mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})=-2\left(\delta_{pr}\delta_{qs}-\delta_{ps}\delta_{qr}\right)\,. tr⁡(𝖳p​q​𝖳r​s)=−2​(δp​r​δq​s−δp​s​δq​r).\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})=-2\left(\delta_{pr}\delta_{qs}-\delta_{ps}\delta_{qr}\right)\,.

(9.3)


These fix the conventions for our color factors.




The currents  J(z)\equiv J[0,0](z) J​(z)≡J​[0,0]​(z)J(z)\equiv J[0,0](z) form a non-unitary  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8) current algebra at level  -2N −2​N-2N,





 J_{pq}(z)\,J_{rs}(z^{\prime})\sim-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})}{(z-z^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}J_{tu}(z^{\prime})}{z-z^{\prime}}\,. Jp​q​(z)​Jr​s​(z′)∼−N​tr​(𝖳p​q​𝖳r​s)(z−z′)2+fp​q,r​s​Jt​ut​u​(z′)z−z′.J_{pq}(z)\,J_{rs}(z^{\prime})\sim-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})}{(z-z^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}J_{tu}(z^{\prime})}{z-z^{\prime}}\,.

(9.4)


This is computed using the free field OPEs (4.4). The first term on the right determines the 2-point function  \langle J[0,0]J[0,0]\rangle ⟨J⁡[0,0]​J​[0,0]⟩\langle J[0,0]J[0,0]\rangle. This is a Burns space analogue of the soft-gluon Kac-Moody algebra [111]. Its non-unitarity is consistent with general expectations in celestial holography. Similar central extensions of the soft gluon algebra have also been observed in other closely related theories and classical backgrounds [33, 112].




The 2-point functions of more general currents  J[k,l] J⁡[k,l]J[k,l] may be computed along the same lines. Since we are working in the vacuum conformal block in which  X,I X,IX,I have vanishing one-point functions, the only terms that survive are those in which all the fields  X,I X,IX,I have been Wick contracted away. Hence,  J[k,l] J⁡[k,l]J[k,l] can only have a 2-point function with  J[l,k] J⁡[l,k]J[l,k].




Also, we only keep planar contractions, since we wish to reproduce a tree level 2-point amplitude in the bulk. Each two point function  \langle J_{pq}[k,l]J_{rs}[l,k]\rangle ⟨Jp​q​[k,l]​Jr​s​[l,k]⟩\langle J_{pq}[k,l]J_{rs}[l,k]\rangle then receives contributions from two planar orderings, one in which  I_{p} IpI_{p} is contracted with  I_{r} IrI_{r}, and another in which  I_{p} IpI_{p} is contracted with  I_{s} IsI_{s}. The rest of the contractions are then fixed by planarity. An example of such a configuration of planar contractions is displayed in figure 4(a).

(a) Wick contractions


 Refer to caption 
(b) Planar diagrams

Figure 4: Planar Wick contractions of  \mathfrak{so}(8) 𝔰​𝔬​(8)\mathfrak{so}(8) currents containing equal number of  X XX’s.




To produce a prediction for the 2-point amplitude, we use the dictionary (8.11). This says that the 2-point amplitude of two WZW4 states  \phi_{i}\equiv\phi_{pq}(x|z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}) ϕi≡ϕp​q​(x|zi,λ~i)\phi_{i}\equiv\phi_{pq}(x|z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}) and  \phi_{j}\equiv\phi_{rs}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) ϕj≡ϕr​s​(x|zj,λ~j)\phi_{j}\equiv\phi_{rs}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) is given by the chiral algebra correlator





 \begin{split}A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})&=\bigl\langle J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\bigr\rangle\\
&=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{z_{i}^{m}z_{j}^{n}}{m!n!}\,\Bigl\langle:\!I_{p}[X\,i]^{m}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{i})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,j]^{n}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{j})\Bigl\rangle\\
&=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{(z_{i}z_{j})^{n}}{(n!)^{2}}\,\Bigl\langle:\!I_{p}[X\,i]^{n}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{i})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,j]^{n}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{j})\Bigl\rangle\,.\end{split} A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)=⟨Jp​q​(zi,λ~i)​Jr​s​(zj,λ~j)⟩=∑m,n≥0zim​zjnm!​n!⟨:Ip[Xi]mIq:(zi):Ir[Xj]nIs:(zj)⟩=∑n≥0(zi​zj)n(n!)2⟨:Ip[Xi]nIq:(zi):Ir[Xj]nIs:(zj)⟩.\begin{split}A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})&=\bigl\langle J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\bigr\rangle\\
&=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{z_{i}^{m}z_{j}^{n}}{m!n!}\,\Bigl\langle:\!I_{p}[X\,i]^{m}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{i})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,j]^{n}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{j})\Bigl\rangle\\
&=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{(z_{i}z_{j})^{n}}{(n!)^{2}}\,\Bigl\langle:\!I_{p}[X\,i]^{n}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{i})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,j]^{n}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{j})\Bigl\rangle\,.\end{split}

(9.5)


In getting the third line, we have discarded terms for which  m\neq n m≠nm\neq n as they do not contain non-zero 2-point functions.




Note that in our conventions





 \bigl\langle[X^{k}{}_{l}\,i](z_{i})\,[X^{m}{}_{n}\,j](z_{j})\bigr\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,(\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l}-\omega^{km}\omega_{ln})\cdot\frac{-[i\,j]}{z_{ij}} ⟨[Xk​il]​(zi)​[Xm​jn]​(zj)⟩=12​(δnk​δlm−ωk​m​ωl​n)⋅−[i​j]zi​j\bigl\langle[X^{k}{}_{l}\,i](z_{i})\,[X^{m}{}_{n}\,j](z_{j})\bigr\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,(\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l}-\omega^{km}\omega_{ln})\cdot\frac{-[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}

(9.6)


because  \epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}}=-[i\,j] ϵα˙​β˙​λ~i​α˙​λ~j​β˙=−[i​j]\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}}=-[i\,j]. Each closed loop in a planar diagram generates a factor of  \omega^{kl}\omega_{lk}=\delta^{k}{}_{k}=2N ωk​lωl​k=δkk=2N\omega^{kl}\omega_{lk}=\delta^{k}{}_{k}=2N. The  n^{\text{th}} nthn^{\text{th}} term in the sum (9.5) will receive contributions from planar diagrams with  n+1 n+1n+1 closed loops, like the ones in figure 4(b). This generates a factor of  (2N)^{n+1} (2​N)n+1(2N)^{n+1}. The  n nn  XX X​XXX OPEs also generate a factor  (\frac{1}{2})^{n} (12)n(\frac{1}{2})^{n} through the antisymmetrizations  \frac{1}{2}(\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l}-\omega^{km}\omega_{ln}) 12​(δnk​δlm−ωk​m​ωl​n)\frac{1}{2}(\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l}-\omega^{km}\omega_{ln}), since only  \delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l} δnk​δlm\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{m}_{l} contributes to planar diagrams. They also generate a factor of  (-[i\,j])^{n} (−[i​j])n(-[i\,j])^{n}. So each summand in (9.5) comes with  (2N)^{n+1}(-[i\,j])^{n}/2^{n}=2N(-N[i\,j])^{n} (2​N)n+1​(−[i​j])n/2n=2​N​(−N⁡[i​j])n(2N)^{n+1}(-[i\,j])^{n}/2^{n}=2N(-N[i\,j])^{n}.




Thus, performing the OPEs and dropping non-planar contributions generated in each summand, the 2-point correlator can be expressed in terms of a Bessel function of the first kind:





 \begin{split}A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})&=2N(\delta_{pr}\delta_{qs}-\delta_{ps}\delta_{qr})\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-Nz_{i}z_{j}[i\,j])^{n}}{z_{ij}^{n+2}}\\
&=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})}{z_{ij}^{2}}\,J_{0}\bigg(\sqrt{4Nz_{i}z_{j}\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}}\bigg)\,.\end{split} A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)=2​N​(δp​r​δq​s−δp​s​δq​r)​∑n≥01(n!)2​(−N​zi​zj​[i​j])nzi​jn+2=−N​tr​(𝖳p​q​𝖳r​s)zi​j2​J0​(4​N​zi​zj​[i​j]zi​j).\begin{split}A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})&=2N(\delta_{pr}\delta_{qs}-\delta_{ps}\delta_{qr})\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-Nz_{i}z_{j}[i\,j])^{n}}{z_{ij}^{n+2}}\\
&=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{pq}\mathsf{T}_{rs})}{z_{ij}^{2}}\,J_{0}\bigg(\sqrt{4Nz_{i}z_{j}\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}}\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(9.7)


Let’s see how this prediction stacks up against a bulk computation of the 2-point amplitude of the WZW4 model. In fact, we will see that the tree level computation in the bulk does not generate any defect singularities  z_{i}^{-1},z_{j}^{-1} zi−1,zj−1z_{i}^{-1},z_{j}^{-1}, so the match with the vacuum block will be perfect. This will act as a spacetime corroboration of the twistorial proof of symmetry enhancement described in section 6.3.




Bulk computation of 2-point amplitude.




Let  \phi_{i} ϕi\phi_{i} be solutions of the linearized field equations of a field theory described by an action  S[\phi] S⁡[ϕ]S[\phi]. Such solutions represent scattering states of particle-like excitations, and  i ii is the particle label. Independently of signature, one can compute the tree level scattering amplitudes of these states from a suitably constructed on-shell action [113, 114]. This is also closely related to the perturbiner method [115, 116].




In this approach, one starts by constructing a solution of the form





 \phi=\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\mathrm{O}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}) ϕ=∑iεi​ϕi+O⁡(εi​εj)\phi=\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\mathrm{O}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})

(9.8)


to the fully nonlinear field equations of  S[\phi] S⁡[ϕ]S[\phi], where the formal variables  \varepsilon_{i} εi\varepsilon_{i} are classical analogues of creation operators. Having found such a solution, one evaluates its on-shell action as a power series in the  \varepsilon_{i} εi\varepsilon_{i}. The amplitude for these states is then obtained by extracting the term in the on-shell action that is multilinear in the  \varepsilon_{i} εi\varepsilon_{i}:





 A(\{\phi_{i}\})=\bigg(\prod_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{j}}\bigg)\,S[\phi]\,\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{i}=0}\,. A⁡({ϕi})=(∏j∂∂εj)​S​[ϕ]|εi=0.A(\{\phi_{i}\})=\bigg(\prod_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{j}}\bigg)\,S[\phi]\,\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{i}=0}\,.

(9.9)


In Lorentzian signature spacetimes with canonical notions of asymptotic infinities and positive vs negative frequency states, this reduces to the standard definition of the S-matrix. More generally, this is the prescription that is commonly employed in computing holographic correlators in AdS/CFT [44].




The evaluation of 2-point amplitudes is particularly simple. Starting with the action (2.30) of the WZW4 model, one simply evaluates its kinetic term on the linear combination





 \phi=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j} ϕ=εi​ϕi+εj​ϕj\phi=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}

(9.10)


of two solutions of the adjoint-valued Laplace equation. Following this, one extracts the amplitude as the coefficient of  \varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j} εi​εj\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}:





 A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{i}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}+\partial\phi_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}\right)\,. A(ϕi,ϕj)=−i8​π2∫Mω∧tr(∂ϕi∧∂¯ϕj+∂ϕj∧∂¯ϕi).A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{i}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}+\partial\phi_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}\right)\,.

(9.11)


Let us determine this for a pair of quasi-momentum eigenstates of the form (7.29) scattering on  M=\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} M=ℂ~2M=\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} equipped with the Burns metric.




Before embarking on the calculation, it is worth remarking that this 2-point amplitude has also been computed in a beautiful and simpler manner via the Klein-Gordon inner product on  \mathbb{CP}^{2} ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2} minus a point in [74]. This is accomplished through analytic continuation to a Lorentzian slice equipped with the complexified Burns or Fubini-Study metric. This method trades the spacetime integral in (9.11) for an integral over the conformal boundary, and hence is only applicable at 2 points. In contrast, the methods we follow below – albeit more cumbersome – will also generalize to the computation of higher point amplitudes.




The Kähler form of the Burns metric was found in (3.36) and can be written as





 \omega=\mathrm{i}\left(\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu_{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\right)\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\,. ω=i⁡(ϵα˙​β˙+N​uα˙​u^β˙‖u‖4)​d​uα˙∧d​u^β˙.\omega=\mathrm{i}\left(\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu_{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\right)\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\,.

(9.12)


Let’s begin by plugging this into the amplitude (9.11). To simplify the resulting expression, recall the definitions





 \partial\phi_{i}=\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}=\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,. ∂ϕi=∂ϕi∂uα˙​d​uα˙,∂¯​ϕi=∂ϕi∂u^α˙​d​u^α˙.\partial\phi_{i}=\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}=\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(9.13)


We can use our orientation convention (2.9), the definition of the quaternionic conjugate  \hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}=(-\bar{u}^{2},\bar{u}^{1}) u^α˙=(−u¯2,u¯1)\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}=(-\bar{u}^{2},\bar{u}^{1}), and standard spinor identities to show that





 \mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\wedge\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\gamma}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\delta}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\gamma}}\epsilon^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\delta}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,. d​uα˙∧d​u^β˙∧d​uγ˙∧d​u^δ˙=ϵα˙​γ˙​ϵβ˙​δ˙​d4​x.\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\alpha}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\wedge\mathrm{d}u^{\dot{\gamma}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\hat{u}^{\dot{\delta}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\gamma}}\epsilon^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\delta}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,.

(9.14)


Using these relations, the 2-gluon amplitude (9.11) takes the form





 A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\bigg(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}+(i\leftrightarrow j)\right)\,. A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)=18​π2​∫ℝ4d4​x​(ϵα˙​β˙+N​uα˙​u^β˙‖u‖4)​tr​(∂ϕi∂uα˙​∂ϕj∂u^β˙+(i↔j)).A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\bigg(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}+\frac{Nu^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{tr}\!\left(\frac{\partial\phi_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}+(i\leftrightarrow j)\right)\,.

(9.15)


In writing this, we are computing the integral over  \widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} ℂ~2\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2} as an integral over the coordinate patch  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}) xα​α˙=(uα˙,u^α˙)x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}). Strictly speaking, the integral is over  \mathbb{C}^{2}-0 ℂ2−0\mathbb{C}^{2}-0 due to the singularities at the origin. But it can be regularized via standard procedures involved in computing Fourier transforms of Coulomb-type potentials. So we will continue to express it as an integral over  \mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{4} ℂ2=ℝ4\mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{4}.




The wavefunctions for the states  \phi_{i},\phi_{j} ϕi,ϕj\phi_{i},\phi_{j} were derived in section 7.1. Let us repeat them for convenience. A quasi-momentum eigenstate  \phi_{i}(x) ϕi​(x)\phi_{i}(x) that solves the adjoint-valued Laplace equation is given by the expansion in (7.20),





 \phi_{i}(x)=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n)!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}{}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)\,, ϕi​(x)=𝖳aiλi​1​λi​2​∑n=0∞1(2​n)!​(−N​λi​1​λi​2​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2)n​F11​(n+1,2​n+1|i​pi⋅x),\phi_{i}(x)=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n)!}\biggl(-N\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}{}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)\,,

(9.16)


where  [u\,i]\equiv[u\,\tilde{\lambda}_{i}] [u​i]≡[u​λ~i][u\,i]\equiv[u\,\tilde{\lambda}_{i}], etc., and  \lambda_{i\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}} λi​α,λ~i​α˙\lambda_{i\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}} are spinor-helicity variables labelling the state. The null momentum  p_{i} pip_{i} has spinor components  p_{i\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{i\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}} pi​α​α˙=λi​α​λ~i​α˙p_{i\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{i\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}, so that its product with  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} xα​α˙x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}} can be expressed as  p_{i}\cdot x=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i] pi⋅x=λi​1​[u​i]+λi​2​[u^​i]p_{i}\cdot x=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i]. The state  \phi_{j}(x) ϕj​(x)\phi_{j}(x) is obtained by replacing the particle label  i ii with the label  j jj.




Let us denote the coefficient of  \mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}N^{n} 𝖳ai​Nn\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}N^{n} in this wavefunction by  \phi_{i}^{n}(x) ϕin​(x)\phi_{i}^{n}(x):





 \phi_{i}^{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,\frac{1}{(2n)!}\biggl(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}{}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)\,. ϕin​(x)=1λi​1​λi​2​1(2​n)!​(−λi​1​λi​2​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2)n​F11​(n+1,2​n+1|i​pi⋅x).\phi_{i}^{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,\frac{1}{(2n)!}\biggl(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}{}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)\,.

(9.17)


The confluent hypergeometric function occurring here has a very useful expansion on flat space momentum eigenstates,





 {}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)=\frac{\Gamma(2n+1)}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}(1-s_{i})^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,. F11​(n+1,2​n+1|i​pi⋅x)=Γ⁡(2​n+1)Γ⁡(n+1)​Γ​(n)​∫01d​si​sin​(1−si)n−1​ei​si​pi⋅x.{}_{1}F_{1}(n+1,2n+1\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x)=\frac{\Gamma(2n+1)}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}(1-s_{i})^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,.

(9.18)


For  n=0 n=0n=0, we recover  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x} ei​pi⋅x\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x} by taking the integral to mean an  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0 limit, understood via the following identity valid within integrals over  s_{i}\in(0,1) si∈(0,1)s_{i}\in(0,1),





 \lim_{n\to 0}\frac{(1-s_{i})^{n-1}}{\Gamma(n)}=\delta(1-s_{i})\,. limn→0(1−si)n−1Γ⁡(n)=δ⁡(1−si).\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{(1-s_{i})^{n-1}}{\Gamma(n)}=\delta(1-s_{i})\,.

(9.19)


This representation of the states will allow us to perform the spacetime integrals as standard Fourier integrals.




Inserting the expansions





 \phi_{i}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}N^{m}\phi_{i}^{m}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}N^{n}\phi_{j}^{n} ϕi=𝖳ai​∑m=0∞Nm​ϕim,ϕj=𝖳aj​∑n=0∞Nn​ϕjn\phi_{i}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}N^{m}\phi_{i}^{m}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}N^{n}\phi_{j}^{n}

(9.20)


into (9.15) yields the amplitude as a formal power series in  N NN,





 A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}})\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}N^{m+n}\left(N\,\mathcal{I}_{m,n}+\mathcal{J}_{m,n}\right)+(i\leftrightarrow j)\,. A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)=tr⁡(𝖳ai​𝖳aj)​∑m,n=0∞Nm+n​(N​ℐm,n+𝒥m,n)+(i↔j).A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}})\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}N^{m+n}\left(N\,\mathcal{I}_{m,n}+\mathcal{J}_{m,n}\right)+(i\leftrightarrow j)\,.

(9.21)


In writing this, we have split our spacetime integrals into two “irreducible” terms that do not contain any further dependence on  N NN:





 \displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m,n}
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\frac{u^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,, =18​π2​∫d4​x​uα˙​u^β˙‖u‖4​∂ϕim∂uα˙​∂ϕjn∂u^β˙,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\frac{u^{\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,,

(9.22)



 \displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{m,n}
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,. =18​π2​∫d4​x​ϵα˙​β˙​∂ϕim∂uα˙​∂ϕjn∂u^β˙.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,.

(9.23)


Both these integrals can be performed using very similar techniques. Let us see how to compute them one by one.




Computation of  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n}.




We will compute the first of these in some detail, as it happens to be the simpler case.




One of the reasons this is easier is because of the following identities





 u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=0\,. uα˙​∂∂uα˙​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2=u^α˙​∂∂u^α˙​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2=0.u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=0\,.

(9.24)


These hold because the vector fields  u\cdot\partial_{u} u⋅∂uu\cdot\partial_{u} and  \hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}} u^⋅∂u^\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}} are the Euler vector fields that extract homogeneities in  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} and  \hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} u^α˙\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}} respectively, and  [u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]/\|u\|^{2}=[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]/[\hat{u}\,u] [u​i]​[u^​i]/‖u‖2=[u​i]​[u^​i]/[u^​u][u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]/\|u\|^{2}=[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]/[\hat{u}\,u] has zero homogeneity in both of these variables. So the derivatives displayed in (9.22) will only act on the dependence in  \mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x i​pi⋅x\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x and  \mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x i​pj⋅x\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x. These can be computed by using the formula for  \phi_{i}^{n} ϕin\phi_{i}^{n} given in (9.17), combined with the integral representation (9.18) for the  {}_{1}F_{1} F11{}_{1}F_{1} factors,





 \displaystyle u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}} uα˙​∂ϕim∂uα˙\displaystyle u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{m}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{m}(1-s_{i})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\times\mathrm{i}s_{i}\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]\,, =1λi​1​λi​2​(−λi​1​λi​2​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2)m​∫01d​si​sim​(1−si)m−1m!​(m−1)!​ei​si​pi⋅x×i​si​λi​1​[u​i],\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{m}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{m}(1-s_{i})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\times\mathrm{i}s_{i}\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]\,,

(9.25)



 \displaystyle\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}} u^β˙​∂ϕjn∂u^β˙\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}}{n!(n-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\times\mathrm{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]\,. =1λj​1​λj​2​(−λj​1​λj​2​[u​j]​[u^​j]‖u‖2)n​∫01d​sj​sjn​(1−sj)n−1n!​(n−1)!​ei​sj​pj⋅x×i​sj​λj​2​[u^​j].\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}}{n!(n-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\times\mathrm{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]\,.

(9.26)


In deriving these, we have remembered that  p_{i}\cdot x=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i] pi⋅x=λi​1​[u​i]+λi​2​[u^​i]p_{i}\cdot x=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i], etc., using which we found  u\cdot\partial_{u}(p_{i}\cdot x)=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i] u⋅∂u(pi⋅x)=λi​1​[u​i]u\cdot\partial_{u}(p_{i}\cdot x)=\lambda_{i1}[u\,i] and so on.




Next note the extremely helpful intertwining relations





 \begin{split}\mathrm{i}[u\,i]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}&=\frac{1}{s_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{i1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,,\\
\mathrm{i}[\hat{u}\,i]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}&=\frac{1}{s_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{i2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,.\end{split} i⁡[u​i]​ei​si​pi⋅x=1si​∂∂λi​1​ei​si​pi⋅x,i⁡[u^​i]​ei​si​pi⋅x=1si​∂∂λi​2​ei​si​pi⋅x.\begin{split}\mathrm{i}[u\,i]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}&=\frac{1}{s_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{i1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,,\\
\mathrm{i}[\hat{u}\,i]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}&=\frac{1}{s_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{i2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,.\end{split}

(9.27)


We can use these to simplify our integrals by trading factors of  [u\,i] [u​i][u\,i],  [\hat{u}\,i] [u^​i][\hat{u}\,i], etc. for derivatives in the external data like the spinor-helicity variables. Doing this, (9.25) and (9.26) can be recast as





 \displaystyle u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}} uα˙​∂ϕim∂uα˙\displaystyle u^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}
 \displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\|u\|^{2m}}\bigg)\,, =λi​1m​λi​2m−1m!​(m−1)!​∫01d​sisim​(1−si)m−1×∂λi​1m+1∂λi​2m(ei​si​pi⋅x‖u‖2​m),\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\|u\|^{2m}}\bigg)\,,

(9.28)



 \displaystyle\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}} u^β˙​∂ϕjn∂u^β˙\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}
 \displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\times\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{n+1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\|u\|^{2n}}\bigg)\,. =λj​1n−1​λj​2nn!​(n−1)!​∫01d​sjsjn​(1−sj)n−1×∂λj​1n∂λj​2n+1(ei​sj​pj⋅x‖u‖2​n).\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\times\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{n+1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\|u\|^{2n}}\bigg)\,.

(9.29)


With these representations for the derivatives in hand, we are finally ready to start integrating.




Substituting (9.28), (9.29) into the definition (9.22) of  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n}, we can segregate the  s_{i},s_{j} si,sjs_{i},s_{j} integrals from the spacetime integrals,





 \mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\\
\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{n+1}\mathcal{S}_{m+n+2}\,, ℐm,n=λi​1m​λi​2m−1m!​(m−1)!​λj​1n−1​λj​2nn!​(n−1)!​∫01d​sisim​(1−si)m−1​∫01d​sjsjn​(1−sj)n−1×∂λi​1m+1∂λi​2m∂λj​1n∂λj​2n+1𝒮m+n+2,\mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\\
\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{n+1}\mathcal{S}_{m+n+2}\,,

(9.30)


where the final factor contains the most basic spacetime integral that we will repeatedly encounter:





 \mathcal{S}_{\ell}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}\,. 𝒮ℓ=∫d4​x8​π2​ei⁡(si​pi+sj​pj)⋅x(x2/2)ℓ.\mathcal{S}_{\ell}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}\,.

(9.31)


In writing this, we have recalled the expression  \|u\|^{2}=x^{2}/2 ‖u‖2=x2/2\|u\|^{2}=x^{2}/2 for the Euclidean norm in our conventions  u^{\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x^{0}+\mathrm{i}x^{3},x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}) uα˙=12​(x0+i​x3,x2+i​x1)u^{\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x^{0}+\mathrm{i}x^{3},x^{2}+\mathrm{i}x^{1}) for the complex coordinates. The spacetime integral has now manifestly reduced to a Coulomb-type Fourier transform in four dimensions. We will perform it by Fourier transforming with respect to real momenta and analytically continuing the result to generic complex momenta like  s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j} si​pi+sj​pjs_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j}.




It is useful for future reference to set this up in some generality. Let  k_{\mu} kμk_{\mu} be a real momentum and consider the Fourier integral





 \int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}\,. ∫d4​x​ei​k⋅x(x2/2)ℓ.\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}\,.

(9.32)


Since  x^{2}>0 x2>0x^{2}>0 in Euclidean signature, we can introduce the Schwinger parametrization





 \frac{1}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{\ell-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tx^{2}/2}\,, 1(x2/2)ℓ=1Γ⁡(ℓ)∫0∞dttℓ−1e−tx2/2,\frac{1}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{\ell-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tx^{2}/2}\,,

(9.33)


where we are assuming that  \ell ℓ\ell is not a negative integer. Doing this allows us to perform the  x^{\mu} xμx^{\mu} integral as a Fourier transform of a Gaussian. This yields a slick identity for obtaining Coulomb-like Fourier transforms:





 \int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tk^{2}/2}\,. ∫d4​x​ei​k⋅x(x2/2)ℓ=4​π2Γ⁡(ℓ)∫0∞d​ttℓ−1e−tk2/2.\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tk^{2}/2}\,.

(9.34)


In arriving at this, we have inverted  t\mapsto 1/t t↦1/tt\mapsto 1/t after doing the  x^{\mu} xμx^{\mu} integral. A useful sanity check is that when  \ell=1 ℓ=1\ell=1, this gives the expected Fourier transform  -8\pi^{2}/k^{2} −8π2/k2-8\pi^{2}/k^{2} of a 4d Coulomb potential  2/x^{2} 2/x22/x^{2}.




Analytically continuing to complex  k_{\mu} kμk_{\mu} away from the singular locus  k^{2}=0 k2=0k^{2}=0 and setting  k=s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j} k=si​pi+sj​pjk=s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j} yields





 \mathcal{S}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts_{i}s_{j}p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}\,. 𝒮ℓ=121Γ⁡(ℓ)∫0∞d​ttℓ−1e−tsisjpi⋅pj.\mathcal{S}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts_{i}s_{j}p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}\,.

(9.35)


If  \ell ℓ\ell is a positive integer greater than  1 11, then this integral diverges. But this is precisely the case we’re in:  \ell=m+n+2 ℓ=m+n+2\ell=m+n+2 for (9.30)! What will save this integral from diverging are the  \lambda_{i\alpha},\lambda_{j\alpha} λi​α,λj​α\lambda_{i\alpha},\lambda_{j\alpha} derivatives present in (9.30). Plugging the value of  \mathcal{S}_{m+n+2} 𝒮m+n+2\mathcal{S}_{m+n+2} into (9.30) and performing just the  \lambda_{i2},\lambda_{j2} λi​2,λj​2\lambda_{i2},\lambda_{j2} derivatives is enough to leave us with a convergent integral





 \mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\\
\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts_{i}s_{j}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]}\,(-s_{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j1}[i\,j])^{m}(s_{i}s_{j}\lambda_{i1}[i\,j])^{n+1}\,, ℐm,n=12​1(m+n+1)!​λi​1m​λi​2m−1m!​(m−1)!​λj​1n−1​λj​2nn!​(n−1)!​∫01d​sisim​(1−si)m−1​∫01d​sjsjn​(1−sj)n−1×∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n∫0∞d​t​e−t​si​sj​⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]​(−si​sj​λj​1​[i​j])m​(si​sj​λi​1​[i​j])n+1,\mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\\
\times\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts_{i}s_{j}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]}\,(-s_{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j1}[i\,j])^{m}(s_{i}s_{j}\lambda_{i1}[i\,j])^{n+1}\,,

(9.36)


having used  p_{i}\cdot p_{j}=\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j] pi⋅pj=⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]p_{i}\cdot p_{j}=\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]. We are abbreviating  \langle i\,j\rangle\equiv\langle\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\rangle ⟨i​j⟩≡⟨λi​λj⟩\langle i\,j\rangle\equiv\langle\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\rangle,  [i\,j]\equiv[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}] [i​j]≡[λ~i​λ~j][i\,j]\equiv[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}] as is common practice, and have computed the derivatives using





 \langle i\,j\rangle=\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\implies\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\langle i\,j\rangle=\lambda_{j1}\,,\quad\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}\langle i\,j\rangle=-\lambda_{i1}\,. ⟨i​j⟩=λi​2​λj​1−λi​1​λj​2⟹∂λi​2⟨i​j⟩=λj​1,∂λj​2⟨i​j⟩=−λi​1.\langle i\,j\rangle=\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\implies\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\langle i\,j\rangle=\lambda_{j1}\,,\quad\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}\langle i\,j\rangle=-\lambda_{i1}\,.

(9.37)


(9.36) is now in a form amenable to straightforward integration.




We can integrate over  t tt by analytically continuing the standard Mellin integral





 \int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-tk^{2}/2}=\frac{2}{k^{2}} ∫0∞dte−tk2/2=2k2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-tk^{2}/2}=\frac{2}{k^{2}}

(9.38)


to the non-vanishing complex value  k^{2}=(s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})^{2}=2s_{i}s_{j}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j] k2=(si​pi+sj​pj)2=2​si​sj​⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]k^{2}=(s_{i}p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})^{2}=2s_{i}s_{j}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]. After some simplifications, this produces





 \mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(-1)^{m}[i\,j]^{m+n}}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\;\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)\\
\times\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{m}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\,. ℐm,n=12​(−1)m​[i​j]m+n(m+n+1)!​λi​1m​λi​2m−1m!​(m−1)!​λj​1n−1​λj​2nn!​(n−1)!​∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)×∫01d​si​sin​(1−si)m−1​∫01d​sj​sjm​(1−sj)n−1.\mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(-1)^{m}[i\,j]^{m+n}}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{n!(n-1)!}\;\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)\\
\times\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{m}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\,.

(9.39)


We can integrate over  s_{i} sis_{i} and  s_{j} sjs_{j} using the Euler Beta integral to find





 \int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{m}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}=\frac{n!(m-1)!}{(m+n)!}\cdot\frac{m!(n-1)!}{(m+n)!}\,. ∫01d​si​sin​(1−si)m−1​∫01d​sj​sjm​(1−sj)n−1=n!​(m−1)!(m+n)!⋅m!​(n−1)!(m+n)!.\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{n}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{m}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}=\frac{n!(m-1)!}{(m+n)!}\cdot\frac{m!(n-1)!}{(m+n)!}\,.

(9.40)


As a result, we are able to perform all the integrals in  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n} and conclude that





 \mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(-1)^{m}[i\,j]^{m+n}}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{((m+n)!)^{2}}\;\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)\,. ℐm,n=12​(−1)m​[i​j]m+n(m+n+1)!​λi​1m​λi​2m−1​λj​1n−1​λj​2n((m+n)!)2​∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩).\mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(-1)^{m}[i\,j]^{m+n}}{(m+n+1)!}\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{m}\lambda_{i2}^{m-1}\lambda_{j1}^{n-1}\lambda_{j2}^{n}}{((m+n)!)^{2}}\;\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)\,.

(9.41)


In the edge cases  m=0 m=0m=0 or  n=0 n=0n=0, the same result is obtained by remembering the identity (9.19).




The last step is to compute the  \lambda_{i1},\lambda_{j1} λi​1,λj​1\lambda_{i1},\lambda_{j1} derivatives. Although this step looks deceptively hard, it will display a few more combinatorial simplifications that precipitate a delightfully compact final answer. In detail, we want to compute





 \partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}}\bigg)\,. ∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)=∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1mλi​2​λj​1−λi​1​λj​2).\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}}\bigg)\,.

(9.42)


Suppose first that  m\geq n m≥nm\geq n. Substituting  \lambda_{i1}^{n+1}=(\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\langle i\,j\rangle)^{n+1}/\lambda_{j2}^{n+1} λi​1n+1=(λi​2​λj​1−⟨i​j⟩)n+1/λj​2n+1\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}=(\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}-\langle i\,j\rangle)^{n+1}/\lambda_{j2}^{n+1} and expanding using the binomial theorem yields





 \partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}{n+1\choose k}\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1-k}\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1-k}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n+1}}\,(-1)^{k}\langle i\,j\rangle^{k-1}\,. ∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)=∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n∑k=0n+1(n+1k)​λi​2n+1−k​λj​1m+n+1−kλj​2n+1​(−1)k​⟨i​j⟩k−1.\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}{n+1\choose k}\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1-k}\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1-k}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n+1}}\,(-1)^{k}\langle i\,j\rangle^{k-1}\,.

(9.43)


Since  \lambda_{i1} λi​1\lambda_{i1} only enters the right hand side through  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle, the  \lambda_{i1} λi​1\lambda_{i1} derivatives only act on the factors of  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle. Moreover,  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle is linear in  \lambda_{i1} λi​1\lambda_{i1}. So in the case  m\geq n m≥nm\geq n, only the  k=0 k=0k=0 term of the sum survives the  m+1 m+1m+1  \lambda_{i1} λi​1\lambda_{i1} derivatives because  k-1 k−1k-1 is bounded above by  n nn.




This simplification allows us to compute the  \lambda_{i1} λi​1\lambda_{i1} derivatives in closed-form:





 \displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg) ∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)
 \displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n+1}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg) =λi​2n+1λj​2n+1​∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λj​1m+n+1⟨i​j⟩)\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n+1}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)





 \displaystyle=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\bigg)\,. =(m+1)!​λi​2n+1λj​2n−m​∂λj​1n(λj​1m+n+1⟨i​j⟩m+2).\displaystyle=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\bigg)\,.

(9.44)


Distributing the remaining  \lambda_{j1} λj​1\lambda_{j1} derivatives among the factors of  \lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1} λj​1m+n+1\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1} and  1/\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2} 1/⟨i​j⟩m+21/\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2} produces






 \displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n-k}\bigl(\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}\bigr)\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{k}\bigg(\frac{1}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\bigg) ∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)=(m+1)!​λi​2n+1λj​2n−m​∑k=0n(nk)​∂λj​1n−k(λj​1m+n+1)​∂λj​1k(1⟨i​j⟩m+2)\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n-k}\bigl(\lambda_{j1}^{m+n+1}\bigr)\,\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{k}\bigg(\frac{1}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\bigg)





 \displaystyle=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\frac{(m+n+1)!}{(m+k+1)!}\,\lambda_{j1}^{m+k+1}\times\frac{(m+k+1)!}{(m+1)!}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i2})^{k}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+k+2}} =(m+1)!​λi​2n+1λj​2n−m​∑k=0n(nk)​(m+n+1)!(m+k+1)!​λj​1m+k+1×(m+k+1)!(m+1)!​(−λi​2)k⟨i​j⟩m+k+2\displaystyle=(m+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}}{\lambda_{j2}^{n-m}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\frac{(m+n+1)!}{(m+k+1)!}\,\lambda_{j1}^{m+k+1}\times\frac{(m+k+1)!}{(m+1)!}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i2})^{k}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+k+2}}





 \displaystyle=(m+n+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m+1}\lambda_{j2}^{m-n}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})^{k}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{k}}\,. =(m+n+1)!​λi​2n+1​λj​1m+1​λj​2m−n⟨i​j⟩m+2​∑k=0n(nk)​(−λi​2​λj​1)k⟨i​j⟩k.\displaystyle=(m+n+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m+1}\lambda_{j2}^{m-n}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{n\choose k}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})^{k}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{k}}\,.

(9.45)


Resumming the last line using the binomial theorem lands us on the identity





 \displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=(-1)^{n}\,(m+n+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n}\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m+1}\lambda_{j2}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+2}}\,. ∂λi​1m+1∂λj​1n(λi​1n+1​λj​1m⟨i​j⟩)=(−1)n​(m+n+1)!​λi​1n​λi​2n+1​λj​1m+1​λj​2m⟨i​j⟩m+n+2.\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\bigg)=(-1)^{n}\,(m+n+1)!\,\frac{\lambda_{i1}^{n}\lambda_{i2}^{n+1}\lambda_{j1}^{m+1}\lambda_{j2}^{m}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+2}}\,.

(9.46)


One obtains the same result for the case  0\leq m<n 0≤m<n0\leq m<n by initially eliminating the factor of  \lambda_{j1}^{m} λj​1m\lambda_{j1}^{m} instead of  \lambda_{i1}^{n+1} λi​1n+1\lambda_{i1}^{n+1}.




With this identity, we can finish off the calculation of  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n},





 \mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{((m+n)!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j])^{m+n}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+2}}\qquad\forall\;m,n\geq 0\,. ℐm,n=12​1((m+n)!)2​(−λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2​[i​j])m+n⟨i​j⟩m+n+2∀m,n≥0.\mathcal{I}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{((m+n)!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j])^{m+n}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+2}}\qquad\forall\;m,n\geq 0\,.

(9.47)


It is very interesting to note that this only depends on the sum  m+n m+nm+n, which will come in handy when resumming the series expansion (9.21) of the 2-point amplitude. Before we turn to resummation, let us also similarly evaluate  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n}.




Computation of  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n}.




The second integral occurring in the 2-gluon amplitude was defined in (9.23). In the case  m=n=0 m=n=0m=n=0, upon symmetrization in  i,j i,ji,j,  \mathcal{J}_{0,0} 𝒥0,0\mathcal{J}_{0,0} reduces simply to the flat space 2-point amplitude:






 \displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0,0}+(i\leftrightarrow j)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}+(i\leftrightarrow j) 𝒥0,0+(i↔j)=18​π2​∫d4​x​ϵα˙​β˙​∂ϕi0∂uα˙​∂ϕj0∂u^β˙+(i↔j)\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0,0}+(i\leftrightarrow j)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}+(i\leftrightarrow j)





 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i1}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}})(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j2}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}})}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}+(i\leftrightarrow j) =18​π2​∫d4​x​ϵα˙​β˙​(i​λi​1​λ~i​α˙)​(i​λj​2​λ~j​β˙)λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2​ei⁡(pi+pj)⋅x+(i↔j)\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\frac{(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i1}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}})(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j2}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}})}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}+(i\leftrightarrow j)





 \displaystyle=-\frac{\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]\,\delta^{4}(p_{i}+p_{j})}{8\pi^{2}\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}=0\,, =−⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]​δ4​(pi+pj)8​π2​λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2=0,\displaystyle=-\frac{\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]\,\delta^{4}(p_{i}+p_{j})}{8\pi^{2}\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}=0\,,

(9.48)


which vanishes for generic momenta due to  0=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}=2\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j] 0=(pi+pj)2=2​⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]0=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}=2\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j] on the support of the delta function. For all other values of  m,n m,nm,n, we expect to get a nontrivial answer arising from working on the Burns background.




Previously in (9.25), (9.26), we obtained expressions for  u\cdot\partial_{u}\phi_{i}^{m} u⋅∂uϕimu\cdot\partial_{u}\phi_{i}^{m} and  \hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}}\phi_{j}^{n} u^⋅∂u^ϕjn\hat{u}\cdot\partial_{\hat{u}}\phi_{j}^{n}. To evaluate  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n}, we need the more general derivatives  \partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\phi_{i}^{m} ∂uα˙ϕim\partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\phi_{i}^{m},  \partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\phi_{j}^{n} ∂u^β˙ϕjn\partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\phi_{j}^{n}. To find these, one first establishes the derivatives





 \frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=\frac{[\hat{u}\,i]^{2}u_{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,,\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}=-\frac{[u\,j]^{2}\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,, ∂∂uα˙​[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2=[u^​i]2​uα˙‖u‖4,∂∂u^β˙​[u​j]​[u^​j]‖u‖2=−[u​j]2​u^β˙‖u‖4,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}=\frac{[\hat{u}\,i]^{2}u_{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,,\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}=-\frac{[u\,j]^{2}\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{4}}\,,

(9.49)


simplified using  \|u\|^{2}=[\hat{u}\,u] ‖u‖2=[u^​u]\|u\|^{2}=[\hat{u}\,u] and Schouten’s identities like  [\hat{u}\,u]\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}+[u\,i]\hat{u}_{\dot{\alpha}}=[\hat{u}\,i]u_{\dot{\alpha}} [u^​u]​λ~i​α˙+[u​i]​u^α˙=[u^​i]​uα˙[\hat{u}\,u]\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}+[u\,i]\hat{u}_{\dot{\alpha}}=[\hat{u}\,i]u_{\dot{\alpha}}, etc. Along with the by now familiar expressions  \partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}(p_{i}\cdot x)=\lambda_{i1}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}} ∂uα˙(pi⋅x)=λi​1​λ~i​α˙\partial_{u^{\dot{\alpha}}}(p_{i}\cdot x)=\lambda_{i1}\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}} and  \partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}(p_{j}\cdot x)=\lambda_{j2}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}} ∂u^β˙(pj⋅x)=λj​2​λ~j​β˙\partial_{\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}(p_{j}\cdot x)=\lambda_{j2}\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}}, these can be used to determine





 \begin{split}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}=-\left(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}&\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{m}(1-s_{i})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\\
&\times\frac{[\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\bigg(\mathrm{i}s_{i}\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}+\frac{m[\hat{u}\,i]u_{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)\end{split} ∂ϕim∂uα˙=−(−λi​1λi​2[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2)m−1∫01d​si​sim​(1−si)m−1m!​(m−1)!​ei​si​pi⋅x×[u^​i]‖u‖2​(i​si​λi​1​[u​i]​λ~i​α˙+m⁡[u^​i]​uα˙‖u‖2)\begin{split}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{m}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}}=-\left(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}&\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}^{m}(1-s_{i})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\\
&\times\frac{[\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\bigg(\mathrm{i}s_{i}\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]\tilde{\lambda}_{i\dot{\alpha}}+\frac{m[\hat{u}\,i]u_{\dot{\alpha}}}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)\end{split}

(9.50)


and similarly





 \begin{split}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}=-\left(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{n-1}\int_{0}^{1}&\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}}{n!(n-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\\
&\times\frac{[u\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\bigg(\mathrm{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}}-\frac{n[u\,j]\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)\,.\end{split} ∂ϕjn∂u^β˙=−(−λj​1λj​2[u​j]​[u^​j]‖u‖2)n−1∫01d​sj​sjn​(1−sj)n−1n!​(n−1)!​ei​sj​pj⋅x×[u​j]‖u‖2​(i​sj​λj​2​[u^​j]​λ~j​β˙−n⁡[u​j]​u^β˙‖u‖2).\begin{split}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{n}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}=-\left(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\right)^{n-1}\int_{0}^{1}&\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}}{n!(n-1)!}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\\
&\times\frac{[u\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\bigg(\mathrm{i}s_{j}\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]\tilde{\lambda}_{j\dot{\beta}}-\frac{n[u\,j]\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}{\|u\|^{2}}\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(9.51)


A useful check on these expressions is the fact that contracting them with  u^{\dot{\alpha}} uα˙u^{\dot{\alpha}} and  \hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}} u^β˙\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}} respectively leads to (9.25) and (9.26). Another good check is that as  m\to 0 m→0m\to 0 or  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0, application of the delta function identity (9.19) implies that these reduce to derivatives of the plane waves  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x} ei​pi⋅x\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x} or  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x} ei​pj⋅x\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}. Since we are working with spinor-helicity variables, covariance under the little group scalings (7.15) also remains available as a consistency check at each step of the calculation.




The next step is to substitute (9.50) and (9.51) into the definition (9.23) of  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n}, which involves a straightforward contraction using  \epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}} ϵα˙​β˙\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}. Following this, one can once again use the intertwining relations (9.27) to replace factors of  [u\,i],[\hat{u}\,i] [u​i],[u^​i][u\,i],[\hat{u}\,i], etc. by derivatives in the external spinor-helicity data. When the dust settles, one obtains





 \mathcal{J}_{m,n}=\frac{(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{(\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2})^{n-1}}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{j}}{s_{j}^{n}}\,(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\\
\hskip-85.35826pt\times\Big\{s_{i}s_{j}\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{n}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{m+n}\\

Yet again, this happens to depend only on the sum  m+n m+nm+n. Lastly, we come to the edge cases  \mathcal{J}_{m,0} 𝒥m,0\mathcal{J}_{m,0} and  \mathcal{J}_{0,n} 𝒥0,n\mathcal{J}_{0,n}. At first glance, they appear to result in ill-defined expressions of the form  0\times\infty 0×∞0\times\infty. Indeed,  \mathcal{J}_{m,0} 𝒥m,0\mathcal{J}_{m,0} can be naively evaluated by repeating the steps used to obtain (9.52) but with  n=0 n=0n=0 chosen from the start. This yields

\times\Big\{s_{i}\lambda_{i1}[i\,j]\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\mathcal{S}_{m}-m\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m-1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m+1}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\mathcal{S}_{m+1}\Big\}\,. 𝒥m,0=(λi​1​λi​2)m−1m!​(m−1)!​1λj​1​∫0∞d​sisim​(1−si)m−1×{si​λi​1​[i​j]​∂λi​1m∂λi​2m𝒮m−m​∂λi​1m−1∂λi​2m+1∂λj​1𝒮m+1}.\mathcal{J}_{m,0}=\frac{(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2})^{m-1}}{m!(m-1)!}\,\frac{1}{\lambda_{j1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{i}}{s_{i}^{m}}\,(1-s_{i})^{m-1}\\

 \displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\mathcal{S}_{m} ∂λi​1m∂λi​2m𝒮m\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{m}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{m}\mathcal{S}_{m}
 \displaystyle=\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2})^{m}(s_{i}[i\,j])^{m-2}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\,, =m⁡(m+1)2​(−λj​1​λj​2)m​(si​[i​j])m−2⟨i​j⟩m+2,\displaystyle=\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2})^{m}(s_{i}[i\,j])^{m-2}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+2}}\,,
