From these, it becomes immediately clear that both terms in the integrand of (9.62) lead to divergent  s_{i} sis_{i} integrals, but also that they cancel against each other. So we are led to try and make sense of the result  \mathcal{J}_{m,0}=0\times\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\log s_{i}=0\times\infty 𝒥m,0=0×∫01d​log⁡si=0×∞\mathcal{J}_{m,0}=0\times\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\log s_{i}=0\times\infty. It turns out that if one naively sets this to  0 00, one finds an answer that matches Hawking et al.’s calculation [74] as well as the chiral algebra prediction, but only up to an overall sign error!




It so happens that our computations suggest a natural regularization procedure to overcome this hurdle.3434
                34
                
                
                
              There may exist other more systematic regularization schemes which we leave for future analyses. We notice that the first line of (9.53) has an apparent pole at  mn=0 m​n=0mn=0. But the residue at that pole vanishes because the coefficient of the pole simplifies to (9.60): an expression with a zero at  mn=0 m​n=0mn=0. This cancels the pole and results in (9.61) which is finite and nonvanishing as  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0 with  m\neq 0 m≠0m\neq 0 or vice versa. This resolves our  0\times\infty 0×∞0\times\infty singularity.




More precisely, one analytically continues the order  N^{n} NnN^{n} term (9.17) to arbitrary  n nn (away from non-negative integers) by replacing factorials with gamma functions:





 \phi_{j}^{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}\frac{1}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\,. ϕjn​(x)=1λj​1​λj​2​(−λj​1​λj​2​[u​j]​[u^​j]‖u‖2)n​1Γ⁡(n+1)​Γ​(n)​∫01d​sj​sjn​(1−sj)n−1​ei​sj​pj⋅x.\phi_{j}^{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\biggl(-\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\biggr)^{n}\frac{1}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}^{n}(1-s_{j})^{n-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\,.

(9.65)


 n nn itself now takes up the role of a regulator for  \mathcal{J}_{m,0} 𝒥m,0\mathcal{J}_{m,0}. Using (9.19), we understand  \phi_{j}^{0}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}/\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2} ϕj0=ei​pj⋅x/λj​1​λj​2\phi_{j}^{0}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}/\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2} as its  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0 limit. Plugging  \phi_{i}^{m} ϕim\phi_{i}^{m} and  \phi_{j}^{n} ϕjn\phi_{j}^{n} in  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n} results in an expression analogous to (9.52), but the  n^{\text{th}} nthn^{\text{th}} order derivatives are no longer well-defined for non-integral  n nn. Instead, one employs Mellin identities of the form





 [u\,j]^{a}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega^{a+1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\omega[u\,j]}\,,\qquad a\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\,, [u​j]a=1Γ⁡(−a)​∫0∞d​ωωa+1​e−ω⁡[u​j],a∉ℤ≤0,[u\,j]^{a}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega}{\omega^{a+1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\omega[u\,j]}\,,\qquad a\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\,,

(9.66)


to bring factors like  [u\,j]^{a},[\hat{u}\,j]^{b} [u​j]a,[u^​j]b[u\,j]^{a},[\hat{u}\,j]^{b} in the exponential. The evaluation then proceeds as before using the Fourier identity (9.34).




But since the integrand is analytic in  n nn and the integrals are convergent, we do not need to repeat the calculation. To find the answer, one simply analytically continues (9.61) to non-integral  n nn by replacing  (m+n-1)!\mapsto\Gamma(m+n) (m+n−1)!↦Γ⁡(m+n)(m+n-1)!\mapsto\Gamma(m+n). Following which, one takes the limit  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0. This results in a finite limit when  m\neq 0 m≠0m\neq 0. So we conclude that, with this choice of regularization,  \mathcal{J}_{m,0} 𝒥m,0\mathcal{J}_{m,0} coincides with the limit of  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n} as  n\to 0 n→0n\to 0. Hence,





 \mathcal{J}_{m,n}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{((m+n-1)!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j])^{m+n-1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+1}}\qquad\forall\;m,n\neq 0\,. 𝒥m,n=−12​1((m+n−1)!)2​(−λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2​[i​j])m+n−1⟨i​j⟩m+n+1∀m,n≠0.\mathcal{J}_{m,n}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{((m+n-1)!)^{2}}\,\frac{(-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j])^{m+n-1}}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{m+n+1}}\qquad\forall\;m,n\neq 0\,.

(9.67)


This expression also subsumes the case  \mathcal{J}_{0,0}=0 𝒥0,0=0\mathcal{J}_{0,0}=0 because of  1/(-1)!=1/\Gamma(0)=0 1/(−1)!=1/Γ⁡(0)=01/(-1)!=1/\Gamma(0)=0.




Matching the 2-point amplitude.




For the reader’s convenience, let’s reprise the fruits of our calculation in the bulk. The final results for  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n} and  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n} are

(9.68)


Both of these have turned out to be trivially symmetric in  m mm and  n nn.

The importance of finding opposite signs in  \mathcal{I}_{m,n} ℐm,n\mathcal{I}_{m,n} and  \mathcal{J}_{m,n} 𝒥m,n\mathcal{J}_{m,n} now becomes manifest.




Adding (9.70) to (9.71) engenders a final cancellation between the  J_{1} J1J_{1} functions. One thus finds the total 2-point amplitude





 A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=-\frac{N}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{2}}\,J_{0}\bigg(\sqrt{4N\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}})\,, A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)=−N⟨i​j⟩2​J0​(4​N​λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2​[i​j]⟨i​j⟩)​tr​(𝖳ai​𝖳aj),A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j})=-\frac{N}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{2}}\,J_{0}\bigg(\sqrt{4N\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}})\,,

(9.72)


This amplitude has little group weight  -2 −2-2 in each of the two particles, which is consistent with  A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j}) A⁡(ϕi,ϕj)A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j}) being an amplitude of two positive helicity gluons. It agrees with the result of Hawking et al. [74] if one divides their scalar amplitude by a factor of  1/\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2} 1/λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​21/\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2} (that came from the gluon polarizations) and dresses it with a color trace.




Finally, fix the little group freedom by setting  \lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}) λi​α=(1,zi)\lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}),  \lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}) λj​α=(1,zj)\lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}). This yields  \langle i\,j\rangle=z_{ij} ⟨i​j⟩=zi​j\langle i\,j\rangle=z_{ij}. If one also identifies  \mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{pq} 𝖳ai↔𝖳p​q\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{pq},  \mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{rs} 𝖳aj↔𝖳r​s\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{rs}, then this result for the amplitude coincides perfectly with the prediction (9.7) from celestial CFT. This is one of the main results of this paper. It is notable how drastically simpler the chiral algebra calculation was, perhaps proving the merit of the search for more celestial CFTs!






9.2 Celestial OPE in WZW4




Combinatorially, higher-point amplitudes in curved backgrounds like Burns space are controlled by the same Feynman diagrammatics encountered in flat space (for example, Witten diagrams arise as Feynman diagrams for AdS). But calculating them in closed-form is much harder than the calculation of 2-point amplitudes. For this reason, in holographic setups, one often resorts to computing only their collinear limits. These are the limits in which boundary insertions approach each other. So they can be matched onto operator product expansions in the dual theory, providing another independent verification of the holographic duality.



Celestial OPE from the chiral algebra.




Let’s start by obtaining the chiral algebra prediction for the  \phi\phi ϕ​ϕ\phi\phi collinear limit of WZW4. It will again turn out to be a dramatically simpler calculation on the 2d side, and will take up a good chunk of time to describe on the 4d side.




We will compute the OPE of  J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}) Jp​q​(zi,λ~i)J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}) and  J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) Jr​s​(zj,λ~j)J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) to zeroth and first order in  N NN. It is the latter that corresponds to a genuine backreaction effect. The contribution at zeroth order in  N NN comes from single contractions. In the planar limit, it follows from the ADHM constraint (4.9) that we are free to reorder the factors of  [X\,i] [X​i][X\,i] and  [X\,j] [X​j][X\,j] – viewed as  N\times N N×NN\times N matrices – inside the normal ordering. So one finds, up to regular terms,





 \begin{split}J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})&\sim\frac{f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}}\,:\!I_{t}\mathrm{e}^{z_{j}([X\,i]+[X\,j])}I_{u}\!:\!(z_{j})\end{split} Jp​q​(zi,λ~i)​Jr​s​(zj,λ~j)∼fp​q,r​st​uzi​j:It​ezj​([X​i]+[X​j])​Iu:(zj)\begin{split}J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})&\sim\frac{f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}}\,:\!I_{t}\mathrm{e}^{z_{j}([X\,i]+[X\,j])}I_{u}\!:\!(z_{j})\end{split}

(9.73)


This follows because only contractions between the  I II’s survive in the planar limit at order  1/z_{ij} 1/zi​j1/z_{ij}, and a typical contribution looks like the contraction in figure 5(a).






 Refer to caption 
(a) Single contraction


 Refer to caption 
(b) Adjacent double contractions

Figure 5: Planar contractions contributing to the OPE of two gluon currents at zeroth and first order in the backreaction strength  N NN respectively.




Abbreviating the composite indices  pq,rs,\dots p​q,r​s,…pq,rs,\dots by adjoint indices  a,b,\dots a,b,…a,b,\dots, this reduces to





 J_{a}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{b}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{f_{ab}{}^{c}}{z_{ij}}\,J_{c}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) Ja​(zi,λ~i)​Jb​(zj,λ~j)∼fa​bczi​j​Jc​(zj,λ~i+λ~j)J_{a}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{b}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{f_{ab}{}^{c}}{z_{ij}}\,J_{c}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})

(9.74)


which is the momentum space version of the like-helicity gluon celestial OPE that one encounters in flat space [33].




At the next order in  N NN, one Wick contracts a pair of  I II’s, then further contracts a pair of adjacent  X XX’s as shown in figure 5(b). The result may be written as a double sum





 J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\\
\sim-\frac{Nz_{i}z_{j}[i\,j]f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\sum_{m,n\geq 1}\frac{1}{m!n!}\,:\!I_{t}\bigl(z_{i})(z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]\bigr)^{m-1}\bigl(z_{j}[X(z_{j})\,j]\bigr)^{n-1}I_{u}(z_{j})\!: Jp​q​(zi,λ~i)​Jr​s​(zj,λ~j)∼−Nzizj[ij]fp​q,r​st​uzi​j2∑m,n≥11m!​n!:It(zi)(zi[X(zi)i])m−1(zj[X(zj)j])n−1Iu(zj):J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\\
\sim-\frac{Nz_{i}z_{j}[i\,j]f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\sum_{m,n\geq 1}\frac{1}{m!n!}\,:\!I_{t}\bigl(z_{i})(z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]\bigr)^{m-1}\bigl(z_{j}[X(z_{j})\,j]\bigr)^{n-1}I_{u}(z_{j})\!:

(9.75)


where we have suppressed the  1/z_{ij} 1/zi​j1/z_{ij} term for clarity. The right hand side is no longer a single hard gluon state. But using the identity





 \int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{\omega_{i}x+\omega_{j}y}=\sum_{m,n\geq 1}\frac{x^{m-1}y^{n-1}}{m!n!}\,, ∫01d​ωi​∫01d​ωj​eωi​x+ωj​y=∑m,n≥1xm−1​yn−1m!​n!,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{\omega_{i}x+\omega_{j}y}=\sum_{m,n\geq 1}\frac{x^{m-1}y^{n-1}}{m!n!}\,,

(9.76)


it can be expressed as a smearing over hard gluon states and their descendants,





 J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}:\!I_{t}\mathrm{e}^{z_{j}\omega_{i}[X\,i]+z_{j}\omega_{j}[X\,j]}I_{u}\!:\!(z_{j})\\

Jp​q(zi,λ~i)Jr​s(zj,λ~j)∼−Nzj2[ij]fp​q,r​st​uzi​j2∫01dωi∫01dωj:Itezj​ωi​[X​i]+zj​ωj​[X​j]Iu:(zj)−Nzj[ij]fp​q,r​st​uzi​j∫01dωi∫01dωj:∂zj(zjItezj​ωi​[X​i])ezj​ωj​[X​j]Iu:(zj).J_{pq}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{pq,rs}{}^{tu}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}:\!I_{t}\mathrm{e}^{z_{j}\omega_{i}[X\,i]+z_{j}\omega_{j}[X\,j]}I_{u}\!:\!(z_{j})\\

(9.77)


The second line of this expression may be expressed as a combination of conformal and Kac-Moody descendants along the lines of [117, 118]. The integration variables  \omega_{i} ωi\omega_{i} are reminiscent of “Feynman parameters”. We will soon see how they arise as genuine Feynman parameters when computing the celestial OPE through bulk Feynman diagrams.




The first term in (9.77) is the more interesting primary term. It can be succinctly summarized as





 J_{a}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{b}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{ab}{}^{c}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,J_{c}(z_{j},\omega_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\omega_{j}\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,. Ja(zi,λ~i)Jb(zj,λ~j)∼−Nzj2[ij]fa​bczi​j2∫01dωi∫01dωjJc(zj,ωiλ~i+ωjλ~j).J_{a}(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{b}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{ab}{}^{c}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,J_{c}(z_{j},\omega_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\omega_{j}\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,.

(9.78)


This is what we will spend most of our time reproducing from the bulk. The second term in (9.77) is proportional to  [i\,j]/z_{ij} [i​j]/zi​j[i\,j]/z_{ij}, so it is easily disentangled from the contributions (9.74), (9.78) that sit at orders  1/z_{ij} 1/zi​j1/z_{ij} and  [i\,j]/z_{ij}^{2} [i​j]/zi​j2[i\,j]/z_{ij}^{2} respectively. Reproducing it from the bulk is left to future work.




Celestial OPE from collinear limits in the bulk.




The bulk quantity that computes the celestial OPE is what is known as (in the scattering amplitudes literature) the tree level splitting function. The tree level splitting function tells us the collinear singularities in any amplitude or, more generally, in any form factor. Its relevance for celestial holography was realized in [119, 120]. It can also be thought of as a Berends-Giele current [121].




Since WZW4 is a gauge-fixed version of self-dual Yang-Mills, we can identify (for rather formal reasons) the tree level splitting function of WZW4 with the terms in the tree level splitting function of self-dual Yang-Mills theory which only involve positive helicity states. One very concrete statement of one aspect of our duality is then:



Conjecture 3.



The tree level splitting function of self-dual Yang-Mills on Burns space, for gauge group  \mathrm{SO}(8) SO⁡(8)\mathrm{SO}(8), is precisely the OPE of the large  N NN chiral algebra.





Let us now explain how to compute the celestial OPE (or splitting function) from the bulk. The 3-point interactions of WZW4 on a scalar-flat Kähler manifold  M MM were displayed in (2.30). If one starts with three solutions  \phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k} ϕi,ϕj,ϕk\phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k} of the adjoint-valued Laplace equation, their scattering amplitude in WZW4 is found by evaluating the on-shell 3-point vertex





 \frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\right) i24​π2​∫Mω∧tr⁡(ϕ⁡[∂ϕ,∂¯​ϕ])\frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\phi\,[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\right)

(9.79)


on the combination  \phi=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}\phi_{k} ϕ=εi​ϕi+εj​ϕj+εk​ϕk\phi=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}\phi_{k} and extracting the coefficient of  \varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k} εi​εj​εk\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}. Recall that we are using the notation  [\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\equiv\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi+\bar{\partial}\phi\wedge\partial\phi [∂ϕ,∂¯​ϕ]≡∂ϕ∧∂¯​ϕ+∂¯​ϕ∧∂ϕ[\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi]\equiv\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi+\bar{\partial}\phi\wedge\partial\phi as is appropriate for the Lie bracket of 1-forms. Thus, the 3-point amplitude reads





 A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\,\Bigl(\phi_{k}\bigl([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\bigr)+\text{cyclic}\Bigr)\,. A⁡(ϕi,ϕj,ϕk)=i24​π2​∫Mω∧tr⁡(ϕk​([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])+cyclic).A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{24\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\,\Bigl(\phi_{k}\bigl([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\bigr)+\text{cyclic}\Bigr)\,.

(9.80)


Instead of trying to determine this 3-point amplitude in all its glory, we confine ourselves to computing its expansion in small  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle. This defines the so-called holomorphic collinear limit.




We also remark that in past work, we actually have computed this 3-point amplitude to leading order in the backreaction, and the result matches the correlator of three leading soft gluon currents  J[0,0] J⁡[0,0]J[0,0] in the dual chiral algebra. We will omit repeating the details of that approach here, but the interested reader is referred to the supplemental materials of [36] for the computation. Alternatively, an analogous computation involving two gluons  \phi_{i},\phi_{j} ϕi,ϕj\phi_{i},\phi_{j} and a gravitational  E EE-type mode  \rho^{E}_{k} ρkE\rho^{E}_{k} will be performed in the next section.




What we will compute now is the holographic OPE. The holographic OPE of two states  \phi_{i}\equiv\phi_{a_{i}}(x|\lambda_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}) ϕi≡ϕai​(x|λi,λ~i)\phi_{i}\equiv\phi_{a_{i}}(x|\lambda_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}),  \phi_{j} ϕj\phi_{j} is defined to be the “off-shell state”  \phi_{ij} ϕi​j\phi_{ij} such that the linear combination





 \varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{ij} εi​ϕi+εj​ϕj+εi​εj​ϕi​j\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{ij}

(9.81)


solves the nonlinear field equation (2.31) to first order in the product  \varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j} εi​εj\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}. To this order, the field equation reads





 \omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\,. ω∧∂∂¯​ϕi​j=−12​ω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi]).\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\,.

(9.82)


From this, one observes that the “2-point amplitude” of an on-shell state  \phi_{k} ϕk\phi_{k} and the off-shell state  \phi_{ij} ϕi​j\phi_{ij}, a priori defined to be





 A(\phi_{ij},\phi_{k})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{ij}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}+\partial\phi_{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{ij}\right)\,, A(ϕi​j,ϕk)=−i8​π2∫Mω∧tr(∂ϕi​j∧∂¯ϕk+∂ϕk∧∂¯ϕi​j),A(\phi_{ij},\phi_{k})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{ij}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}+\partial\phi_{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{ij}\right)\,,

(9.83)


computes the on-shell 3-point amplitude (9.80) on integrating by parts and summing over cyclic permutations of the particle labels  i,j,k i,j,ki,j,k. This idea is again adapted from the perturbiner formalism [115, 116]. In the flat space limit  N\to 0 N→0N\to 0, the OPE  \phi_{ij} ϕi​j\phi_{ij} reduces to the usual notion of a (holomorphic) collinear splitting function.




In what follows, we will solve the field equation for  \phi_{ij} ϕi​j\phi_{ij} order-by-order in the backreaction. Crucially, this takes the form of a series in  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle whose coefficients can be expressed in terms of on-shell states. Plugging this expansion into (9.83) and summing over permutations, one obtains an expansion of the 3-point amplitude  A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k}) A⁡(ϕi,ϕj,ϕk)A(\phi_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k}) as a series in  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle whose coefficients are on-shell 2-point amplitudes. This is exactly the way a holographic correlator is supposed to behave. And indeed, following this computation, we will apply our holographic dictionary to convert this into a celestial OPE. An independent computation in the chiral algebra will then match this celestial OPE on the nose.




Remembering the expression (7.1) for the Laplacian on Kähler manifolds, along with the normalization convention  \omega^{2}=-2\sqrt{|g|}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x ω2=−2​|g|​d4​x\omega^{2}=-2\sqrt{|g|}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x, one can invert (9.82) to find the solution





 \phi_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G(x,x^{\prime})\,, ϕi​j​(x)=i​∫Mω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])|x′​G​(x,x′),\phi_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G(x,x^{\prime})\,,

(9.84)


where the integral is over spacetime points  x^{\prime}\in M x′∈Mx^{\prime}\in M, and  G(x,x^{\prime}) G⁡(x,x′)G(x,x^{\prime}) is the Green’s function of the Laplacian satisfying





 \triangle_{x}G(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\,\delta^{4}(x-x^{\prime})\,. △x​G​(x,x′)=1|g|​δ4​(x−x′).\triangle_{x}G(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\,\delta^{4}(x-x^{\prime})\,.

(9.85)


We anticipate that the result of the integral (9.84) will be expandable on universal singularities in  \langle i\,j\rangle=z_{ij} ⟨i​j⟩=zi​j\langle i\,j\rangle=z_{ij}, with coefficients that are on-shell states. This “splitting” idea is depicted in figure 6, with the right hand side showing the rough structure of the holographic OPE.

To be clear, the Green’s function here is just the scalar propagator and does not contain any color factors. (9.86)


where  x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}) xα​α˙=(uα˙,u^α˙)x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\dot{\alpha}}) and  x^{\prime\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}) x′α​α˙=(u′α˙,u^′α˙)x^{\prime\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}},\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}), etc. This is the Green’s function of the Laplacian on Burns space. We will use this in conjunction with formula (9.84) to determine our holographic OPE.

with  G_{0}(x,x^{\prime}) G0​(x,x′)G_{0}(x,x^{\prime}) being the flat space propagator

Also recall the expansions (9.20) of the quasi-momentum eigenstates, with coefficients given by (9.17).

Flat space celestial OPE.




The leading term  \phi^{0}_{ij} ϕi​j0\phi^{0}_{ij} should reproduce the momentum space celestial OPE of positive helicity hard gluons that one obtains in flat space [119]. Equivalently, its collinear expansion should reduce to the flat space tree level splitting function.3535

 \displaystyle\phi^{0}_{ij} ϕi​j0\displaystyle\phi^{0}_{ij}

 \displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})}{2\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}}{\langle i\,j\rangle} =(λi​1​λj​2+λi​2​λj​1)2​λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2​ei⁡(pi+pj)⋅x⟨i​j⟩\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})}{2\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}}{\langle i\,j\rangle}

(9.94)


The second line is the exact result. The third line displays the singular term in its holomorphic collinear expansion around  \langle i\,j\rangle=0 ⟨i​j⟩=0\langle i\,j\rangle=0. The weird factors of  \lambda_{i1}/\lambda_{j1} λi​1/λj​1\lambda_{i1}/\lambda_{j1} in various places are present purely to ensure correct little group scalings. We also remark that, as the Fourier transform of  1/(x-x^{\prime})^{2} 1/(x−x′)21/(x-x^{\prime})^{2} is well-known, one can also obtain (9.94) by direct integration of (9.92) for real  p_{i}^{\mu}+p_{j}^{\mu} piμ+pjμp_{i}^{\mu}+p_{j}^{\mu} and analytic continuation to complex momenta.




To recast this as an OPE expansion of a 2d CFT, it is useful to fix the little group scalings by setting  \lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}) λi​α=(1,zi)\lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}),  \lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}) λj​α=(1,zj)\lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}). Expanding in small  z_{ij} zi​jz_{ij} gives the singular part of the leading OPE





 \phi_{ij}\sim\frac{[\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}},\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}]}{z_{ij}}\,\phi^{0}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})+\mathrm{O}(N)\,. ϕi​j∼[𝖳ai,𝖳aj]zi​j​ϕ0​(x|zj,λ~i+λ~j)+O⁡(N).\phi_{ij}\sim\frac{[\mathsf{T}_{a_{i}},\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}]}{z_{ij}}\,\phi^{0}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})+\mathrm{O}(N)\,.

(9.95)


We have denoted by  \phi^{0}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda}) ϕ0​(x|z,λ~)\phi^{0}(x|z,\tilde{\lambda}) the flat space momentum eigenstate  \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle\lambda|x|\tilde{\lambda}]}/\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2} ei⟨λ|x|λ~]/λ1λ2\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle\lambda|x|\tilde{\lambda}]}/\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2} for the little group fixing  \lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z) λα=(1,z)\lambda_{\alpha}=(1,z). This is the expected Kac-Moody OPE of two positive helicity hard gluons. It encodes the celestial OPE of infinitely many soft gluons, as was first realized in [4]. Having obtained consistency with the bottom-up expectations of celestial holography, we are emboldened to embark on computing some backreaction effects!




Absence of branch cuts in the celestial OPE.




Before we embark on the rather challenging computation of the OPE in the backreacted geometry, let us explain why the celestial OPE must always yield a rational functional of the kinematic variables  [i\,j] [i​j][i\,j],  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle. From the CFT point of view this is of course obvious. However, certain intermediate steps in the OPE computation can lead to logarithms, and it is useful to have a general conceptual argument for why they must all cancel in the end.




Recall that the celestial OPE is defined by





 \phi_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G(x,x^{\prime})\,, ϕi​j​(x)=i​∫Mω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])|x′​G​(x,x′),\phi_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G(x,x^{\prime})\,,

(9.96)


We then take the limit as  p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\to 0 pi⋅pj→0p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\to 0 and retain the singular part.




However, we don’t have to use the full Green’s function to compute the celestial OPE. The Green’s function can be written as an integral of the heat kernel:





 G(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,K_{t}(x,x^{\prime}) G⁡(x,x′)=∫0∞d​t​Kt​(x,x′)G(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,K_{t}(x,x^{\prime})

(9.97)


(This is a version of the Schwinger parameterization). We can consider the Green’s function with an infrared cutoff:





 G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}t\,K_{t}(x,x^{\prime}) GT​(x,x′)=∫0Td​t​Kt​(x,x′)G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}t\,K_{t}(x,x^{\prime})

(9.98)


Unlike the actual Green’s function, the IR cutoff Green’s function has exponential decay for  \left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| ‖x−x′‖\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| large:





 G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\sim e^{-\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|^{2}/T} GT(x,x′)∼e−‖x−x′‖2/TG_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\sim e^{-\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|^{2}/T}

(9.99)


We will show that the celestial OPE defined using the infra-red cutoff is the same as the OPE defined using the Green’s function. The OPE is only sensitive to the UV singularities in the Green’s function.




Although we present the argument with the heat-kernel cutoff, it will be clear that any reasonable IR cutoff can be used.




The celestial OPE with the IR cutoff is





 \phi^{T}_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,. ϕi​jT​(x)=i​∫Mω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])|x′​GT​(x,x′).\phi^{T}_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,.

(9.100)


If send  T\to\infty T→∞T\to\infty, obviously it becomes our original expression for the celestial OPE. On the other hand, we can differentiate it with respect to  T TT to get





 \partial_{T}\phi^{T}_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,K_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,. ∂Tϕi​jT​(x)=i​∫Mω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])|x′​KT​(x,x′).\partial_{T}\phi^{T}_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,K_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,.

(9.101)


The heat kernel  K_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) KT​(x,x′)K_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) has rapid decay when  \left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| ‖x−x′‖\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| is large, and (unlike the Green’s function) has no singularities when  \left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| ‖x−x′‖\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| is small. This implies that the integral





 \int_{x}F(x)K_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) ∫xF⁡(x)​KT​(x,x′)\int_{x}F(x)K_{T}(x,x^{\prime})

(9.102)


is absolutely convergent for any function  F FF which grows like  P(x)e^{C\left\|x\right\|} P⁡(x)​eC​‖x‖P(x)e^{C\left\|x\right\|} for any polynomial  P PP and constant  C CC. In particular, the integral





 \mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,K_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,, i​∫Mω∧([∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi])|x′​KT​(x,x′),\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\omega\wedge\left([\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right)\Bigr|_{x^{\prime}}\,K_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\,,

(9.103)


converges absolutely even in the collinear limit  p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\to 0 pi⋅pj→0p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\to 0.




This tells us that the singular part of the celestial OPE, defined with the IR cutoff propagator  G_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) GT​(x,x′)G_{T}(x,x^{\prime}), is independent of  T TT. It remains the same in the  T\to\infty T→∞T\to\infty limit. The singular part is, of course, what we are ultimately interested in.




Let us now verify that the celestial OPE (defined using the IR cutoff propagator) is an entire analytic function of the complex momenta  p_{i} pip_{i}, and so has no branch cuts. Let us look again at equation (9.100). For any value of the momenta, the term depending on the  \phi_{i} ϕi\phi_{i} has growth bounded by an exponential:





 \left\|[\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right\|\leq P(x)e^{C\left\|x\right\|} ‖[∂ϕi,∂¯​ϕj]+[∂ϕj,∂¯​ϕi]‖≤P⁡(x)​eC​‖x‖\left\|[\partial\phi_{i},\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}]+[\partial\phi_{j},\bar{\partial}\phi_{i}]\right\|\leq P(x)e^{C\left\|x\right\|}

(9.104)


for some polynomial  P PP and constant  C CC, which both depend on the momenta  p_{i} pip_{i}.




For large  \left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\| ‖x−x′‖\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|, we have





 G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\leq Ce^{-\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|^{2}/T} GT(x,x′)≤Ce−‖x−x′‖2/TG_{T}(x,x^{\prime})\leq Ce^{-\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|^{2}/T}

(9.105)


for some constant  C CC. Therefore, the integral (9.100) converges absolutely in the IR region  \left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|\gg 0 ‖x−x′‖≫0\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|\gg 0. It also converges in the UV region, simply because  G_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) GT​(x,x′)G_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) is a distribution. This convergence guarantees analyticity of (9.100) as a function of the momenta.




This argument is a version of the familiar Paley-Weiner theorem, in its distributional incarnation proved by Schwartz. This result states that the Fourier transform of a distribution (in this case  G_{T}(x,x^{\prime}) GT​(x,x′)G_{T}(x,x^{\prime})) with sufficiently rapid decay is always an entire analytic function of the momenta.




Celestial OPE with backreaction.




At  p=1 p=1p=1, one encounters three terms in the sum (9.91). Let us list them in order of increasing complexity that we will encounter when evaluating them,





 \displaystyle\phi^{p}_{ij}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}+\mathcal{I}^{2}_{ij}+\mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\,. ϕi​jp=ℐi​j1+ℐi​j2+ℐi​j3.\displaystyle\phi^{p}_{ij}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}+\mathcal{I}^{2}_{ij}+\mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\,.

ℐi​j1=−∫ℝ4u′α˙​u^′β˙‖u′‖4(∂ϕi0∂u′α˙∂ϕj0∂u^′β˙−∂ϕj0∂u′α˙∂ϕi0∂u^′β˙)G0(x,x′)d4x′.\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\frac{u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}{\|u^{\prime}\|^{4}}\,\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}-\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}\bigg)\,G_{0}(x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,.

The integral has again been written as an integral over  \mathbb{R}^{4} ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, meant in the sense of appropriate regularizations at the origin. ℐi​j2=−∫ℝ4ϵα˙​β˙(∂ϕi0∂u′α˙∂ϕj0∂u^′β˙−∂ϕj0∂u′α˙∂ϕi0∂u^′β˙)G1(x,x′)d4x′.\mathcal{I}^{2}_{ij}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}-\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}\bigg)\,G_{1}(x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,.

(9.108)


The final term is the most involved to compute, but also the term that will give rise to the actual backreaction effects in the OPE,





 \mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{1}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}-\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial u^{\prime\dot{\alpha}}}\frac{\partial\phi_{i}^{1}}{\partial\hat{u}^{\prime\dot{\beta}}}-(i\leftrightarrow j)\bigg)\,G_{0}(x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,. (9.109)


We will evaluate all three as series in  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle. Let us also recall the leading and subleading terms in the scattering wavefunctions that we will need:





 \phi_{i}^{0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{i}^{1}(x)=-\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x} ϕi0(x)=ei​pi⋅xλi​1​λi​2,ϕi1(x)=−[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2∫01dsisiei​si​pi⋅x\phi_{i}^{0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{i}^{1}(x)=-\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,s_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}

(9.110)


and similarly for  \phi_{j}^{0},\phi_{j}^{1} ϕj0,ϕj1\phi_{j}^{0},\phi_{j}^{1}.




In the course of evaluating these terms, we will encounter spacetime integrals of the form





 \mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x^{\prime}}}{[(x^{\prime}-x)^{2}/2]^{m}(x^{\prime 2}/2)^{n}}\,,\qquad m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} 𝒮m,n​(k,x)=18​π2​∫d4​x′​ei​k⋅x′[(x′−x)2/2]m​(x′2/2)n,m,n∈ℤ+\mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x^{\prime}}}{[(x^{\prime}-x)^{2}/2]^{m}(x^{\prime 2}/2)^{n}}\,,\qquad m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

 \mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\left(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}-\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\right)\mathcal{S}_{1,2}(p_{i}+p_{j},x) ℐi​j1=1λi​1​λi​2​λj​1​λj​2(λi​1λj​2∂λi​1∂λj​2−λi​2λj​1∂λi​2∂λj​1)𝒮1,2(pi+pj,x)\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\left(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}-\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\right)\mathcal{S}_{1,2}(p_{i}+p_{j},x)

(9.112)


So it is worthwile computing  \mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x) 𝒮m,n​(k,x)\mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x) in some generality.




The integration technique that we apply to compute it is a 4d adaptation of methods developed in [123] to Fourier transform products of 3d Coulomb potentials with differing centers. It is culturally appealing to see that, in this way, our calculations happen to be souped-up cousins of standard calculations performed in quantum chemistry.




One begins by performing a Feynman parametrization to combine the two propagator denominators:





 \mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{\Gamma(m+n)}{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot(x^{\prime}+sx)}}{[(x^{\prime 2}+s(1-s)x^{2})/2]^{m+n}}\,, 𝒮m,n​(k,x)=18​π2​Γ⁡(m+n)Γ⁡(m)​Γ​(n)​∫01d​s​sm−1​(1−s)n−1​∫d4​x′​ei​k⋅(x′+s​x)[(x′2+s⁡(1−s)​x2)/2]m+n,\mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{\Gamma(m+n)}{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot(x^{\prime}+sx)}}{[(x^{\prime 2}+s(1-s)x^{2})/2]^{m+n}}\,,

(9.113)


having shifted  x^{\prime}\mapsto x^{\prime}+sx x′↦x′+s​xx^{\prime}\mapsto x^{\prime}+sx for further simplification. A further Schwinger parametrization allows for the evaluation of the spacetime integral as a Fourier transform of a Gaussian:






 \displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sk\cdot x} 𝒮m,n​(k,x)=18​π2​1Γ⁡(m)​Γ​(n)​∫01d​s​sm−1​(1−s)n−1​ei​s​k⋅x\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{m,n}(k,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sk\cdot x}





 \displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{m+n-1}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x^{\prime}-t(x^{\prime 2}+s(1-s)x^{2})/2} ×∫0∞d​t​tm+n−1​∫d4​x′​ei​k⋅x′−t⁡(x′2+s⁡(1−s)​x2)/2\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{m+n-1}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot x^{\prime}-t(x^{\prime 2}+s(1-s)x^{2})/2}





 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sk\cdot x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{m+n-3}\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts(1-s)x^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-k^{2}/2t}\,. =12​Γ​(m)​Γ​(n)∫01dssm−1(1−s)n−1ei​s​k⋅x∫0∞dttm+n−3e−ts(1−s)x2/2e−k2/2t.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,s^{m-1}\,(1-s)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sk\cdot x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{m+n-3}\,\mathrm{e}^{-ts(1-s)x^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-k^{2}/2t}\,.

(9.114)


In practice, this is the formula for  \mathcal{S}_{m,n} 𝒮m,n\mathcal{S}_{m,n} that we use when evaluating the  \lambda_{i\alpha},\lambda_{j\alpha} λi​α,λj​α\lambda_{i\alpha},\lambda_{j\alpha} derivatives in expressions like (9.112).




The  t tt integral can also be performed explicitly using the Schläfli integral formula for modified Bessel functions of the second kind,





 K_{\nu}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\,\bigg(\frac{z}{2}\bigg)^{\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\nu+1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-t-z^{2}/4t}\,, Kν​(z)=12​(z2)ν​∫0∞d​ttν+1​e−t−z2/4​t,K_{\nu}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\,\bigg(\frac{z}{2}\bigg)^{\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\nu+1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-t-z^{2}/4t}\,,

valid if the real part of  z^{2} z2z^{2} is positive. This yields

 \displaystyle\times\left[s^{2}\bigl(\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}[\hat{u}\,i][u\,j]-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[u\,i][\hat{u}\,j]\bigr)-\frac{\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]^{2}}{{t^{2}}}\,(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})\right. ×[s2(λi​2λj​1[u^i][uj]−λi​1λj​2[ui][u^j])−⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]2t2(λi​1λj​2+λi​2λj​1)\displaystyle\times\left[s^{2}\bigl(\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}[\hat{u}\,i][u\,j]-\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[u\,i][\hat{u}\,j]\bigr)-\frac{\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]^{2}}{{t^{2}}}\,(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1})\right.





 \displaystyle\left.+\;\frac{[i\,j]}{t}\,\Bigl(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\bigl(\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]+1\bigr)+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\bigl(\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i]+\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{j1}[u\,j]+1\bigr)\Bigr)\right]\,. +[i​j]t(λi​1λj​2(isλi​1[ui]+isλj​2[u^j]+1)+λi​2λj​1(isλi​2[u^i]+isλj​1[uj]+1))].\displaystyle\left.+\;\frac{[i\,j]}{t}\,\Bigl(\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}\bigl(\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{i1}[u\,i]+\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{j2}[\hat{u}\,j]+1\bigr)+\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j1}\bigl(\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{i2}[\hat{u}\,i]+\mathrm{i}s\lambda_{j1}[u\,j]+1\bigr)\Bigr)\right]\,.

(9.117)

Terms like  z^{n}\log z zn​log⁡zz^{n}\log z with  n>0 n>0n>0 vanish in the  z\to 0 z→0z\to 0 limit, so will not contribute to the singular part of our OPE computation. Terms like  \log z log⁡z\log z will give rise to  \log\,\langle i\,j\rangle log⁡⟨i​j⟩\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle singularities, but they are destined to cancel among themselves to ensure the absence of branch cuts.3636
                36
                
                
                
              Although this holds quite generally due to our heat kernel arguments, it is good to see that very similar evaluations of the celestial OPE on Eguchi-Hanson display similar cancellations of the logs [35]. We thank Roland Bittleston for bringing this to our attention. The remaining contributions of interest will be generated by the terms containing non-logarithmic poles.




Let’s use these expansions to study  \mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij} ℐi​j1\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij}. On the other hand, the third line only generates a mild logarithmic singularity, leading to the asymptotics

that any state on the right hand side of our OPEs would carry. It equals the exchanged momentum  p_{i}+p_{j} pi+pjp_{i}+p_{j} modulo terms of order  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle.




Since it only contains a logarithmic singularity, this result for  \mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij} ℐi​j1\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij} does not contribute to the celestial OPE. This is because, by our heat kernel arguments earlier, the final answer for the celestial OPE cannot have branch cuts. And more generally, we expect the logarithmic singularities to not contribute to on-shell observables. Hence, we choose to neglect terms involving  \log\,\langle i\,j\rangle log⁡⟨i​j⟩\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle and only focus on rational singularities. The twistorial arguments of section 6.3 show that any branch cuts must also drop out from actual 3-point amplitudes, but it will be interesting to verify this explicitly.

The main difference between this term and the previous two terms is that now the derivatives have produced a Bessel  K_{2} K2K_{2}. It is this  K_{2} K2K_{2} function whose series expansion will finally reproduce the anticipated  1/\langle i\,j\rangle^{2} 1/⟨i​j⟩21/\langle i\,j\rangle^{2} singularity in the OPE.




To show this, use the series expansion  K_{2}(z)\sim 2/z^{2} K2​(z)∼2/z2K_{2}(z)\sim 2/z^{2} around  z=0 z=0z=0 mentioned in (9.121). It is clear from (9.131) that the terms containing  K_{1} K1K_{1} or  K_{0} K0K_{0} would expand into singularities of the type  [i\,j]/\langle i\,j\rangle [i​j]/⟨i​j⟩[i\,j]/\langle i\,j\rangle or  \log\,\langle i\,j\rangle log⁡⟨i​j⟩\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle. As we are only interested in extracting the  [i\,j]/\langle i\,j\rangle^{2} [i​j]/⟨i​j⟩2[i\,j]/\langle i\,j\rangle^{2} and  1/\langle i\,j\rangle 1/⟨i​j⟩1/\langle i\,j\rangle singularities, we suppress the rest to find the small  \langle i\,j\rangle ⟨i​j⟩\langle i\,j\rangle expansion





 \mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\sim-\frac{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{2}}\left\{\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,\mathrm{d}s\,s\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}+\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,\mathrm{d}s\,s\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}\right\}\\
+\mathrm{O}\bigg(\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle},\,\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle\bigg)\,. ℐi​j3∼−λi​1​λj​2​[i​j]⟨i​j⟩2​{∫[0,1]2d​si​ds​s​ei​s​(si​pi+pj)⋅x+∫[0,1]2d​sj​ds​s​ei​s​(pi+sj​pj)⋅x}+O⁡([i​j]⟨i​j⟩,log⁡⟨i​j⟩).\mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\sim-\frac{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{2}}\left\{\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,\mathrm{d}s\,s\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}+\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,\mathrm{d}s\,s\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}\right\}\\

 \mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\sim-\frac{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle^{2}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\omega_{i}p_{i}+\omega_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}+\mathrm{O}\bigg(\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle},\,\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle\bigg)\,. (9.133)


Substituting the little group fixed spinor-helicity variables  \lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}) λi​α=(1,zi)\lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}), etc., this reduces to the expected collinear singularity





 \mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij}\sim-\frac{z_{j}^{2}[i\,j]}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\omega_{i}p_{i}+\omega_{j}p_{j})\cdot x}}{z_{j}}+\mathrm{O}\bigg(\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}},\,\log\,\langle i\,j\rangle\bigg)\,. (9.134)


Since the result (9.122) for  \mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij} ℐi​j1\mathcal{I}^{1}_{ij} only contained a log singularity, all that’s left is to put  \mathcal{I}^{2}_{ij} ℐi​j2\mathcal{I}^{2}_{ij} and  \mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij} ℐi​j3\mathcal{I}^{3}_{ij} together.




Combining the result (9.95) for the order  N^{0} N0N^{0} OPE with the results (9.129) and (9.133), and fixing the little group scalings, we obtain the backreacted celestial OPE,





 \phi_{ij}\sim\frac{f_{a_{i}a_{j}}{}^{b}}{z_{ij}}\,\phi_{b}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{a_{i}a_{j}}{}^{b}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\phi_{b}(x|z_{j},\omega_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\omega_{j}\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,. ϕi​j∼fai​ajbzi​j​ϕb​(x|zj,λ~i+λ~j)−Nzj2[ij]fai​ajbzi​j2​∫01d​ωi​∫01d​ωj​ϕb​(x|zj,ωi​λ~i+ωj​λ~j).\phi_{ij}\sim\frac{f_{a_{i}a_{j}}{}^{b}}{z_{ij}}\,\phi_{b}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})-\frac{Nz_{j}^{2}[i\,j]f_{a_{i}a_{j}}{}^{b}}{z_{ij}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\,\phi_{b}(x|z_{j},\omega_{i}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\omega_{j}\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,.

(9.135)


valid up to terms of order  N^{2} N2N^{2} or terms proportional to  [i\,j]/z_{ij} [i​j]/zi​j[i\,j]/z_{ij}. Comparing this with the OPEs (9.74), (9.78) obtained from 2d Wick contractions, we find a perfect match with the predictions of our celestial CFT.






9.3 Tests involving gravitational modes




Up till now, we have only computed observables involving gluon degrees of freedom  \phi ϕ\phi. A similar calculation of two and three point amplitudes involving only the Kähler perturbations  \rho ρ\rho is tough, so for the purposes of this work, we focus on 3-point scattering of two gluon modes against an  E EE-type gravitational mode.




In what follows, we will compute the OPE of a gravitational mode  \rho^{E}_{i} ρiE\rho^{E}_{i} with a gluon mode  \phi_{j} ϕj\phi_{j} using the dual chiral algebra. We will show that this matches the OPE computed from solving the equations of motion of WZW4 coupled to Mabuchi gravity, although we will not venture beyond the simple pole  1/z_{ij} 1/zi​j1/z_{ij} for now. Following this, we will explicitly compute the 3-point amplitude to order  N NN and show that it takes the expected form.



 \rho\phi\to\phi ρ​ϕ→ϕ\rho\phi\to\phi OPE and 3-point amplitude from the chiral algebra.




The operator dual to  \rho_{i}^{E} ρiE\rho_{i}^{E} is





 E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})=\frac{1}{z_{i}^{2}}\bigl\{\text{Tr}\exp\!\left(z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]\right)-1\bigr\}\,. E⁡(zi,λ~i)=1zi2​{Tr​exp⁡(zi​[X⁡(zi)​i])−1}.E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})=\frac{1}{z_{i}^{2}}\bigl\{\text{Tr}\exp\!\left(z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]\right)-1\bigr\}\,.

(9.136)


Its OPE with a hard gluon operator  J(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) J⁡(zj,λ~j)J(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j}) to zeroth order in  N NN is computed by performing a single  XX X​XXX Wick contraction,





 E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{z_{i}z_{j}}{z_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}:\!I_{r}(z_{j})\mathrm{e}^{z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]+z_{j}[X(z_{j})\,j]}I_{s}(z_{j})\!:+\cdots\,. E(zi,λ~i)Jr​s(zj,λ~j)∼zi​zjzi2[i​j]zi​j:Ir(zj)ezi​[X⁡(zi)​i]+zj​[X⁡(zj)​j]Is(zj):+⋯.E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{z_{i}z_{j}}{z_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}:\!I_{r}(z_{j})\mathrm{e}^{z_{i}[X(z_{i})\,i]+z_{j}[X(z_{j})\,j]}I_{s}(z_{j})\!:+\cdots\,.

(9.137)


An example of this is depicted in double-line notation in figure 7.

 Refer to caption 
Figure 7: Scattering gluons off Mabuchi gravitons: a view from the boundary. Such  XX X​XXX Wick contractions contribute to the  E\,J\sim J E​J∼JE\,J\sim J OPE. Each line denotes a contraction on the  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) indices. There are no closed loops or factors of  N NN generated at the displayed order.




In simplifying this, we have freely commuted factors of  [X(z_{i})\,i] [X⁡(zi)​i][X(z_{i})\,i] past factors of  [X(z_{j})\,j] [X⁡(zj)​j][X(z_{j})\,j] inside the normal ordering. This uses the fact that  X^{\dot{1}},X^{\dot{2}} X1˙,X2˙X^{\dot{1}},X^{\dot{2}} – viewed as  N\times N N×NN\times N matrices – commute in the large  N NN limit due to the ADHM constraint (4.9). Expanding around  z_{ij}=0 zi​j=0z_{ij}=0 yields the OPE





 E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})+\mathrm{O}(N)\,. E⁡(zi,λ~i)​Jr​s​(zj,λ~j)∼[i​j]zi​j​Jr​s​(zj,λ~i+λ~j)+O⁡(N).E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\sim\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,J_{rs}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})+\mathrm{O}(N)\,.

(9.138)


One can also compute the OPE to higher order in  N NN, but reproducing it from the bulk promises to be a daunting task.




It is a straightforward matter to evaluate the 3-point amplitude  A(\rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j},\phi_{k}) A⁡(ρiE,ϕj,ϕk)A(\rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j},\phi_{k}) to leading order in  N NN using such Wick contractions. Since we subtracted off  E[0,0]=1 E⁡[0,0]=1E[0,0]=1 when defining  E(z,\tilde{\lambda}) E⁡(z,λ~)E(z,\tilde{\lambda}) in (8.17), the 3-point correlator again starts at first order in  N NN,





 \bigl\langle E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{pq}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,J_{rs}(z_{k},\tilde{\lambda}_{k})\bigr\rangle\\
=\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}\,\bigl\langle\text{Tr}\,[X\,i](z_{i})\,:\!I_{p}[X\,j]I_{q}\!:\!(z_{j})\,:\!I_{r}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{k})\bigr\rangle\\
+\frac{z_{k}}{z_{i}}\,\bigl\langle\text{Tr}\,[X\,i](z_{i})\,:\!I_{p}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{j})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,k]I_{s}\!:\!(z_{k})\bigr\rangle+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,. ⟨E⁡(zi,λ~i)​Jp​q​(zj,λ~j)​Jr​s​(zk,λ~k)⟩=zjzi⟨Tr[Xi](zi):Ip[Xj]Iq:(zj):IrIs:(zk)⟩+zkzi⟨Tr[Xi](zi):IpIq:(zj):Ir[Xk]Is:(zk)⟩+O(N2).\bigl\langle E(z_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\,J_{pq}(z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,J_{rs}(z_{k},\tilde{\lambda}_{k})\bigr\rangle\\
=\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}\,\bigl\langle\text{Tr}\,[X\,i](z_{i})\,:\!I_{p}[X\,j]I_{q}\!:\!(z_{j})\,:\!I_{r}I_{s}\!:\!(z_{k})\bigr\rangle\\
+\frac{z_{k}}{z_{i}}\,\bigl\langle\text{Tr}\,[X\,i](z_{i})\,:\!I_{p}I_{q}\!:\!(z_{j})\,:\!I_{r}[X\,k]I_{s}\!:\!(z_{k})\bigr\rangle+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,.

(9.139)


Performing maximal Wick contractions using the OPE (4.6) that keeps the center of mass modes in play, one finds the following prediction for the amplitude:





 A(\rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})}{z_{jk}^{2}}\left\{\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}+\frac{[i\,k]}{z_{ik}}\,\frac{z_{k}}{z_{i}}\right\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,, A⁡(ρiE,ϕj,ϕk)=−N​tr​(𝖳aj​𝖳ak)zj​k2​{[i​j]zi​j​zjzi+[i​k]zi​k​zkzi}+O⁡(N2),A(\rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})}{z_{jk}^{2}}\left\{\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,\frac{z_{j}}{z_{i}}+\frac{[i\,k]}{z_{ik}}\,\frac{z_{k}}{z_{i}}\right\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,,

(9.140)


having identified  \mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{pq} 𝖳aj↔𝖳p​q\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{pq},  \mathsf{T}_{a_{k}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{rs} 𝖳ak↔𝖳r​s\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{T}_{rs}, and used (9.3) to identify the color factor as a trace. This brings us to the bulk computation.




 \rho\phi\to\phi ρ​ϕ→ϕ\rho\phi\to\phi OPE from the bulk.




To see this OPE emerging from the collinear splitting function in the bulk, we simply repeat the strategy of the previous section. As was explained in section 2.3, the WZW4 model couples to Kähler perturbations by the shift  \omega\mapsto\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho ω↦ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ\omega\mapsto\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho:





 S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\rho]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int\left(\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho\right)\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi\right)+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{3})\,, SWZW4[ϕ,ρ]=−i8​π2∫(ω+i∂∂¯ρ)∧tr(∂ϕ∧∂¯ϕ)+O(ϕ3),S_{\text{WZW}_{4}}[\phi,\rho]=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{8\pi^{2}}\int\left(\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho\right)\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi\right)+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{3})\,,

(9.141)


where  \omega ω\omega is the Kähler form of Burns space. The equation of motion of  \phi ϕ\phi, including the  \rho\phi^{2} ρ​ϕ2\rho\phi^{2} coupling, now takes the form





 \omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{2})\,. ω∧∂∂¯ϕ=−i∂∂¯ρ∧∂∂¯ϕ+O(ϕ2).\omega\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho\wedge\partial\bar{\partial}\phi+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{2})\,.

(9.142)


The right hand side gives rise to the  \rho\phi ρ​ϕ\rho\phi OPE.




The holographic OPE  \phi_{ij} ϕi​j\phi_{ij} of a WZW4 state  \phi_{j} ϕj\phi_{j} with a Kähler perturbation  \rho^{E}_{i} ρiE\rho^{E}_{i} is defined by demanding that the linear combinations





 \rho=\varepsilon_{i}\rho_{i}^{E}\,,\qquad\phi=\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{ij} ρ=εi​ρiE,ϕ=εj​ϕj+εi​εj​ϕi​j\rho=\varepsilon_{i}\rho_{i}^{E}\,,\qquad\phi=\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{j}+\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\phi_{ij}

(9.143)


solve the  \phi ϕ\phi equation of motion at order  \varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j} εi​εj\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}. This leads to the PDE





 \triangle\phi_{ij}=-2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho^{E}_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\frac{\partial^{2}\phi_{j}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\,, △​ϕi​j=−2​∂2ρiE∂uα˙​∂u^β˙​∂2ϕj∂uα˙​∂u^β˙,\triangle\phi_{ij}=-2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho^{E}_{i}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\frac{\partial^{2}\phi_{j}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\,,

(9.144)


which can be solved iteratively in  N NN as in the previous section.




We will compute the OPE only to zeroth order in  N NN, so we are free to replace  \triangle △\triangle and  \rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j} ρiE,ϕj\rho_{i}^{E},\phi_{j} by their flat space counterparts. Plugging in





 \rho^{E}_{i}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,, ρiE=ei​pi⋅xλi​12​λi​22,ϕj=𝖳aj​ei​pj⋅xλj​1​λj​2,\rho^{E}_{i}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,,

(9.145)


one immediately finds the solution





 \phi_{ij}=\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\,\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,. ϕi​j=[i​j]⟨i​j⟩​𝖳aj​ei⁡(pi+pj)⋅xλi​1​λi​2.\phi_{ij}=\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\,\frac{\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot x}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,.

(9.146)


Setting  \lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}) λi​α=(1,zi)\lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}),  \lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}) λj​α=(1,zj)\lambda_{j\alpha}=(1,z_{j}), and expanding in small  z_{ij} zi​jz_{ij}, one finds the holographic OPE





 \phi_{ij}\sim\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,\phi_{a_{j}}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,. ϕi​j∼[i​j]zi​j​ϕaj​(x|zj,λ~i+λ~j).\phi_{ij}\sim\frac{[i\,j]}{z_{ij}}\,\phi_{a_{j}}(x|z_{j},\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\,.

(9.147)


This matches the prediction of the celestial chiral algebra.




More singular terms in  z_{ij} zi​jz_{ij} will enter at higher powers of  N NN (with each term receiving its own set of  1/N 1/N1/N loop corrections as usual). Their determination will be a very useful test of our duality, but they lie beyond the scope of this paper. In the rest of this section, we instead focus on obtaining this OPE in a different way: by directly computing the order  N NN term in the 3-point  \rho\phi\phi ρ​ϕ​ϕ\rho\phi\phi amplitude.




 \rho\phi\phi ρ​ϕ​ϕ\rho\phi\phi 3-point amplitude.




Using the perturbiner formalism as before, the amplitude for scattering a gravitational mode  \rho^{E}_{i} ρiE\rho^{E}_{i} against two gluon modes  \phi_{j},\phi_{k} ϕj,ϕk\phi_{j},\phi_{k} is given by the symmetrized on-shell interaction vertex [124]





 A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E}_{i}\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}+\partial\phi_{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}\right)\,. A⁡(ρiE,ϕj,ϕk)=18​π2​∫ℂ~2∂∂¯​ρiE∧tr⁡(∂ϕj∧∂¯​ϕk+∂ϕk∧∂¯​ϕj).A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E}_{i}\wedge\mathrm{tr}\left(\partial\phi_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}+\partial\phi_{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}\right)\,.

(9.148)


If we evaluate this as a series in  N NN, we find that just like the 3-point  \phi\phi\phi ϕ​ϕ​ϕ\phi\phi\phi amplitude, the zeroth order term is the flat space 3-point amplitude which is distributional (see the supplemental material to [36]). It only finds support at  z_{i}=z_{j}=z_{k} zi=zj=zkz_{i}=z_{j}=z_{k}. We will work at generic kinematics  z_{i}\neq z_{j}\neq z_{k} zi≠zj≠zkz_{i}\neq z_{j}\neq z_{k} and drop this term.3737
                37
                
                
                
              As always with distributional terms, it is unclear how to obtain it as a local chiral algebra correlator. The first term of interest is then the term of order  N NN. This is what we will compute below.




Expand  \rho_{i}^{E}=\rho_{i}^{E,0}+N\rho_{i}^{E,1}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2}) ρiE=ρiE,0+N​ρiE,1+O⁡(N2)\rho_{i}^{E}=\rho_{i}^{E,0}+N\rho_{i}^{E,1}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2}),  \phi_{j}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}(\phi_{j}^{0}+N\phi_{j}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})) ϕj=𝖳aj​(ϕj0+N​ϕj1+O⁡(N2))\phi_{j}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}(\phi_{j}^{0}+N\phi_{j}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})), etc. For the Kähler perturbation, the coefficients are read off from (7.74) (up to the usual subtraction by  1 11 to remove  E[0,0] E⁡[0,0]E[0,0]),





 \rho_{i}^{E,0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}-1}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}}\,,\qquad\rho_{i}^{E,1}(x)=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,. ρiE,0(x)=ei​pi⋅x−1λi​12​λi​22,ρiE,1(x)=−1λi​1​λi​2[u​i]​[u^​i]‖u‖2∫01dsiei​si​pi⋅x.\rho_{i}^{E,0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x}-1}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}}\,,\qquad\rho_{i}^{E,1}(x)=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\,\frac{[u\,i][\hat{u}\,i]}{\|u\|^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{i}p_{i}\cdot x}\,.

(9.149)


The first is the flat space wavefunction, while the second is the leading order backreaction. The  {}_{1}F_{1}(1,2\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x) F11​(1,2|i​pi⋅x){}_{1}F_{1}(1,2\,|\,\mathrm{i}p_{i}\cdot x) factor in the latter has been expressed through its integral representation as usual. For the reader’s convenience, let us also repeat the corresponding terms in the  \phi ϕ\phi wavefunctions,





 \phi_{j}^{0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}^{1}(x)=-\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\,. ϕj0(x)=ei​pj⋅xλj​1​λj​2,ϕj1(x)=−[u​j]​[u^​j]‖u‖2∫01dsjsjei​sj​pj⋅x.\phi_{j}^{0}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{j}\cdot x}}{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}\,,\qquad\phi_{j}^{1}(x)=-\frac{[u\,j][\hat{u}\,j]}{\|u\|^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,s_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s_{j}p_{j}\cdot x}\,.

(9.150)


Similar expressions for  \phi_{k}^{0},\phi_{k}^{1} ϕk0,ϕk1\phi_{k}^{0},\phi_{k}^{1} are obtained by replacing  j jj with  k kk.




The 3-point amplitude can be broken down into two types of terms,





 A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})\,\bigl\{\mathcal{I}_{ijk}+\mathcal{J}_{ijk}+(j\leftrightarrow k)\bigr\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,. A⁡(ρiE,ϕj,ϕk)=N​tr​(𝖳aj​𝖳ak)​{ℐi​j​k+𝒥i​j​k+(j↔k)}+O⁡(N2).A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})\,\bigl\{\mathcal{I}_{ijk}+\mathcal{J}_{ijk}+(j\leftrightarrow k)\bigr\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,.

(9.151)


The first term is an integral containing the backreaction associated to  \rho^{E}_{i} ρiE\rho^{E}_{i},





 \mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E,1}_{i}\wedge\partial\phi_{j}^{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}^{0}\,. ℐi​j​k=18​π2​∫ℂ~2∂∂¯​ρiE,1∧∂ϕj0∧∂¯​ϕk0.\mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E,1}_{i}\wedge\partial\phi_{j}^{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}^{0}\,.

(9.152)


The second term encodes the backreaction coming from the WZW4 fields,





 \mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E,0}_{i}\wedge\left(\partial\phi_{j}^{1}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}^{0}+\partial\phi_{k}^{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}^{1}\right)\,. 𝒥i​j​k=18​π2​∫ℂ~2∂∂¯​ρiE,0∧(∂ϕj1∧∂¯​ϕk0+∂ϕk0∧∂¯​ϕj1).\mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}\partial\bar{\partial}\rho^{E,0}_{i}\wedge\left(\partial\phi_{j}^{1}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{k}^{0}+\partial\phi_{k}^{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi_{j}^{1}\right)\,.

(9.153)


As we have already developed quite a bit of technology while computing the 2-point amplitude, evaluating these integrals proves to be relatively straightforward.




Let us start with (9.152). On the coordinate patch  u^{\dot{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}-0 uα˙∈ℂ2−0u^{\dot{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}-0, it expands out to





 \mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}^{E,1}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\,. ℐi​j​k=18​π2​∫d4​x​∂2ρiE,1∂uα˙​∂u^β˙​∂ϕj0∂uα˙​∂ϕk0∂u^β˙.\mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}^{E,1}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{0}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\,.

(9.154)


Plugging in the various wavefunctions from (9.149) and (9.150), and remembering the intertwining trick (9.27), this can be reduced to





 \mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j2}\lambda_{k1}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\left\{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}[i\,j][i\,k]\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(P)+\frac{\lambda_{i1}[i\,j]}{s_{i}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda_{k2}}\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)\right.\\
\left.-\frac{\lambda_{i2}[i\,k]}{s_{i}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)-\frac{2}{s_{i}^{2}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\partial_{\lambda_{k2}}\mathcal{S}_{3}(P)\right\}\biggr|_{P=s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k}}\,. ℐi​j​k=1λi​1​λi​2​λj​2​λk​1∫01dsi{λi​1λi​2[ij][ik]∂λi​1∂λi​2𝒮1(P)+λi​1​[i​j]si∂λi​12∂λk​2𝒮2(P)−λi​2​[i​k]si∂λi​22∂λj​1𝒮2(P)−2si2∂λi​1∂λi​2∂λj​1∂λk​2𝒮3(P)}|P=si​pi+pj+pk.\mathcal{I}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\lambda_{j2}\lambda_{k1}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\left\{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}[i\,j][i\,k]\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(P)+\frac{\lambda_{i1}[i\,j]}{s_{i}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda_{k2}}\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)\right.\\
\left.-\frac{\lambda_{i2}[i\,k]}{s_{i}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)-\frac{2}{s_{i}^{2}}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\partial_{\lambda_{k2}}\mathcal{S}_{3}(P)\right\}\biggr|_{P=s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k}}\,.

(9.155)


We have again abbreviated the spacetime integrals by defining





 \mathcal{S}_{\ell}(P)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}P\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{2\,\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tP^{2}/2}\,. 𝒮ℓ(P)=18​π2∫d4​x​ei​P⋅x(x2/2)ℓ=12​Γ​(ℓ)∫0∞d​ttℓ−1e−tP2/2.\mathcal{S}_{\ell}(P)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}P\cdot x}}{(x^{2}/2)^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{2\,\Gamma(\ell)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\ell-1}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-tP^{2}/2}\,.

(9.156)


Their value has been obtained using (9.34).




In our case,  \frac{1}{2}P^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}=s_{i}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]+s_{i}\langle i\,k\rangle[i\,k]+\langle j\,k\rangle[j\,k] 12​P2=12​(si​pi+pj+pk)2=si​⟨i​j⟩​[i​j]+si​⟨i​k⟩​[i​k]+⟨j​k⟩​[j​k]\frac{1}{2}P^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}=s_{i}\langle i\,j\rangle[i\,j]+s_{i}\langle i\,k\rangle[i\,k]+\langle j\,k\rangle[j\,k]. As in the previous sections, the  t tt integral is understood in the sense of analytic continuation: evaluated for  P^{2}>0 P2>0P^{2}>0, then continued to complex  P^{\mu} PμP^{\mu} away from the singular locus  P^{2}=0 P2=0P^{2}=0. Thereby performing the spacetime integrals as well as the  t tt and  s_{i} sis_{i} integrals, one finds a simple if somewhat obscure expression





 \mathcal{I}_{ijk}=-\frac{(\lambda_{j1}[i\,j]+\lambda_{k1}[i\,k])(\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]+\lambda_{k2}[i\,k])}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\,\langle j\,k\rangle^{2}}\,\frac{(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}+(p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}}{(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4}}\,. ℐi​j​k=−(λj​1​[i​j]+λk​1​[i​k])​(λj​2​[i​j]+λk​2​[i​k])λi​1​λi​2​⟨j​k⟩2​(pi+pj+pk)2+(pj+pk)2(pi+pj+pk)4.\mathcal{I}_{ijk}=-\frac{(\lambda_{j1}[i\,j]+\lambda_{k1}[i\,k])(\lambda_{j2}[i\,j]+\lambda_{k2}[i\,k])}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\,\langle j\,k\rangle^{2}}\,\frac{(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}+(p_{j}+p_{k})^{2}}{(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4}}\,.

(9.157)


We will soon see that it drastically simplifies when combined with  \mathcal{J}_{ijk} 𝒥i​j​k\mathcal{J}_{ijk}.




The second term (9.153) can also be written in coordinates as





 \mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}^{E,0}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{1}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}+\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{1}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\bigg)\,. 𝒥i​j​k=18​π2​∫d4​x​∂2ρiE,0∂uα˙​∂u^β˙​(∂ϕj1∂uα˙​∂ϕk0∂u^β˙+∂ϕk0∂uα˙​∂ϕj1∂u^β˙).\mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{i}^{E,0}}{\partial u^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial\hat{u}^{\dot{\beta}}}\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{1}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}+\frac{\partial\phi_{k}^{0}}{\partial u_{\dot{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\partial\phi_{j}^{1}}{\partial\hat{u}_{\dot{\beta}}}\bigg)\,.

(9.158)


With the aid of (9.149), (9.150), this is found to reduce to





 \mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}\lambda_{k1}\lambda_{k2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,\bigg\{[i\,j][i\,k](\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{k2}+\lambda_{j2}\lambda_{k1})\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(P)\\
+\frac{[i\,k]}{s_{j}}\big(\lambda_{k2}\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{2}-\lambda_{k1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{2}\big)\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)\bigg\}\biggr|_{P=p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j}+p_{k}}\,. 𝒥i​j​k=1λi​12​λi​22​λk​1​λk​2∫01dsj{[ij][ik](λj​1λk​2+λj​2λk​1)∂λj​1∂λj​2𝒮1(P)+[i​k]sj(λk​2∂λi​1∂λj​22−λk​1∂λi​2∂λj​12)𝒮2(P)}|P=pi+sj​pj+pk.\mathcal{J}_{ijk}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i1}^{2}\lambda_{i2}^{2}\lambda_{k1}\lambda_{k2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\,\bigg\{[i\,j][i\,k](\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{k2}+\lambda_{j2}\lambda_{k1})\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(P)\\
+\frac{[i\,k]}{s_{j}}\big(\lambda_{k2}\partial_{\lambda_{i1}}\partial_{\lambda_{j2}}^{2}-\lambda_{k1}\partial_{\lambda_{i2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j1}}^{2}\big)\mathcal{S}_{2}(P)\bigg\}\biggr|_{P=p_{i}+s_{j}p_{j}+p_{k}}\,.

(9.159)


Performing all the integrals, we are left with





 \mathcal{J}_{ijk}=-\frac{4\,[i\,k]\,(\lambda_{i1}[j\,i]+\lambda_{k1}[j\,k])(\lambda_{i2}[j\,i]+\lambda_{k2}[j\,k])}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\,\langle i\,k\rangle\,(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4}}\,. 𝒥i​j​k=−4​[i​k]​(λi​1​[j​i]+λk​1​[j​k])​(λi​2​[j​i]+λk​2​[j​k])λi​1​λi​2​⟨i​k⟩​(pi+pj+pk)4.\mathcal{J}_{ijk}=-\frac{4\,[i\,k]\,(\lambda_{i1}[j\,i]+\lambda_{k1}[j\,k])(\lambda_{i2}[j\,i]+\lambda_{k2}[j\,k])}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}\,\langle i\,k\rangle\,(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4}}\,.

(9.160)


With this, we have all the ingredients we need.




Substituting (9.157) and (9.160) into (9.151), and symmetrizing over  j\leftrightarrow k j↔kj\leftrightarrow k as instructed, one finds dramatic cancellations. The final result for the 3-point amplitude collapses to





 A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})}{\langle j\,k\rangle^{2}}\left\{\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\frac{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}+\frac{[i\,k]}{\langle i\,k\rangle}\frac{\lambda_{k1}\lambda_{k2}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\right\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,. A⁡(ρiE,ϕj,ϕk)=−N​tr​(𝖳aj​𝖳ak)⟨j​k⟩2​{[i​j]⟨i​j⟩​λj​1​λj​2λi​1​λi​2+[i​k]⟨i​k⟩​λk​1​λk​2λi​1​λi​2}+O⁡(N2).A(\rho^{E}_{i},\phi_{j},\phi_{k})=-\frac{N\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathsf{T}_{a_{j}}\mathsf{T}_{a_{k}})}{\langle j\,k\rangle^{2}}\left\{\frac{[i\,j]}{\langle i\,j\rangle}\frac{\lambda_{j1}\lambda_{j2}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}+\frac{[i\,k]}{\langle i\,k\rangle}\frac{\lambda_{k1}\lambda_{k2}}{\lambda_{i1}\lambda_{i2}}\right\}+\mathrm{O}(N^{2})\,.

(9.161)


An easy check is that this has little group weight  -4 −4-4 in  i ii, and weight  -2 −2-2 in  j,k j,kj,k each. Yet again, this matches the chiral algebra 3-point prediction (9.140) if one sets  \lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}) λi​α=(1,zi)\lambda_{i\alpha}=(1,z_{i}), etc.




It is remarkable that the 3-particle singularities  1/(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4} 1/(pi+pj+pk)41/(p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k})^{4} completely drop out, leaving one with only 2-particle singularities that a CFT can generate. Of course, one also finds natural singularities at the defect loci  z_{i}=0,\infty zi=0,∞z_{i}=0,\infty, but at this stage they result purely from dressing the boundary operators with factors of  z_{i} ziz_{i} to ensure the correct scaling dimensions.3838
                38
                
                
                
              The expression (9.161) is reminiscent of Hodges’ formula [125] for MHV graviton amplitudes in flat space, except here the reference spinors are the defect locations, and they do not drop out because we no longer have momentum conservation. It would be interesting to find genuine defect effects by matching nontrivial defect conformal blocks of the chiral algebra to form-factors of operators wrapping the core of Burns space.
