(B.16)


displayed in (2.26).





B.2 BCOV theory




The reduction of gravitational theories like BCOV theory to spacetime is much harder in practice. Usually, one argues equivalence of BCOV to Mabuchi gravity by showing that their equations of motion coincide. The normalization of the spacetime action is then evaluated in terms of the normalization of the twistor action by working in the free limit and KK reducing just the kinetic terms along the twistor lines.




In the rest of this appendix, we provide a brief summary of the on-shell equivalence. The interested reader is referred to [15] for more details, as well as for a determination of the normalization from a Green-Schwarz mechanism.4040
              40
              
              
              
            This is done in a flat space background, but the normalization of the action is of course background independent. A similar but more explicit calculation may also be found in [127], where the off-shell reduction of the twistor action of self-dual conformal gravity is worked out. This is relevant to Mabuchi gravity because Mabuchi gravity is just a gauge fixed Kähler subsector of self-dual conformal gravity.




Let  Z ZZ, with Dolbeault operator  \bar{\partial} ∂¯\bar{\partial}, be the twistor space of a scalar-flat Kähler 4-manifold  (M,g) (M,g)(M,g) as usual. BCOV theory [63] deals with complex structure deformations  \bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu ∂¯↦∂¯+μ\bar{\partial}\mapsto\bar{\partial}+\mu that are integrable (on-shell) and divergence-free (via an off-shell constraint):





 \bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\,[\mu,\mu]=0\,,\qquad\partial(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)=0\,, ∂¯​μ+12​[μ,μ]=0,∂(μ​⌟​Ω)=0,\bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\,[\mu,\mu]=0\,,\qquad\partial(\mu\,\lrcorner\,\Omega)=0\,,

(B.17)


where  \Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2}\tau\wedge\theta^{\dot{1}}\wedge\theta^{\dot{2}} Ω=ωˇ−2​τ∧θ1˙∧θ2˙\Omega=\check{\omega}^{-2}\tau\wedge\theta^{\dot{1}}\wedge\theta^{\dot{2}} as before. Assuming that  \mu μ\mu is on-shell, we can construct a deformed Kähler potential  K\mapsto K+\rho K↦K+ρK\mapsto K+\rho as follows.




Let  L_{x} LxL_{x} be the twistor line of a point  x\in M x∈Mx\in M in the undeformed twistor space  Z ZZ. Let  L_{x}[\mu] Lx​[μ]L_{x}[\mu] denote the deformed twistor line. This is constructed as a rational curve in  Z ZZ that is holomorphic with respect to  \bar{\partial}+\mu ∂¯+μ\bar{\partial}+\mu. If  \mu μ\mu is small (but finite), then  L_{x}[\mu] Lx​[μ]L_{x}[\mu] will act as a small perturbation of  L_{x} LxL_{x}. In particular, if  L_{x} LxL_{x} has normal bundle  \mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1) 𝒪⁡(1)⊕𝒪⁡(1)\mathscr{O}(1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(1), then so will  L_{x}[\mu] Lx​[μ]L_{x}[\mu]. In this case,  M MM continues to act as the moduli space of the deformed twistor lines.




Additionally, as is standard in the theory of deformations of Calabi-Yau structures, one can construct a deformed volume form from  \Omega Ω\Omega using the Tian-Todorov lemma [63, 128, 129, 130]:





 \Omega[\mu]=\mathrm{e}^{\mu}\!\,\lrcorner\,\Omega\,. Ω⁡[μ]=eμ​⌟​Ω.\Omega[\mu]=\mathrm{e}^{\mu}\!\,\lrcorner\,\Omega\,.

(B.18)


This is holomorphic with respect to  \bar{\partial}+\mu ∂¯+μ\bar{\partial}+\mu, away from its poles. The polar divisor of  \Omega[\mu] Ω⁡[μ]\Omega[\mu] continues to be the same as the polar divisor of  \Omega Ω\Omega: the quadric  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0. This is because  \mu μ\mu has second order zeroes at  \check{\omega}=0 ωˇ=0\check{\omega}=0 and is regular everywhere else.




This is precisely the data that goes into the construction of a scalar-flat Kähler metric on  M MM. Following Pontecorvo’s theorem, the deformed Kähler form may be found from a contour integral of the deformed volume form around one of the zeroes of  \check{\omega}|_{L_{x}[\mu]} ωˇ|Lx​[μ]\check{\omega}|_{L_{x}[\mu]}:





 \varpi(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Gamma\subset L_{x}[\mu]}\Omega[\mu]\,. ϖ⁡(x)=12​π​∮Γ⊂Lx​[μ]Ω⁡[μ].\varpi(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Gamma\subset L_{x}[\mu]}\Omega[\mu]\,.

(B.19)


Since the polar quadric  \check{\omega} ωˇ\check{\omega} is preserved, the complex structure  \partial,\bar{\partial} ∂,∂¯\partial,\bar{\partial} on spacetime is undeformed. Only the Kähler structure gets deformed:  \omega\mapsto\varpi ω↦ϖ\omega\mapsto\varpi. This allows us to introduce a scalar field  \rho ρ\rho that captures the deformation of the Kähler potential,  K\mapsto\mathcal{K}=K+\rho K↦𝒦=K+ρK\mapsto\mathcal{K}=K+\rho. That is, one sets





 \varpi=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho ϖ=i​∂∂¯​𝒦=ω+i​∂∂¯​ρ\varpi=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\mathcal{K}=\omega+\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}\rho

(B.20)


where  \omega=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}K ω=i​∂∂¯​K\omega=\mathrm{i}\,\partial\bar{\partial}K is the background Kähler form.




The deformed Kähler metric is scalar-flat by the Penrose transform [37, 56]. Hence, we conclude that on-shell Beltrami differentials  \mu μ\mu map to Kähler perturbations  \rho ρ\rho on spacetime that give rise to scalar-flat metrics. The latter are precisely the solutions of the equations of motion of Mabuchi gravity. This confirms, at least on-shell, that BCOV theory reduces to Mabuchi gravity on spacetime. Unlike WZW4, it remains an important open problem to prove this directly at the level of the off-shell BCOV action. As of now, going beyond the preliminary results of [15] in this direction has been prohibitively difficult.






Appendix C BRST cohomology at large  N NN




Here we will verify that the BRST cohomology of the chiral algebra at large  N NN has a basis given by normally ordered products of modes of the currents  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s],  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] described before.




This computation is only concerned with the size of the BRST cohomology, and says nothing about the operator product. As such, we can compute the BRST cohomology using a spectral sequence whose second page computes the classical BRST cohomology. The classical BRST cohomology is the Lie algebra cohomology of a certain infinite dimensional Lie algebra.




First, we build a graded Lie algebra we call  \mathfrak{g}_{N} 𝔤N\mathfrak{g}_{N} which encodes the gauge algebra and the symplectic reduction. This algebra is non-zero in degrees  0,1,2 0,1,20,1,2:





 \mathfrak{g}_{N}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{sp}_{N}&\text{ in degree }0\\
\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes F\oplus\wedge^{2}_{0}F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}&\text{ in degree }1\\
\mathfrak{sp}_{N}&\text{ in degree }2.\end{cases} 𝔤N={𝔰​𝔭N in degree ​0ℂ8⊗F⊕∧20F⊗ℂ2 in degree ​1𝔰​𝔭N in degree ​2.\mathfrak{g}_{N}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{sp}_{N}&\text{ in degree }0\\
\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes F\oplus\wedge^{2}_{0}F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}&\text{ in degree }1\\
\mathfrak{sp}_{N}&\text{ in degree }2.\end{cases}

(C.1)


The copy of  \mathfrak{sp}_{N} 𝔰​𝔭N\mathfrak{sp}_{N} in degree zero acts on everything in the natural way, and the bracket of two degree  1 11 elements is the moment map viewed as an element of  \mathfrak{sp}_{N} 𝔰​𝔭N\mathfrak{sp}_{N} in degree  2 22. All other brackets are zero.




Then, the classical BRST cohomology is the relative Lie algebra cohomology4141
            41
            
            
            
          Here, and below, when we discuss Lie algebra homology or cohomology of an algebra like  \mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]] 𝔤N​[[z]]\mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]], we mean in the topological sense. Sometimes this is called Gelfand-Fuchs cohomology. of





 C^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]]\mid\mathfrak{sp}_{N}). C∗​(𝔤N​[[z]]∣𝔰​𝔭N).C^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]]\mid\mathfrak{sp}_{N}).

(C.2)


The use of relative Lie algebra cohomology corresponds to the fact that in the BRST complex, we should not include the  \mathrm{c} c\mathrm{c}-ghost, only its derivatives; invariance under constant gauge transformations in  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) is imposed by hand.




At large  N NN, this Lie algebra cohomology can be readily computed using a variant of the techniques developed by Loday-Quillen-Tsygan [87, 88]. The underlying cochain complex of the relative Lie algebra cohomology is





 \operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}\left((\mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]]/\mathfrak{sp}_{N})^{\vee}[-1]\right)^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}. Sym∗⁡((𝔤N​[[z]]/𝔰​𝔭N)∨​[−1])Sp⁡(N).\operatorname{Sym}^{\ast}\left((\mathfrak{g}_{N}[[z]]/\mathfrak{sp}_{N})^{\vee}[-1]\right)^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}.

(C.3)


Classical invariant theory tells us that at large  N NN, the ring of invariants under  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) is freely generated by the open strings (products of fields starting and ending with  I II), and the closed strings.




The differential does not necessarily preserve the number of open and closed strings. This is because, inside any open or closed string expression, one might have the operator  \mathrm{b} b\mathrm{b}, and the BRST differential can turn this into  :\!II\!: :I​I::\!II\!:. This is the only term in the differential which can change the number of open and closed strings. This tells us that the different possibilities are:





 \begin{split}\text{open string}&\mapsto\text{one or two open strings}\\
\text{closed string}&\mapsto\text{a closed string or an open string}.\end{split} open string↦one or two open stringsclosed string↦a closed string or an open string.\begin{split}\text{open string}&\mapsto\text{one or two open strings}\\
\text{closed string}&\mapsto\text{a closed string or an open string}.\end{split}

(C.4)


We can filter the complex by the number of open strings. By taking the spectral sequence associated to this filtration, we bring ourselves to a complex where the differential preserves (separately) the number of open and closed strings.




The cohomology of this complex is the graded symmetric algebra on the subcomplex consisting of states which are either a single open string, or a single closed string. We will compute the cohomology of this complex and find that the open string part is spanned by the operator  J[r,s] J⁡[r,s]J[r,s] and derivatives, and the closed string part by  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] and derivatives.




This will complete the proof. Indeed, all these expressions are in ghost number zero so that any further pages of the spectral sequences we have used must have zero differential.




The computation of the cohomology for a single open or closed string uses the techniques of [87, 88]. We let  A AA be the graded commutative algebra





 A=\mathbb{C}[[z,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}]] A=ℂ⁡[[z,ϵ1,ϵ2]]A=\mathbb{C}[[z,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}]]

(C.5)


where  \epsilon_{1} ϵ1\epsilon_{1},  \epsilon_{2} ϵ2\epsilon_{2} are anti-commuting variables of ghost number  1 11.




We let  \iota:A\to A ι:A→A\iota:A\to A be the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 action which sends  \epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i} ϵi→−ϵi\epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i}. Define an involution





 \begin{split}\rho:\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A&\mapsto\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A\\
Z&\mapsto-\iota(Z^{t})\end{split} ρ:𝔤​𝔩2​N⊗A↦𝔤​𝔩2​N⊗AZ↦−ι⁡(Zt)\begin{split}\rho:\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A&\mapsto\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A\\
Z&\mapsto-\iota(Z^{t})\end{split}

(C.6)


where  M^{t} MtM^{t} is the symplectic transpose of  M MM, defined so that  \mathfrak{sp}(N) 𝔰​𝔭​(N)\mathfrak{sp}(N) is the subalgebra  M+M^{t}=0 M+Mt=0M+M^{t}=0.




Let





 A_{N}\subset A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N} AN⊂A⊗𝔤​𝔩2​NA_{N}\subset A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}

(C.7)


be the subspace of elements  Z\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A Z∈𝔤​𝔩2​N⊗AZ\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}\otimes A satisfying  \rho(Z)=Z ρ⁡(Z)=Z\rho(Z)=Z. The space of such elements is





 \begin{split}\mathfrak{sp}_{N}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }0\\
\wedge^{2}F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }1\\
\mathfrak{sp}_{N}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }2\end{split} 𝔰​𝔭N​[[z]] in degree ​0∧2F⊗ℂ2[[z]] in degree ​1𝔰​𝔭N​[[z]] in degree ​2\begin{split}\mathfrak{sp}_{N}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }0\\
\wedge^{2}F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }1\\
\mathfrak{sp}_{N}[[z]]&\text{ in degree }2\end{split}

(C.8)


Thus, these elements match the  \mathrm{b} b\mathrm{b},  X XX and  \mathrm{c} c\mathrm{c} fields, except that we have not removed the trace from the  X XX fields.




We will use this description to compute the closed string part of the BRST cohomology, which is essentially the same as the single-trace part of the Lie algebra cohomology of  A_{N} ANA_{N}. A small variant of the results of [87, 88] on dihedral homology tells us the following.



Lemma C.1.



Let  HC^{\ast}(A) H​C∗​(A)HC^{\ast}(A) be the cyclic cohomology of  A AA. The single closed string part of the Lie algebra homology of  A_{N} ANA_{N}, at large  N NN, is the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 invariants in  HC^{\ast}(A)[-1] H​C∗​(A)​[−1]HC^{\ast}(A)[-1], where the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 action comes from an action on cyclic cochains described in the proof.




Proof.



We let  \operatorname{Cyc}(A) Cyc⁡(A)\operatorname{Cyc}(A) be the cyclic cochain complex of  A AA. As a graded vector space, without the differential, this is defined as follows.




First, we let  \operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A) Cycn⁡(A)\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A) be the quotient of the space of linear functionals





 \phi:A^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C} ϕ:A⊗n→ℂ\phi:A^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}

(C.9)


be the relation





 \phi=(-1)^{n}\sigma\phi ϕ=(−1)n​σ​ϕ\phi=(-1)^{n}\sigma\phi

(C.10)


where  \sigma σ\sigma is a cyclic permutation. Then,





 \operatorname{Cyc}(A)=\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A)[-n]. Cyc⁡(A)=∏n≥1Cycn⁡(A)​[−n].\operatorname{Cyc}(A)=\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A)[-n].

(C.11)


We will not need right now the explicit formula for the differential.




Loday-Quillen-Tsygan defined a cochain map





 \operatorname{Cyc}(A)\to C^{\ast}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)} Cyc⁡(A)→C∗​(A⊗𝔤​𝔩2​N)GL⁡(2​N)\operatorname{Cyc}(A)\to C^{\ast}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)}

(C.12)


which at large  N NN is an isomorphism onto the single trace part. The explicit formula is the following. If  \phi:A^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C} ϕ:A⊗n→ℂ\phi:A^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}, then we define





 \widehat{\phi}\in C^{n}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)} ϕ^∈Cn​(A⊗𝔤​𝔩2​N)GL⁡(2​N)\widehat{\phi}\in C^{n}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)}

(C.13)


by the expression





 \widehat{\phi}((M_{1}\otimes a_{1})\dots(M_{n}\otimes a_{n}))=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)}\operatorname{tr}(M_{\sigma_{1}}\dots M_{\sigma_{n}})\phi(a_{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{\sigma_{n}}). ϕ^​((M1⊗a1)​…​(Mn⊗an))=1n!​∑σ∈Sn(−1)sign⁡(σ)​tr⁡(Mσ1​…​Mσn)​ϕ​(aσ1⊗⋯⊗aσn).\widehat{\phi}((M_{1}\otimes a_{1})\dots(M_{n}\otimes a_{n}))=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)}\operatorname{tr}(M_{\sigma_{1}}\dots M_{\sigma_{n}})\phi(a_{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{\sigma_{n}}).

(C.14)


Clearly  \widehat{\phi} ϕ^\widehat{\phi} only depends on  \phi\in\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A) ϕ∈Cycn⁡(A)\phi\in\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A).




The Lie algebra map  A_{N}\to A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N} AN→A⊗𝔤​𝔩2​NA_{N}\to A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N} gives rise to a map





 C^{\ast}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)}\to C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}. C∗​(A⊗𝔤​𝔩2​N)GL⁡(2​N)→C∗​(AN)Sp⁡(N).C^{\ast}(A\otimes\mathfrak{gl}_{2N})^{\mathrm{GL}(2N)}\to C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}.

(C.15)


By composing these two maps we get a cochain map





 \operatorname{Cyc}(A)\to C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}. Cyc⁡(A)→C∗​(AN)Sp⁡(N).\operatorname{Cyc}(A)\to C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)}.

(C.16)


Explicitly, this is given by the formula (C.14), where the  M_{i} MiM_{i} are in  \mathfrak{sp}_{N} 𝔰​𝔭N\mathfrak{sp}_{N} if  a_{i}\in A^{even} ai∈Ae​v​e​na_{i}\in A^{even} and in  \wedge^{2}F ∧2F\wedge^{2}F if  a_{i}\in A^{odd} ai∈Ao​d​da_{i}\in A^{odd}.




We need to characterize the image and kernel of this map at large  N NN, on single-trace expressions.
By considering invariant theory for the group  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N),  N NN large, it is clear that the map (C.16) is surjective on single-trace expressions.




However, it is not an isomorphism, because contractions of indices under  \mathrm{Sp}(N) Sp⁡(N)\mathrm{Sp}(N) have a dihedral symmetry not present for  \mathrm{GL}(2N) GL⁡(2​N)\mathrm{GL}(2N). Consider a collection of elements  \mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2N} 𝒳i,(r)∈𝔤​𝔩2​N\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2N}, where the index  r rr takes value  0 00 or  1 11, and  \mathcal{X}_{i,(r)} 𝒳i,(r)\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)} satisfies





 \mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}+(-1)^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}^{t}=0 𝒳i,(r)+(−1)r​𝒳i,(r)t=0\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}+(-1)^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)}^{t}=0

(C.17)


where  \mathcal{X}^{t} 𝒳t\mathcal{X}^{t} is the symplectic transpose. Thus,  \mathcal{X}_{i,(r)} 𝒳i,(r)\mathcal{X}_{i,(r)} is in  \mathfrak{sp}(N) 𝔰​𝔭​(N)\mathfrak{sp}(N) if  r=0 r=0r=0 and in  \wedge^{2}F ∧2F\wedge^{2}F if  r=1 r=1r=1. Then,





 \operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{X}_{1,r_{1}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{n,r_{n}})=(-1)^{n+\sum r_{i}}\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{X}_{n,r_{n}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{1,r_{1}}) tr⁡(𝒳1,r1​…​𝒳n,rn)=(−1)n+∑ri​tr⁡(𝒳n,rn​…​𝒳1,r1)\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{X}_{1,r_{1}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{n,r_{n}})=(-1)^{n+\sum r_{i}}\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{X}_{n,r_{n}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{1,r_{1}})

(C.18)


This is the only relation (on top of cyclic (anti)-symmetry) we need to describe single trace expressions in  A_{N} ANA_{N}.




From this we find that the single trace part of  C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)} C∗​(AN)Sp⁡(N)C^{\ast}(A_{N})^{\mathrm{Sp}(N)} is isomorphic to the coinvariants of the cyclic cochain complex under the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 action which, on  \operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A) Cycn⁡(A)\operatorname{Cyc}_{n}(A), reverse the order of the elements, acts by  (-1)^{n} (−1)n(-1)^{n}, and also applies the automorphism of  A AA sending  \epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i} ϵi→−ϵi\epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i}.
∎





The single trace part of the cyclic cohomology of  A AA was computed in [16]: it is given by the single-trace local operators of the vertex algebra with  \mathfrak{gl}_{N} 𝔤​𝔩N\mathfrak{gl}_{N} BRST reduction considered there. There it was found that it is given by four towers: the two bosonic towers  E[r,s] E⁡[r,s]E[r,s],  F[r,s] F⁡[r,s]F[r,s] we have discussed before, and two new fermionic towers





 \begin{split}B[r,s]&=\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{b}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)})\\
C[r,s]&=\operatorname{Tr}(\partial cX_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)})\end{split} B⁡[r,s]=Tr⁡(b​X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs))C⁡[r,s]=Tr⁡(∂c​X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs))\begin{split}B[r,s]&=\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{b}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)})\\
C[r,s]&=\operatorname{Tr}(\partial cX_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)})\end{split}

(C.19)


It remains to check that the  B,C B,CB,C towers are odd under the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 action on cyclic cohomology. This is not hard to verify.




The final thing to do is to compute the open string states. We can do this by a similar argument. Let  A AA be the algebra as above, and let  M MM be the graded  A AA-module





 M=\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes\mathbb{C}[[z]] M=ℂ8⊗ℂ⁡[[z]]M=\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes\mathbb{C}[[z]]

(C.20)


Define the bar complex by





 \begin{split}\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)=\operatorname{Hom}(M\otimes A^{\otimes n}\otimes M,\mathbb{C})\\
\operatorname{Bar}(M,A,A)=\prod_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)[-n]\end{split} Barn⁡(M,A,M)=Hom⁡(M⊗A⊗n⊗M,ℂ)Bar⁡(M,A,A)=∏n≥0Barn⁡(M,A,M)​[−n]\begin{split}\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)=\operatorname{Hom}(M\otimes A^{\otimes n}\otimes M,\mathbb{C})\\
\operatorname{Bar}(M,A,A)=\prod_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)[-n]\end{split}

(C.21)


where  [-n] [−n][-n] indicates a cohomological shift up by  n nn. When equipped with the standard bar differential, we have





 \operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}M,\mathbb{C}) Barn⁡(M,A,M)≃Hom⁡(M⊗A𝕃M,ℂ)\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}M,\mathbb{C})

(C.22)


where on the right hand side we have the dual of the derived tensor product. Thus, the cohomology of the bar complex can be computed as the dual of the Tor groups of  M MM with itself over  A AA.




There is a natural map





 \operatorname{Bar}(M,A,M)\to C^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g}_{N}) Bar⁡(M,A,M)→C∗​(𝔤N)\operatorname{Bar}(M,A,M)\to C^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g}_{N})

(C.23)


which at infinite  N NN maps surjetively onto the single-string open string states. The expression is the obvious one: an element of  \operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M) Barn⁡(M,A,M)\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M) maps to an open string expression starting with  I II, with  n nn elements of  A^{\vee} A∨A^{\vee} (representing  X,\mathrm{b},\mathrm{c} X,b,cX,\mathrm{b},\mathrm{c} and derivatives) and ending with  I II.




If we were working with a  \mathfrak{gl}_{N} 𝔤​𝔩N\mathfrak{gl}_{N} gauge theory, this map would be an isomorphism onto the open string states. It is not, however, for the same reason we saw when discussing closed string states: when working with a  \mathfrak{sp}_{N} 𝔰​𝔭N\mathfrak{sp}_{N} gauge theory, there is a symmetry which reverses the order in a trace (and introduces a sign).




This  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 action acts on  \operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M) Barn⁡(M,A,M)\operatorname{Bar}_{n}(M,A,M) by reversing the order, sending  \epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i} ϵi→−ϵi\epsilon_{i}\to-\epsilon_{i}, and introducing a sign of  (-1)^{n+1} (−1)n+1(-1)^{n+1}.




We find, then, that the single open string states are the dual of the  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 invariants in





 \operatorname{Tor}_{A}(M,M). TorA⁡(M,M).\operatorname{Tor}_{A}(M,M).

(C.24)





The next step is to compute these Tor groups. This is done by writing down a free resolution for  M MM as an  A AA module. The resolution is





 F=\mathbb{C}[[z,\epsilon_{i},\gamma_{i}]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} F=ℂ⁡[[z,ϵi,γi]]⊗ℂ8F=\mathbb{C}[[z,\epsilon_{i},\gamma_{i}]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8}

(C.25)


with differential





 \mathrm{d}=\epsilon_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_{i}}. d=ϵi​∂∂γi.\mathrm{d}=\epsilon_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_{i}}.

(C.26)


The  \gamma_{i} γi\gamma_{i} are bosonic variables in ghost number  0 00. At the level of cohomology, the  \epsilon_{i} ϵi\epsilon_{i} and  \gamma_{i} γi\gamma_{i} cancel and  F FF becomes  M MM.




The Tor group is then the cohomology of the complex





 F\otimes_{A}M F⊗AMF\otimes_{A}M

(C.27)


Tensoring over  A AA with  M MM has the effect of setting the  \epsilon_{i} ϵi\epsilon_{i} to  0 00 in  F FF, so that this tensor product is  \mathbb{C}[[z,\gamma_{i}]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} ℂ⁡[[z,γi]]⊗ℂ8⊗ℂ8\mathbb{C}[[z,\gamma_{i}]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} with zero differential.




This tells us that the cohomology of the bar complex is spanned by  z zz-derivatives of expressions like





 I_{k}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}I_{l} Ik​X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs)​IlI_{k}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}I_{l}

(C.28)


where the indices  k,l k,lk,l run over a basis of  \mathbb{C}^{8} ℂ8\mathbb{C}^{8}, and thee expression is not anti-symmetric in  i,j i,ji,j. The integers that  r,s r,sr,s that appear here corresond to the powers of  \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} γ1,γ2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} in the dual vector space  \mathbb{C}[[z,\gamma_{i}]] ℂ⁡[[z,γi]]\mathbb{C}[[z,\gamma_{i}]]. Passing to  \mathbb{Z}/2 ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 invariants means that we only retain the expressions which are anti-symmetric in  k,l k,lk,l, as desired.




C.1 BRST cohomology in the presence of the boundary condition




Now let us repeat the analysis in the case that we have the boundary condition discussed in section 4.3. Recall that with this boundary condition,  X_{\dot{\alpha}} Xα˙X_{\dot{\alpha}} have first order poles,  \mathrm{b} b\mathrm{b} has a second order pole and  I II has a branch cut and a pole of order  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2}.




The poles in the  X XX and  \mathrm{b} b\mathrm{b} field can be engineered very easily in the framework of the previous section, simply by multiplying the fermionic variables  \epsilon_{i} ϵi\epsilon_{i} by  z^{-1} z−1z^{-1}. This does not change the algebra in any way, except by changing the spin of the  \epsilon_{i} ϵi\epsilon_{i}. Thus, the analysis goes through without any change.




For the open string sector, the analysis is also almost identical. The module  z^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{C}[[z]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} z−12​ℂ​[[z]]⊗ℂ8z^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{C}[[z]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} associated to giving  I II a pole of order  \frac{1}{2} 12\frac{1}{2} is isomorphic to the module  \mathbb{C}[[z]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} ℂ⁡[[z]]⊗ℂ8\mathbb{C}[[z]]\otimes\mathbb{C}^{8} we used in the case without boundary condition. Thus, the same argument applies.




The result is that a basis of single-trace closed string operators at  z=0 z=0z=0, in the presence of the boundary condition, is given by the same expressions we used before, but with extra powers of  z zz:





 \begin{split}z^{r+s+2}\operatorname{tr}\left(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}X_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial X_{\dot{\beta}}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}\right)\\
z^{r+s}\operatorname{tr}\left(X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}\right)\\
\end{split} zr+s+2​tr⁡(ϵα˙​β˙​Xα˙​∂Xβ˙​X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs))zr+s​tr⁡(X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs))\begin{split}z^{r+s+2}\operatorname{tr}\left(\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}X_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial X_{\dot{\beta}}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}\right)\\
z^{r+s}\operatorname{tr}\left(X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}\right)\\
\end{split}

(C.29)


and the open string expressions are similar:





 z^{r+s+1}I_{k}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}I_{l} zr+s+1​Ik​X1˙(rCLOSE​X2˙OPENs)​Ilz^{r+s+1}I_{k}X_{\dot{1}}^{(r}X_{\dot{2}}^{s)}I_{l}

(C.30)


(Of course, we also must include derivatives of these expressions).






Appendix D A basis for the boundary module (when defined as a quotient of the mode algebra)




We have given two definitions of the boundary module: one in terms of boundary conditions for the fundamental fields in section 4.3, where we then take BRST cohomology; and another, where we simply declare what poles the currents  E,F,J E,F,JE,F,J have at  z=0 z=0z=0 and  z=\infty z=∞z=\infty as in section 4.2.




In this section, for completeness, we will verify that the second definition leads to a boundary module of the correct size.
The boundary module is defined by the left ideal generated by





 \begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0.\end{split} Jl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n<0El​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n<0Fl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n<0.\begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n<0.\end{split}

(D.1)


We will prove that this module has a basis given by ordered products of the modes





 \begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0.\end{split} Jl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0El​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0Fl​[m,n] for ​2​l+m+n≥0.\begin{split}J_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
E_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0\\
F_{l}[m,n]&\text{ for }2l+m+n\geq 0.\end{split}

(D.2)





Before we check this, we need to introduce some notation. Given a single-trace mode  J_{l}[m,n] Jl​[m,n]J_{l}[m,n],  E_{l}[m,n] El​[m,n]E_{l}[m,n],  F_{l}[m,n] Fl​[m,n]F_{l}[m,n] we say its length is  (m+n)/2 (m+n)/2(m+n)/2. The length labels which  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) representation the mode lives in.




We define the weight of a single-trace mode by the formula





 \text{weight }=2\text{ spin }+2\text{ length}. weight =2​ spin +2​ length.\text{weight }=2\text{ spin }+2\text{ length}.

(D.3)


Then the ideal defining the module is the left ideal generated by those single-trace modes of weight  <0 <0<0.




We say a product of single-trace modes  \mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪1​…​𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} of weights  w_{1},\dots,w_{n} w1,…,wnw_{1},\dots,w_{n} satisfies the modified normal ordering  w_{1}>w_{2}>\dots>w_{n} w1>w2>⋯>wnw_{1}>w_{2}>\dots>w_{n}. Let us also choose arbitrarily a normal ordering perscription on those modes of the same weight. Then, products of single-trace modes in modified normal ordering form a topological basis for the mode algebra.




In this basis, we say the modified normally ordered product  \mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪1​…​𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} is of weight  w_{1}+\dots+w_{n} w1+⋯+wnw_{1}+\dots+w_{n} (where  w_{i} wiw_{i} is the weight of  \mathcal{O}_{i} 𝒪i\mathcal{O}_{i}).




We have the following lemma.



Lemma D.1.



If  \mathcal{O}_{1} 𝒪1\mathcal{O}_{1},  \mathcal{O}_{2} 𝒪2\mathcal{O}_{2} are any two states of weight  w_{1},w_{2} w1,w2w_{1},w_{2}, then the commutator  [\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}] [𝒪1,𝒪2][\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}] is a sum of states of weight  \leq w_{1}+w_{2} ≤w1+w2\leq w_{1}+w_{2}.




Proof.



This is immediate from the representation theory. Let  \mathcal{O}_{i} 𝒪i\mathcal{O}_{i} be of spin  s_{i} sis_{i} and length  l_{i} lil_{i}, where  w_{i}=2s_{i}+2l_{i} wi=2​si+2​liw_{i}=2s_{i}+2l_{i}. Then,  [\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}] [𝒪1,𝒪2][\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}] is of spin  s_{1}+s_{2} s1+s2s_{1}+s_{2}. It also transforms in some representations of  \mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL2​(ℂ)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) which are contained in the tensor product of the representation of spin  l_{1} l1l_{1} and that of spin  l_{2} l2l_{2}. Such a representation is of spin at most  l_{1}+l_{2} l1+l2l_{1}+l_{2}.
∎




Proposition D.1.



A basis for the ideal defining the module is given by modified normally-ordered products of single-trace generators  \mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪1​…​𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} where  \mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{n} has weight  <0 <0<0.




Proof.



Clearly these expressions are in the ideal. However, a priori, the ideal could be larger. It is spanned by elements of the form  (\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1})\mathcal{O}_{n} (𝒪1​…​𝒪n−1)​𝒪n(\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1})\mathcal{O}_{n}, where  \mathcal{O}_{i} 𝒪i\mathcal{O}_{i} are single-trace operators of weights  w_{i} wiw_{i}, where  w_{n}<0 wn<0w_{n}<0 and  \mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1} 𝒪1​…​𝒪n−1\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1} are in modified normal order  w_{1}\geq\dots\geq w_{n-1} w1≥⋯≥wn−1w_{1}\geq\dots\geq w_{n-1}.




We need to show that we can re-order an expression like this to make the whole expression in modified normal order. Suppose that we need to move  \mathcal{O}_{k}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1} 𝒪k​…​𝒪n−1\mathcal{O}_{k}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1} to the right of  \mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{n} to place the expression in modified normal order. This can only happen if  w_{i}\leq w_{n} wi≤wnw_{i}\leq w_{n} for  i=k,\dots,n-1 i=k,…,n−1i=k,\dots,n-1. When we re-order in this way, we pick up the commutator





 [\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{k}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1}]. [𝒪n,𝒪k​…​𝒪n−1].[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{k}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n-1}].

(D.4)


By the previous lemma, this commutator is a sum of expressions in modified normal order of total weight which is at most  w_{k}+\dots+w_{n} wk+⋯+wnw_{k}+\dots+w_{n}. Since  w_{n}<0 wn<0w_{n}<0 and  w_{i}\leq w_{n} wi≤wnw_{i}\leq w_{n} for  i=k,\dots,n-1 i=k,…,n−1i=k,\dots,n-1, this total weight is less than  0 00. Any expression in modified normal order of total weight  <0 <0<0 has rightmost element of weight  <0 <0<0. We conclude that we can re-write any element of the ideal as a sum of expressions in modified normal order where the rightmost element has weight  <0 <0<0, as desired.
∎




Corollary D.1.



A basis for the module which is a quotient of the mode algebra by the ideal described above is given by products of single-trace modes in modified normal order





 \mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n} 𝒪1​…​𝒪n\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{n}

(D.5)


where the weights  w_{i} wiw_{i} are  \geq 0 ≥0\geq 0.




Proof.



This is exactly the complement, in our chosen basis, of those words in modified normal order whose rightmost element has weight  <0 <0<0.
∎






Appendix E Vanishing of some Dolbeault cohomology groups 




In this section we will show that





 \begin{split}H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))&=0\\
H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathscr{O}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E)&=0.\end{split} H∗∂¯(SL¯2(ℂ),𝒪(−∂SL¯2(ℂ)))=0H∂¯∗​(𝔽~,𝒪⁡(−D¯0−D¯∞−E)CLOSE=0.\begin{split}H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathscr{O}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))&=0\\
H^{\ast}_{\bar{\partial}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},\mathscr{O}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E)&=0.\end{split}

(E.1)


These cohomology groups are easily seen to vanish. Let us first check it for  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}), which is a quadric in  \mathbb{CP}^{4} ℂ​ℙ4\mathbb{CP}^{4}. The boundary divisor in  \overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) SL¯2​(ℂ)\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) is the pull-back of  \mathscr{O}(-1) 𝒪⁡(−1)\mathscr{O}(-1). There is an exact sequence in the category of sheaves on  \mathbb{CP}^{4} ℂ​ℙ4\mathbb{CP}^{4}





 0\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{4}}(-3)\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{4}}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-1)\to 0. 0→𝒪ℂ​ℙ4​(−3)→𝒪ℂ​ℙ4​(−1)→𝒪SL¯2​(ℂ)​(−1)→0.0\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{4}}(-3)\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{4}}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-1)\to 0.

(E.2)


The coherent sheaf cohomology of the first two terms vanishes, therefore that of the last term vanishes too.




To prove the corresponding result for  \widetilde{\mathbb{F}} 𝔽~\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}, we first note that





 R\pi_{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}=\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})} R​π∗​𝒪𝔽~=𝒪SL¯2​(ℂ)R\pi_{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}=\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}

(E.3)


where  R\pi_{\ast} R​π∗R\pi_{\ast} is the derived push-forward along the map  \pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) π:𝔽~→SL¯2​(ℂ)\pi:\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}\to\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}). This holds essentially because all fibers of the map  \pi π\pi are either a point or  \mathbb{CP}^{1} ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. Then, since





 \pi^{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E) π∗𝒪SL¯2​(ℂ)(−∂SL¯2(ℂ))=𝒪𝔽~(−D¯0−D¯∞−E)\pi^{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E)

(E.4)


we also have





 R\pi_{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E)=\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})) Rπ∗𝒪𝔽~(−D¯0−D¯∞−E)=𝒪SL¯2​(ℂ)(−∂SL¯2(ℂ))R\pi_{\ast}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}}(-\overline{D}_{0}-\overline{D}_{\infty}-E)=\mathscr{O}_{\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(-\partial\overline{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

(E.5)


from which the result follows.

Instructions for reporting errors
    
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
    
      Click the "Report Issue" (
            
          ) button, located in the page header.
    
    
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
    
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
    
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
